Machine Learning Background

This appendix supplies the machine learning and statistical tools needed to interpret learned action proposals, size their computation, and evaluate the evidence for their use. It develops uncertainty measures, spatial representations, policy-learning objectives, and rare-event bounds as consultable references for the main text. Each tool separates what a model or test establishes from the physical clearance and timing conditions that the permission path must enforce.

How to Use This Appendix

Use the table to enter at the relevant problem. The probability section explains expectations, densities, and uncertainty; spatial representations then connect image arrays to metric geometry. Policy sections distinguish the training objective from runtime execution, and the final sections distinguish statistical evidence from a physical guarantee. The derivations use matrix multiplication, derivatives, and integrals; the prose following each result states the engineering consequence so that a reader can consult the result before working through its proof.

When you encounter this systems symptom Consult For this quantitative tool
A vision-language model hallucinates actions under perceptual ambiguity section 1.2 Posterior entropy metrics, Shannon uncertainty, and refusal thresholds
Overconfident neural outputs command aggressive actions out-of-distribution section 1.2.3 Softmax simplex geometry and Expected Calibration Error (ECE)
Multi-camera extrinsics, 2D tokenization, or 3D scene geometry drift section 1.3 \(\SpecialEuclideanGroup\) transforms, patch embeddings, attention, and metric 3D lifting
Camera features must be placed in one metric ground-plane grid section 1.3.3 Lift-Splat depth hypotheses, frustum size, and BEV splatting
Differentiable 3D scene reconstruction diverges during real-time tracking section 1.3.5 3D Gaussian Splatting parameters and covariance projection
Occupancy mapping retains phantom obstacles after objects move section 1.3.6 Log-odds voxel updates, TSDF fusion, and raycast DRAM traffic
Small sensor noise produces explosive, unconstrained actuator commands section 1.4.2 Lipschitz continuity bounds and spectral normalization
A behavioral cloning policy drifts into unrecoverable off-trajectory states section 1.5 \(\CovariateDriftBound\) compounding error and DAgger linear reduction
\(\ell_2\) regression averages bimodal demonstrations into lethal obstacle collisions section 1.5.1 Conditional expectation proof and multimodal policy decoders
A generative decoder cannot finish a chunk within the setpoint period section 1.5.2 Denoising evaluations \(\DecoderEvaluationCount\) latency-expressiveness trade-off and flow matching
Offline reinforcement learning selects ungrounded, destructive motor setpoints section 1.6 Bellman value overestimation and SayCan affordance grounding
Continuous policy optimization diverges under function approximation error section 1.6.1 DDPG, TD3 clipped double Q-learning, SAC maximum entropy, and PPO clipping
Sim-to-real transfer fails due to unobservable physical environmental parameters section 1.6.6 Asymmetric actor-critic architectures and privileged simulation states
Comparing policy decoders across latency, compute intensity, and memory footprint section 1.6.9 Comparative systems taxonomy (MLP, CNN, ACT, Diffusion, Flow, RL)
Verifying time-varying physical safety properties across continuous traces section 1.7 Signal Temporal Logic (STL) syntax and quantitative robustness degree
Zero-failure fleet testing encounters the Poisson Exposure Wall section 1.8 Rule of Three (\(\FailureObservationInterval \ge 3.0/\ConstantHazardRate\)), Wald breakdown, and Clopper-Pearson bounds
Physical testbed wear explodes during fixed-sample policy qualification section 1.9 Sequential Probability Ratio Test (SPRT) and Wald stopping boundaries
Contact collision shocks and latency spikes exceed Gaussian \(6\GaussianStandardDeviation\) bounds section 1.10 Tail-family selection, GEV/GPD fits, and conditional high-quantile sizing

Probability, Information Theory, and Calibration

Commanding an uncalibrated motor torque stresses gearboxes, violates physical clearance envelopes, and risks destructive contact collisions across an irreversible causal boundary, unlike a software prediction error that merely degrades a recommendation or text token. Autonomous machines must therefore reason quantitatively about uncertainty across perception, intent estimation, and world modeling. In closed-loop physical AI, uncertainty quantification determines whether a candidate action proposal is dispatched to motor controllers or intercepted by a safety governor.

Conditional probability and Bayes’ rule

When a sensor returns observed evidence \(\ModelInput\), the conditional distribution \(\ProbabilityFunction(\ExampleLabel \mid \ModelInput)\) characterizes the updated belief over \(\ExampleLabel\). For continuous parameters, \(\ProbabilityFunction\) in the following expressions denotes a density; probabilities over regions require integration. By Bayes’ rule: \[ \ProbabilityFunction(\ModelParameters \mid \ObservationDataset) = \frac{\ProbabilityFunction(\ObservationDataset \mid \ModelParameters) \ProbabilityFunction(\ModelParameters)}{\ProbabilityFunction(\ObservationDataset)} = \frac{\ProbabilityFunction(\ObservationDataset \mid \ModelParameters) \ProbabilityFunction(\ModelParameters)}{\int_\ParameterSpace \ProbabilityFunction(\ObservationDataset \mid \MarginalizedModelParameters) \ProbabilityFunction(\MarginalizedModelParameters) \, d\MarginalizedModelParameters} \] where \(\ProbabilityFunction(\ModelParameters)\) represents prior belief over model parameters or latent states \(\ModelParameters \in \ParameterSpace\), \(\ProbabilityFunction(\ObservationDataset \mid \ModelParameters)\) represents the likelihood of physical dataset or sensor observation sequence \(\ObservationDataset = \{\ObservedDataPoint_1, \dots, \ObservedDataPoint_{\ObservationCount}\}\), and \(\ProbabilityFunction(\ModelParameters \mid \ObservationDataset)\) represents the updated posterior belief.

In high-dimensional learning systems, integrating over the parameter manifold \(\ParameterSpace\) to compute the marginal likelihood (evidence) \(\ProbabilityFunction(\ObservationDataset) = \int_\ParameterSpace \ProbabilityFunction(\ObservationDataset \mid \ModelParameters) \ProbabilityFunction(\ModelParameters) \, d\ModelParameters\) is analytically and computationally intractable. Real-time autonomy resolves this intractability through two complementary paradigms:

  1. Maximum a posteriori (MAP) estimation: Replacing posterior integration with point optimization: \[ \MapParameterEstimate = \arg\max_\ModelParameters \left[ \sum_{i=1}^\ObservationCount \ln \ProbabilityFunction(\ObservedDataPoint_i \mid \ModelParameters) + \ln \ProbabilityFunction(\ModelParameters) \right] \] With conditionally independent observations and a summed log-likelihood, a zero-mean isotropic Gaussian prior \(\ProbabilityFunction(\ModelParameters) = \GaussianDistribution(\ZeroVector, \PriorStandardDeviation^2 \IdentityMatrix)\) gives the log-prior \(\ln \ProbabilityFunction(\ModelParameters) = -\frac{\|\ModelParameters\|_2^2}{2\PriorStandardDeviation^2} - \frac{\RandomVectorDimension}{2}\ln(2\pi\PriorStandardDeviation^2)\). Maximizing the posterior objective is mathematically identical to minimizing negative log-likelihood with an explicit \(\ell_2\) penalty \(\frac{\WeightPenaltyStrength}{2} \|\ModelParameters\|_2^2\), where \(\WeightPenaltyStrength = 1/\PriorStandardDeviation^2\). However, MAP estimation yields only a single deterministic point estimate \(\MapParameterEstimate\). The point estimate does not report posterior variance; it does not establish that the underlying uncertainty is zero. In continuous control, epistemic uncertainty (ignorance arising from lack of training data in unfamiliar operational domains) must be sharply distinguished from aleatoric uncertainty (stochasticity inherent to sensor noise, turbulent airflow, or non-deterministic contact friction). A point-estimated model can still predict observation noise, but that output alone does not quantify uncertainty about its fitted parameters.
  2. Variational inference: Approximating the true posterior \(\ProbabilityFunction(\ModelParameters \mid \ObservationDataset)\) with a tractable variational distribution family \(\VariationalDistribution(\ModelParameters)\) by maximizing the Evidence Lower Bound (ELBO): \[ \ln \ProbabilityFunction(\ObservationDataset) = \mathbb{E}_{\ModelParameters \sim \VariationalDistribution(\ModelParameters)} \left[ \ln \frac{\ProbabilityFunction(\ObservationDataset, \ModelParameters)}{\VariationalDistribution(\ModelParameters)} \right] + \KlDivergence\left( \VariationalDistribution(\ModelParameters) \,\parallel\, \ProbabilityFunction(\ModelParameters \mid \ObservationDataset) \right) \ge \mathbb{E}_{\ModelParameters \sim \VariationalDistribution(\ModelParameters)} [\ln \ProbabilityFunction(\ObservationDataset \mid \ModelParameters)] - \KlDivergence\left( \VariationalDistribution(\ModelParameters) \,\parallel\, \ProbabilityFunction(\ModelParameters) \right) \] Because the Kullback-Leibler divergence is non-negative (\(\KlDivergence \ge 0\)), the lower bound holds, with equality when the variational distribution equals the posterior almost everywhere. Variational inference converts intractable high-dimensional integration into continuous gradient-based optimization over variational parameters \(\VariationalParameters\). This formulation provides the analytical foundation for the latent-variable action chunking and generative decoders derived in section 1.5.1, where latent variables capture human demonstrator intent and style without collapsing multimodal physical actions into destructive spatial averages.

Shannon entropy and ambiguity thresholds

When a perception or intent model spreads probability across several candidate targets, entropy quantifies the average uncertainty or information content contained in that distribution. For a discrete random variable \(\DiscreteRandomVariable\) taking values in alphabet \(\OutcomeAlphabet\):1 \[ \ShannonEntropy(\DiscreteRandomVariable) = - \sum_{\DiscreteOutcome \in \OutcomeAlphabet} \ProbabilityFunction(\DiscreteOutcome) \log_2 \ProbabilityFunction(\DiscreteOutcome) = \mathbb{E}_{\DiscreteOutcome \sim \ProbabilityFunction} \left[ \log_2 \frac{1}{\ProbabilityFunction(\DiscreteOutcome)} \right] \] For continuous random variables governing physical state \(\ContinuousOutcome \in \mathbb{R}^\RandomVectorDimension\) with probability density \(\ProbabilityFunction(\ContinuousOutcome)\), the differential entropy evaluates to: \[ \DifferentialEntropy(\ContinuousRandomVariable) = - \int_{\mathbb{R}^\RandomVectorDimension} \ProbabilityFunction(\ContinuousOutcome) \ln \ProbabilityFunction(\ContinuousOutcome) \, d\ContinuousOutcome \] Under a multivariate Gaussian distribution \(\ContinuousOutcome \sim \GaussianDistribution(\GaussianMeanVector, \GaussianCovarianceMatrix)\) with positive-definite covariance \(\GaussianCovarianceMatrix \in \mathbb{R}^{\RandomVectorDimension \times \RandomVectorDimension}\), substituting the Gaussian density \(\ProbabilityFunction(\ContinuousOutcome) = (2\pi)^{-\RandomVectorDimension/2} (\det \GaussianCovarianceMatrix)^{-1/2} \exp(-\frac{1}{2}(\ContinuousOutcome-\GaussianMeanVector)^\top \GaussianCovarianceMatrix^{-1} (\ContinuousOutcome-\GaussianMeanVector))\) yields: \[ \begin{aligned} \DifferentialEntropy(\ContinuousRandomVariable) &= -\mathbb{E}\left[ -\frac{\RandomVectorDimension}{2}\ln(2\pi) - \frac{1}{2}\ln(\det \GaussianCovarianceMatrix) - \frac{1}{2}(\ContinuousOutcome - \GaussianMeanVector)^\top \GaussianCovarianceMatrix^{-1}(\ContinuousOutcome - \GaussianMeanVector) \right] \\ &= \frac{\RandomVectorDimension}{2}\ln(2\pi) + \frac{1}{2}\ln(\det \GaussianCovarianceMatrix) + \frac{1}{2}\operatorname{Tr}\left(\GaussianCovarianceMatrix^{-1} \mathbb{E}[(\ContinuousOutcome-\GaussianMeanVector)(\ContinuousOutcome-\GaussianMeanVector)^\top]\right) \\ &= \frac{1}{2} \ln\left( (2\pi \NaturalLogarithmBase)^\RandomVectorDimension \det \GaussianCovarianceMatrix \right) \end{aligned} \] Unlike discrete entropy, differential entropy depends on coordinate units and can be negative. Comparisons require the same coordinates and units. For a Gaussian it scales with the logarithmic volume of the physical uncertainty ellipsoid \(\det \GaussianCovarianceMatrix\). In state estimation, sensor fusion, and Kalman filtering, an informative measurement update shrinks \(\det \GaussianCovarianceMatrix\), producing an exact physical information gain \(\FiniteChange \DifferentialEntropy = \PriorDifferentialEntropy - \PosteriorDifferentialEntropy = \frac{1}{2} \ln(\det \PriorCovarianceMatrix / \det \PosteriorCovarianceMatrix)\). Under an isotropic Gaussian distribution \(\GaussianDistribution(\GaussianMeanVector, \GaussianStandardDeviation^2 \IdentityMatrix)\), this reduces to \(\DifferentialEntropy(\ContinuousRandomVariable) = \frac{\RandomVectorDimension}{2} \ln(2\pi \NaturalLogarithmBase \GaussianStandardDeviation^2)\). In continuous reinforcement learning, such as Soft Actor-Critic (SAC), the unsquashed diagonal Gaussian policy has entropy \(\PolicyEntropy(\pi(\cdot \mid \PolicyObservedState)) = \frac{\ActionDimension}{2}(1 + \ln(2\pi)) + \sum_{i=1}^{\ActionDimension} \ln \PolicyActionStandardDeviation_i(\PolicyObservedState)\) with \(\ActionDimension\) action dimensions and conditional standard deviations \(\PolicyActionStandardDeviation_i\). Entropy regularization encourages exploration; after bounding actions with a nonlinear transformation, its Jacobian changes this entropy, as the SAC derivation below shows.

1 Shannon Information Entropy: Entropy quantifies average uncertainty within a specified discrete distribution. Its physical meaning depends on whether that distribution is calibrated to the relevant referents and observations.

To quantify information divergence between an empirical demonstrator distribution \(\ProbabilityFunction\) and a learned policy distribution \(\ApproximatingDistribution\), information theory defines the Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence (relative entropy): \[ \KlDivergence(\ProbabilityFunction \,\parallel\, \ApproximatingDistribution) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^\RandomVectorDimension} \ProbabilityFunction(\ContinuousOutcome) \ln \left( \frac{\ProbabilityFunction(\ContinuousOutcome)}{\ApproximatingDistribution(\ContinuousOutcome)} \right) d\ContinuousOutcome \] where \(\KlDivergence(\ProbabilityFunction \parallel \ApproximatingDistribution) \ge 0\) by Gibbs’ inequality, with equality if and only if \(\ProbabilityFunction = \ApproximatingDistribution\) almost everywhere.

In closed-loop decision making, Shannon entropy provides an instantaneous monitor of policy ambiguity. When a vision-language planner encounters an underspecified scene with two equally probable referents (such as two identical cups), the discrete action entropy approaches its maximum \(\ShannonEntropy(\DiscreteRandomVariable) \to \log_2 2 = 1.0\text{ bit}\). A calibrated threshold gates actuation, issuing an ambiguity fault (ERR_GROUNDING_AMBIGUITY) that commands a stationary hold while active perception collects disambiguating viewpoints. However, because an overfitted neural network can be confidently wrong, entropy alone cannot guarantee semantic correctness; it must operate in tandem with physical clearance monitors.

Softmax overconfidence and calibration error

A perception classifier that reports a \(0.99\) softmax score on a wet-floor reflection or an unfamiliar obstacle is measuring logit separation rather than empirical accuracy. Standard neural classification heads normalize raw logit vectors \(\LogitVector = [\OutputLogit_1, \dots, \OutputLogit_{\ClassCount}]^\top \in \mathbb{R}^\ClassCount\) into categorical distributions using the softmax function: \[ \SoftmaxFunction(\LogitVector)_i = \frac{e^{\OutputLogit_i / \CalibrationTemperature}}{\sum_{j=1}^\ClassCount e^{\OutputLogit_j / \CalibrationTemperature}} \] where \(\CalibrationTemperature > 0\) is a temperature hyperparameter (defaulting to \(\CalibrationTemperature = 1\)). The softmax operator maps logits onto the open probability simplex \(\ProbabilitySimplex^{\ClassCount-1} = \{\ClassProbabilityVector \in \mathbb{R}^\ClassCount : \sum_i \SoftmaxOutputProbability_i = 1, \SoftmaxOutputProbability_i > 0\}\) strictly through algebraic exponentiation.

A cross-entropy training objective does not by itself establish calibration. At \(\CalibrationTemperature=1\), for target class \(\ExampleLabel \in \{1, \dots, \ClassCount\}\), the cross-entropy loss evaluates to \(\CrossEntropyLoss(\LogitVector, \ExampleLabel) = -\CorrectClassLogit + \ln \sum_{j=1}^\ClassCount e^{\OutputLogit_j}\). Differentiating with respect to any logit \(\OutputLogit_i\): \[ \frac{\partial \CrossEntropyLoss}{\partial \OutputLogit_i} = \frac{e^{\OutputLogit_i}}{\sum_{j=1}^\ClassCount e^{\OutputLogit_j}} - \EventIndicator(\LabelCategoryIndex = \ExampleLabel) = \SoftmaxFunction(\LogitVector)_i - \EventIndicator(\LabelCategoryIndex = \ExampleLabel) \] For the ground-truth class \(\ExampleLabel\), the derivative is negative until its predicted probability approaches unity. For an individually fitted example, cross-entropy decreases as the correct class separates from the other logits; separable, unregularized training can therefore keep increasing those margins. This is not a proof that every trained network is overconfident or that noisy examples can all be fitted simultaneously. A large softmax output measures relative logit separation, while calibration asks whether predictions with that confidence are correct at the corresponding frequency.

To quantify whether predicted probabilities match empirical physical frequencies, reliability engineering computes the Expected Calibration Error (ECE) and Maximum Calibration Error (MCE):2 \[ \text{ECE} = \sum_{\CalibrationBinIndex=1}^{\CalibrationBinCount} \frac{|\CalibrationBinExampleSet|}{\EvaluationSampleCount} \left| \text{acc}(\CalibrationBinExampleSet) - \text{conf}(\CalibrationBinExampleSet) \right|, \qquad \text{MCE} = \max_{\CalibrationBinIndex \in \{1, \dots, \CalibrationBinCount\}} \left| \text{acc}(\CalibrationBinExampleSet) - \text{conf}(\CalibrationBinExampleSet) \right| \] Empty bins contribute zero to ECE and are omitted from MCE; accuracy and confidence averages are defined only for nonempty bins. Here \(\CalibrationBinExampleSet\) is the set of validation examples whose predicted confidence falls in \(((\CalibrationBinIndex-1)/\CalibrationBinCount,\CalibrationBinIndex/\CalibrationBinCount]\). The size \(|\CalibrationBinExampleSet|\) is the sample count in bin \(\CalibrationBinIndex\), and \(\EvaluationSampleCount\) is the total validation sample count. The mean predicted confidence is \(\text{conf}(\CalibrationBinExampleSet) = \frac{1}{|\CalibrationBinExampleSet|} \sum_{i \in \CalibrationBinExampleSet} \PredictedConfidence_i\), and the empirical accuracy is \(\text{acc}(\CalibrationBinExampleSet) = \frac{1}{|\CalibrationBinExampleSet|} \sum_{i \in \CalibrationBinExampleSet} \EventIndicator(\ModelPrediction_i = \ExampleLabel_i)\).

2 Expected Calibration Error: Binned calibration metrics quantify the divergence between neural prediction confidence and realized empirical accuracy across confidence intervals. Separable, unregularized cross-entropy training can keep increasing logit margins; whether a fitted network is overconfident must be assessed on representative held-out data. Relying on uncalibrated confidence scores to gate safety fallbacks permits undetected distribution shifts to corrupt closed-loop actuation.

To recalibrate an overconfident model post-hoc without altering its classification accuracy, temperature scaling optimizes a single scalar parameter \(\CalibrationTemperature > 0\) on a held-out calibration dataset \(\ConfidenceCalibrationDataset\): \[ \CalibrationTemperature^* = \arg\min_{\CalibrationTemperature > 0} \left[ - \sum_{(\InputFeatureVector, \ExampleLabel) \in \ConfidenceCalibrationDataset} \ln \left( \frac{e^{\CorrectClassLogit(\InputFeatureVector) / \CalibrationTemperature}}{\sum_{j=1}^\ClassCount e^{\OutputLogit_j(\InputFeatureVector) / \CalibrationTemperature}} \right) \right] \] Because \(\CalibrationTemperature^*\) scales all logits uniformly, the ranking \(\arg\max_i (\OutputLogit_i / \CalibrationTemperature) = \arg\max_i \OutputLogit_i\) remains unchanged for all \(\CalibrationTemperature > 0\), preserving top-1 accuracy. Temperatures above one soften probabilities, while temperatures below one sharpen them; optimizing calibration loss does not guarantee exact calibration. However, temperature scaling rests on the classical assumption that deployment data is identically distributed to calibration data (\(\DeploymentDistribution = \CalibrationDataDistribution\)). In physical AI, an embodied agent continually encounters non-stationary environmental shifts: lens flare, wet floor reflectance, novel obstacle textures, or altered motor dynamics. Temperature scaling cannot repair or detect out-of-distribution inputs: scaling an out-of-distribution logit vector by scalar \(\CalibrationTemperature^*\) still yields near-unity confidence if the raw relative logit separation remains large. In safety-critical autonomy, post-hoc calibration requires separately validated uncertainty and clearance checks. Conformal coverage also depends on its sampling assumptions, commonly exchangeability; it does not automatically survive deployment shift.

Spatial Representations and Scene Parameterization

Detecting a door handle at pixel \((142, 88)\) in a wrist-camera image cannot tell a joint controller where to move until that pixel ray is combined with metric depth and transformed through the camera, wrist, arm, and mobile-base coordinate frames. Unlike language models that operate on discrete semantic tokens, an embodied policy must bind high-dimensional sensor arrays (RGB-D cameras, time-of-flight rangefinders, and lidars) to rigid physical links and Euclidean 3D reference frames.

Special Euclidean group \(\SpecialEuclideanGroup\) transforms

Rigid body transformations in 3D Euclidean space belong to the Special Euclidean Lie group \(\SpecialEuclideanGroup\), combining a \(3 \times 3\) orthonormal rotation matrix \(\DirectionalCosineMatrix \in \SpecialOrthogonalGroup\) (\(\DirectionalCosineMatrix^\top \DirectionalCosineMatrix = \IdentityMatrix\), \(\det(\DirectionalCosineMatrix) = +1\)) with a translation vector \(\RigidTransformTranslation \in \mathbb{R}^3\): \[ \Pose = \begin{bmatrix} \DirectionalCosineMatrix & \RigidTransformTranslation \\ \ZeroVector^\top & 1 \end{bmatrix} \in \SpecialEuclideanGroup \subset \mathbb{R}^{4 \times 4} \] A homogeneous coordinate vector \(\HomogeneousPointCoordinates = [\CartesianPosition^\top, 1]^\top \in \mathbb{R}^4\) transforms across coordinate frames via matrix-vector multiplication: \[ \TransformedHomogeneousPointCoordinates = \Pose \HomogeneousPointCoordinates \iff \TransformedCartesianPosition = \DirectionalCosineMatrix \CartesianPosition + \RigidTransformTranslation \] Let \(\HomogeneousTransform\) denote the transform mapping coordinates from frame \(\Glyph{B}\) into frame \(\Glyph{A}\). For a kinematic chain comprising world frame \(\Glyph{W}\), mobile base \(\Glyph{B}\), end effector \(\Glyph{E}\), and wrist camera \(\Glyph{C}\), the forward composite transformation evaluates via associative matrix multiplication: \[ \CameraToWorldTransform = \WorldBaseTransform \BodyEndEffectorTransform \EndEffectorCameraTransform \] The inverse transform admits an exact closed-form evaluation avoiding general \(4 \times 4\) matrix inversion: \[ \Pose^{-1} = \begin{bmatrix} \DirectionalCosineMatrix^\top & -\DirectionalCosineMatrix^\top \RigidTransformTranslation \\ \ZeroVector^\top & 1 \end{bmatrix} \]

Lie algebra \(\SpecialEuclideanAlgebra\) and manifold perturbation

Because \(\SpecialEuclideanGroup\) forms a smooth, non-Euclidean Riemannian manifold rather than a linear vector space, unconstrained optimization (such as gradient descent during camera pose estimation or neural trajectory generation) cannot directly add matrices (\(\Pose_1 + \Pose_2 \notin \SpecialEuclideanGroup\)). Regressing 3D rotations directly using Euler angles induces topological singularities (gimbal lock at pitch \(\pm 90^\circ\)) where degrees of freedom collapse. Unconstrained unit quaternions \(\UnitQuaternion \in \UnitQuaternionSphere\) suffer from the antipodal double-cover ambiguity (\(\UnitQuaternion\) and \(-\UnitQuaternion\) represent the identical physical rotation). In continuous neural policy training, minimizing \(\ell_2\) regression loss over quaternions produces severe gradient cancellation when the network predicts opposite hemispheres for nearby states. To ensure topological continuity across continuous rotations, modern spatial policies either output continuous 6D rotation representations (the first two columns of \(\DirectionalCosineMatrix\) orthonormalized via Gram-Schmidt) or parameterize local variations in the tangent space at the identity: the Lie algebra \(\SpecialEuclideanAlgebra\).

For the finite pose increment below, a twist coordinate vector \(\TwistCoordinates = [\Velocity^\top, \TwistRotationCoordinates^\top]^\top \in \mathbb{R}^6\) combines integrated translation coordinates \(\Velocity\) and rotation coordinates \(\TwistRotationCoordinates\). If these symbols instead denote velocities, multiply the matrix twist by elapsed time before exponentiating. The wedge operator \((\cdot)^{\TwistWedgeOperator}\) maps \(\TwistCoordinates\) to its matrix Lie algebra representation: \[ \TwistMatrix = \TwistCoordinates^{\TwistWedgeOperator} = \begin{bmatrix} [\TwistRotationCoordinates]_\times & \Velocity \\ \ZeroVector^\top & 0 \end{bmatrix} \in \SpecialEuclideanAlgebra, \quad \text{where } [\TwistRotationCoordinates]_\times = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & -\ZAxisRotationCoordinate & \YAxisRotationCoordinate \\ \ZAxisRotationCoordinate & 0 & -\XAxisRotationCoordinate \\ -\YAxisRotationCoordinate & \XAxisRotationCoordinate & 0 \end{bmatrix} \] The exponential map \(\exp: \SpecialEuclideanAlgebra \to \SpecialEuclideanGroup\) computes finite rigid transformations in closed form via Rodrigues’ formula: \[ \exp(\TwistMatrix) = \begin{bmatrix} \exp([\TwistRotationCoordinates]_\times) & \TwistTranslationMatrix \Velocity \\ \ZeroVector^\top & 1 \end{bmatrix} \] where \(\TwistRotationAngle = \|\TwistRotationCoordinates\|\), \(\exp([\TwistRotationCoordinates]_\times) = \IdentityMatrix + \frac{\sin\TwistRotationAngle}{\TwistRotationAngle} [\TwistRotationCoordinates]_\times + \frac{1 - \cos\TwistRotationAngle}{\TwistRotationAngle^2} [\TwistRotationCoordinates]_\times^2\), and \(\TwistTranslationMatrix = \IdentityMatrix + \frac{1 - \cos\TwistRotationAngle}{\TwistRotationAngle^2} [\TwistRotationCoordinates]_\times + \frac{\TwistRotationAngle - \sin\TwistRotationAngle}{\TwistRotationAngle^3} [\TwistRotationCoordinates]_\times^2\).

In spatial tracking and state estimation, small pose uncertainties are modeled by perturbing the mean transform with a zero-mean Gaussian twist vector in the local frame: \(\PerturbedPose = \Pose \exp(\PoseTwistPerturbationMatrix)\), with \(\PoseTwistPerturbation \sim \GaussianDistribution(\ZeroVector, \TwistCovarianceMatrix)\) and covariance \(\TwistCovarianceMatrix \in \mathbb{R}^{6 \times 6}\). The exponential keeps the updated pose on \(\SpecialEuclideanGroup\). The covariance describes local tangent coordinates, not a Gaussian distribution over matrix entries; its approximation is reliable only while the local perturbation model is adequate. At zero rotation, evaluate the coefficients above by their continuous limits to avoid division by zero.

Visual patch tokenization, attention, and 3D metric lifting

A vision transformer that slices three \(224 \times 224\) camera frames into \(14 \times 14\)-pixel patches produces 768 visual tokens per control step. Yet none of those tokens carries metric 3D distance until camera intrinsics and depth measurements lift the 2D image plane into Euclidean space. Embodied vision-language-action (VLA) models and spatial transformers convert continuous sensory streams into discrete tokens for neural attention, then lift candidate spatial predictions into 3D coordinates for physical reasoning. Learned Representations and The Cost of a Fresh Evaluation use the token counts derived here to size the application processor’s memory traffic.

Convolutional encoders versus vision transformer tokenization

Physical AI architectures employ two primary paradigms for encoding raw camera observations into visual features:

  1. Convolutional Encoders (ConvNets): Classical visuomotor backbones (such as ResNet-18/34/50 or modern ConvNeXt variants) process images through hierarchical sliding-window convolutions. Convolutions possess an intrinsic inductive bias: translational equivariance (\(\text{Conv}(\text{Shift}(\CameraImage)) = \text{Shift}(\text{Conv}(\CameraImage))\)) and local spatial compactness. Because small \(3 \times 3\) filter weights are repeatedly applied across thousands of image pixel locations, convolutional feature extraction achieves high arithmetic intensity (FLOPs per byte transferred from DRAM) in early layers. Intermediate feature activations stream through on-chip SRAM line buffers, minimizing off-chip memory bandwidth consumption and reducing traffic when the accelerator mapping and buffer capacity permit reuse. Deterministic latency still requires a timing analysis under contention. However, local receptive fields require deep network stacks to capture global scene context, limiting their ability to resolve long-range semantic relationships (such as attending from a robot gripper to a distant receptacle).
  2. Vision Transformers (ViTs): Vision transformers discard local sliding windows in favor of non-overlapping patch tokenization and global multi-head self-attention. While ViTs lack hardwired translational equivariance, they establish global receptive fields across the entire image at layer 1. The operational trade-off is computational complexity and memory bandwidth: self-attention scales quadratically (\(\mathcal{O}(\PatchTokenCount^2)\)) in token count \(\PatchTokenCount\). On embedded edge accelerators operating at single-frame batch sizes, materializing full \(\PatchTokenCount \times \PatchTokenCount\) attention matrices generates substantial activation memory traffic that stresses shared system memory buses.

2D vision transformer patch tokenization

Consider an RGB image \(\CameraImage \in \mathbb{R}^{\ImageHeight \times \ImageWidth \times \ImageChannelCount}\) captured by an onboard camera with spatial resolution \(\ImageHeight \times \ImageWidth\) and \(\ImageChannelCount\) color channels. Feeding raw pixels to a multilayer perceptron would discard spatial locality and inflate the parameter count. A vision transformer frontend therefore partitions the image into a non-overlapping sequence of \(\PatchTokenCount\) patches of \(\VitPatchSize \times \VitPatchSize\) pixels, with \(\ImageHeight\) and \(\ImageWidth\) divisible by \(\VitPatchSize\) after any chosen crop or padding: \[ \PatchTokenCount = \frac{\ImageHeight \cdot \ImageWidth}{\VitPatchSize^2} \] Each patch, indexed by its row and column \((\PatchRowIndex, \PatchColumnIndex)\) in the camera plane, is flattened into a vector \(\VitImagePatch^{(\PatchRowIndex, \PatchColumnIndex)} \in \mathbb{R}^{\VitPatchSize^2 \ImageChannelCount}\). A trainable linear projection \(\VitPatchProjectionWeights \in \mathbb{R}^{(\VitPatchSize^2 \ImageChannelCount) \times \HiddenDimension}\) maps each flattened patch into the transformer’s hidden dimension \(\HiddenDimension\). Without positional information or position-dependent masks, self-attention is permutation-equivariant: permuting the input rows permutes the output rows. Therefore the model knows nothing of spatial proximity unless position is added explicitly: \[ \VitPatchTokenEmbedding_0^{(\PatchRowIndex, \PatchColumnIndex)} = \VitImagePatch^{(\PatchRowIndex, \PatchColumnIndex)} \VitPatchProjectionWeights + \PlanarPositionEmbedding(\PatchRowIndex, \PatchColumnIndex) + \CameraIdentifierEmbedding \] The 2D position embedding \(\PlanarPositionEmbedding(\PatchRowIndex, \PatchColumnIndex)\) decomposes along the vertical axis \(\PatchRowIndex\) and the horizontal axis \(\PatchColumnIndex\), using sinusoidal frequency bands or learned coordinate embeddings. It encodes position in the image plane and carries no metric 3D distance. When several cameras feed one model, such as an overhead camera for scene context and a wrist camera for millimeter-scale alignment, a learned camera identifier \(\CameraIdentifierEmbedding \in \{\OverheadCameraEmbedding, \WristCameraEmbedding\}\) distinguishes their reference frames. Some architectures also prepend a summary token \(\SummaryTokenEmbedding \in \mathbb{R}^{\HiddenDimension}\) to the sequence.

To make the dimensions concrete, take a square RGB frame 224 pixels on a side (illustrative; see the Reader Guide) with patch size \(\VitPatchSize =\) 14. The frame divides into 16 patches on a side, 256 in all. Each patch holds 196 pixels across 3 color channels, a flattened vector of 588 values, and \(\VitPatchProjectionWeights\) maps each such vector into a 4096-dimensional model space. One frame therefore becomes 256 tokens of width 4096. A robot with 3 cameras (two wrist cameras and one overhead) supplies 3 \(\times\) 256 = 768 visual tokens per step before any text or proprioceptive token is added.

The visual tokens then join the rest of the input. A natural-language directive such as “grasp the red mug by its handle” is tokenized by byte-pair encoding into text embeddings \(\TextTokenEmbeddings \in \mathbb{R}^{\TextTokenCount \times \HiddenDimension}\). The robot’s configuration (joint angles, joint velocities, and gripper state) passes through a linear layer into proprioceptive tokens \(\ProprioceptiveTokenEmbeddings \in \mathbb{R}^{\StateTokenCount \times \HiddenDimension}\). The three streams concatenate into one multimodal input sequence: \[ \MultimodalFusedTokenStream = [\VisionTokenEmbeddings \,|\, \TextTokenEmbeddings \,|\, \ProprioceptiveTokenEmbeddings] \in \mathbb{R}^{\FusedTokenCount \times \HiddenDimension} \]

Self-attention over the combined sequence

The combined sequence \(\MultimodalFusedTokenStream\) passes through a stack of transformer layers, each consisting of multi-head self-attention and a feed-forward network. Self-attention projects each token through learned matrices \(\QueryProjectionMatrix\), \(\KeyProjectionMatrix\), and \(\AttentionValuesProjectionMatrix\) into a query \(\QueryVector_i\), a key \(\KeyVector_j\), and a value, and computes pairwise attention weights over the whole sequence: \[ \AttentionWeightEntry = \frac{\exp\left(\QueryVector_i^\top \KeyVector_j / \sqrt{\AttentionHeadDimension}\right)}{\sum_{\NormalizingKeyIndex} \exp\left(\QueryVector_i^\top \KeyVector_{\NormalizingKeyIndex} / \sqrt{\AttentionHeadDimension}\right)} \] A text token such as “handle” can attend to visual patch tokens, but a high attention weight is not a verified grasp location or metric pose. A trained spatial decoder may produce a 2D affordance heatmap, and placing the gripper in 3D still takes depth evidence, camera calibration, and the metric lifting below. A rolling key-value (KV) cache stores prior token states for attention. It neither tracks object permanence nor maintains a 3D position and velocity; an explicit state estimator must associate observations across frames and widen its uncertainty while the mug is occluded.

Pinhole back-projection and 3D metric lifting

Standard 2D patch tokens lack metric scale due to projective ambiguity: along any optical ray \(\ProjectiveRayScale [\PixelColumnCoordinate, \PixelRowCoordinate, 1]^\top\), infinitely many 3D points project to identical pixel coordinates. To interface with physical control barrier filters, kinetic stopping calculators, and obstacle clearance envelopes, 2D visual predictions must be lifted into metric 3D coordinates \(\WorldFramePoint \in \mathbb{R}^3\).

Let \([\PixelColumnCoordinate, \PixelRowCoordinate]^\top\) denote pixel coordinates on the image sensor, and let the calibrated pinhole camera intrinsic matrix be: \[ \CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix = \begin{bmatrix} \HorizontalFocalLength & 0 & \PrincipalPointColumn \\ 0 & \VerticalFocalLength & \PrincipalPointRow \\ 0 & 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} \] where \((\HorizontalFocalLength, \VerticalFocalLength)\) are focal lengths in pixels and \((\PrincipalPointColumn, \PrincipalPointRow)\) is the principal point. Given an aligned optical-axis depth measurement \(\MeasuredPixelDepth = \CameraFrameDepth > 0\) (obtained from stereo disparity, time-of-flight lidar, or calibrated depth sensors), the 3D position in the camera coordinate frame \(\CameraFramePoint \in \mathbb{R}^3\) is recovered via inverse intrinsic projection: \[ \CameraFramePoint = \MeasuredPixelDepth \cdot \CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix^{-1} \begin{bmatrix} \PixelColumnCoordinate \\ \PixelRowCoordinate \\ 1 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} (\PixelColumnCoordinate - \PrincipalPointColumn) \frac{\MeasuredPixelDepth}{\HorizontalFocalLength} \\ (\PixelRowCoordinate - \PrincipalPointRow) \frac{\MeasuredPixelDepth}{\VerticalFocalLength} \\ \MeasuredPixelDepth \end{bmatrix} \] Transforming \(\CameraFramePoint\) into the robot base or world frame \(\Glyph{W}\) uses the rigid extrinsic transform \(\CameraToWorldTransform \in \SpecialEuclideanGroup\) defined in this section: \[ \HomogeneousWorldFramePoint = \CameraToWorldTransform \HomogeneousCameraFramePoint \implies \WorldFramePoint = \CameraToWorldRotation \CameraFramePoint + \CameraToWorldTranslation \] A sensor reporting range along a ray must first convert it to optical-axis depth; substituting raw range for \(\MeasuredPixelDepth\) distorts off-axis geometry. The permission path and kinematic reachability evaluators require this metric 3D reconstruction; ungrounded 2D tokens cannot establish whether a proposed trajectory satisfies physical clearance boundaries \(\StoppingDistance\).

Camera-to-bird’s-eye-view lifting

Checking whether a mobile base can pass a warehouse pillar requires obstacle footprints in meters on the floor plane, whereas each onboard camera observes the aisle in perspective pixels along rays of unknown depth. A bird’s-eye-view (BEV) representation places the features of every camera in one metric grid on the ground plane, the frame in which planners and clearance checks work. Figure 1 stages this transformation across four sequential processing blocks. Monocular 2D feature extraction works on the raw camera images. Categorical depth distribution estimation lifts each feature ray into a 3D frustum of discrete hypothesis bins. Extrinsic coordinate transformation splats the frustum points into a metric ground-plane grid of vertical pillars. A final permission gate bounds spatial clearance residuals before dispatching commands.

  1. Feature extraction. A vision backbone, such as DINOv2 (Oquab et al. 2023) or a lightweight convolutional network, maps image \(\CameraImage \in \mathbb{R}^{\ImageHeight \times \ImageWidth \times 3}\) to a grid of feature vectors: \[ \FeatureCellVector = \text{Backbone}(\CameraImage)[\PixelColumnCoordinate, \PixelRowCoordinate] \in \mathbb{R}^\FeatureChannelCount \] Forward projection (equation 8.2) collapses each 3D ray onto one pixel. Consequently, a feature \(\FeatureCellVector\) stands for a whole ray in space while its metric depth remains unknown.

  2. Lift. LSS represents that ambiguity rather than resolving it. Along the bearing ray \(\CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix^{-1}[\PixelColumnCoordinate, \PixelRowCoordinate, 1]^\top\) of equation 8.3, the network scores \(\DepthBinCount\) discrete depth bins spanning \([\MinimumDepthHypothesis, \MaximumDepthHypothesis]\), for example 64 bins from 0.5 m to 20 m, spaced linearly or logarithmically: \[ \DepthBinDistribution = \text{softmax}(\DepthBinLogits) \in \ProbabilitySimplex^{\DepthBinCount-1} \] An outer product lifts each 2D feature into a frustum of 3D points, one per depth hypothesis: \[ \FrustumFeature = \DepthBinProbability(\DepthBinIndex) \cdot \FeatureCellVector \in \mathbb{R}^\FeatureChannelCount \quad \text{at } \CameraFramePoint(\PixelColumnCoordinate, \PixelRowCoordinate, \DepthHypothesis_k) = \DepthHypothesis_k \CameraIntrinsicCalibrationMatrix^{-1} \begin{bmatrix} \PixelColumnCoordinate \\ \PixelRowCoordinate \\ 1 \end{bmatrix} \] For a feature map 128 cells on a side, with 64 depth bins and 64 feature channels, the frustum tensor holds about 67.1 million values, 134.2 MB per frame at FP16. This is a materialized tensor footprint for one camera. Actual DRAM traffic depends on whether the runtime materializes, tiles, or fuses lifting with pooling; zero-copy handoff avoids an extra copy but does not eliminate tensor reads and writes.

  3. Splat. The calibrated extrinsic transform \(\BaseCameraTransform\in \SpecialEuclideanGroup\), with rotation \(\BaseCameraRotation\) and translation \(\BaseCameraTranslation\), maps every frustum point from the camera’s optical frame into the robot base frame: \[ \BodyFramePoint = \BaseCameraRotation \CameraFramePoint + \BaseCameraTranslation \] The workspace is discretized into a grid of vertical pillars, for example 200 cells on a side covering a 20 m square at 0.1 m resolution. The splat sums every frustum feature that falls within pillar \((\BevPillarXIndex, \BevPillarYIndex)\), using accelerated cumulative sums or point-pillar pooling (Philion and Fidler 2020; Liu et al. 2023): \[ \BevPillarFeature(\BevPillarXIndex, \BevPillarYIndex) = \sum_{(\PixelColumnCoordinate, \PixelRowCoordinate, k) \in \text{Pillar}(\BevPillarXIndex, \BevPillarYIndex)} \FrustumFeature \] On parallel GPU accelerators, naive scatter-add operations create severe write-port contention and non-coalesced DRAM transactions when hundreds of frustum points map to the identical BEV pillar. Optimized runtimes eliminate this bottleneck by sorting frustum points by pillar index and executing parallel cumulative-sum prefix scans or precomputed index-table indirection (Liu et al. 2023). The splat aligns the cameras’ hypotheses in one metric frame. It cannot fill a region no camera saw or remove depth-scale error, so the footprint it assigns to a pedestrian still depends on the depth hypotheses and the calibration. That dependence is why the clearance panel closing figure 1 marks depth as estimated and only the inset as checked.

Oquab, Maxime, Timothée Darcet, Théo Moutakanni, Huy Vo, Marc Szafraniec, Vasil Khalidov, Pierre Fernandez, et al. 2023. “Dinov2: Learning Robust Visual Features Without Supervision.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2304.07193.
Liu, Zhijian, Haotian Tang, Alexander Amini, Xinyu Yang, Huizi Mao, Daniela Rus, and Song Han. 2023. “BEVFusion: Multi-Task Multi-Sensor Fusion with Unified Bird’s-Eye View Representation.” IEEE International Conference on Robotics and Automation (ICRA), 2774–81.

The softmax over depth bins expresses weights the model assigns, and its entropy is not a calibrated metric covariance. The clearance inset of Spatial Error and Clearance Bounds therefore rests on separately validated spatial residuals, not on the lifted weights.

Inverse-depth parameterization

In visual-inertial odometry and SLAM, representing distant 3D landmarks in standard Cartesian coordinates \((\CartesianXCoordinate, \CartesianYCoordinate, \CartesianZCoordinate)\) induces extreme non-linearity: small pixel tracking errors produce quadratic depth errors (\(\SmallPerturbation \CartesianZCoordinate \propto \CartesianZCoordinate^2\)). Under inverse-depth parameterization, a 3D feature is parameterized by the 6D vector \(\InverseDepthLandmarkParameters = [\CartesianXCoordinate_0, \CartesianYCoordinate_0, \CartesianZCoordinate_0, \AzimuthAngle, \ElevationAngle, \InverseDepth]^\top\). Here \((\CartesianXCoordinate_0, \CartesianYCoordinate_0, \CartesianZCoordinate_0)\) is the optical center when first observed, \((\AzimuthAngle, \ElevationAngle)\) are azimuth and elevation angles, and \(\InverseDepth = 1/\LandmarkDepth\) is inverse depth:3 \[ \CartesianPosition(\LandmarkDepth) = \begin{bmatrix} \CartesianXCoordinate_0 \\ \CartesianYCoordinate_0 \\ \CartesianZCoordinate_0 \end{bmatrix} + \frac{1}{\InverseDepth} \begin{bmatrix} \cos\ElevationAngle \sin\AzimuthAngle \\ -\sin\ElevationAngle \\ \cos\ElevationAngle \cos\AzimuthAngle \end{bmatrix} \] At optical infinity (\(\LandmarkDepth \to \infty\)), inverse depth approaches zero (\(\InverseDepth \to 0\)). This parameterization can improve estimator conditioning for distant features; covariance shape and numerical stability still depend on the measurement model, noise assumptions, and estimator implementation.

3 Inverse-Depth Parameterization: Civera and colleagues used inverse depth to represent distant visual landmarks in monocular estimation.

3D Gaussian splatting parameters

A neural scene reconstructed from camera images can render a photorealistic view of a workcell while filling unobserved regions with semi-transparent floaters or hollow shells that offer no conservative collision surface. Continuous neural scene representations parameterize physical environments through collections of 3D Gaussians (Kerbl et al. 2023), where each Gaussian \(\GaussianPrimitiveDensity_i(\CartesianPosition)\) is defined by four core physical and radiometric attributes:

  1. Spatial mean (\(\GaussianPrimitiveMean_i \in \mathbb{R}^3\)): The 3D centroid in world coordinates.
  2. Covariance matrix (\(\GaussianPrimitiveCovariance_i \in \mathbb{R}^{3 \times 3}\)): Defining ellipsoidal shape and orientation. To guarantee that \(\GaussianPrimitiveCovariance_i\) remains symmetric and positive semi-definite throughout gradient descent, it is factorized into an orthonormal rotation matrix \(\DirectionalCosineMatrix_i \in \SpecialOrthogonalGroup\) (parameterized by a unit quaternion \({\UnitQuaternion}_i\)) and a positive scaling diagonal matrix \(\GaussianPrimitiveScaleMatrix_i = \text{diag}({\GaussianFirstAxisScale}_i, {\GaussianSecondAxisScale}_i, {\GaussianThirdAxisScale}_i)\): \[ \GaussianPrimitiveCovariance_i = \DirectionalCosineMatrix_i \GaussianPrimitiveScaleMatrix_i \GaussianPrimitiveScaleMatrix_i^\top \DirectionalCosineMatrix_i^\top \]
  3. Radiance appearance (\(\GaussianViewDependentColor_i\)): Parameterized via Spherical Harmonics (SH) coefficients up to degree 3 (16 coefficients per color channel) to capture view-dependent specular reflections.
  4. Opacity (\(\GaussianPrimitiveOpacity_i \in [0, 1]\)): Modeled via a sigmoid activation \(\GaussianPrimitiveOpacity_i = \SigmoidFunction(\GaussianOpacityLogit_i) = \frac{1}{1 + e^{-\GaussianOpacityLogit_i}}\) over unbounded logit \(\GaussianOpacityLogit_i \in \mathbb{R}\) to bound opacity. Sigmoid gradients can still vanish near saturation.

Camera projection and screen-space covariance

During real-time novel-view synthesis, 3D Gaussians are transformed into the camera optical frame via rigid extrinsic transform \(\WorldToCameraTransform\in \SpecialEuclideanGroup\), where \(\WorldToCameraRotation \in \SpecialOrthogonalGroup\) is the rotational component: \[ \CameraFramePoint = [\CartesianXCoordinate, \CartesianYCoordinate, \CartesianZCoordinate]^\top = \WorldToCameraRotation \GaussianPrimitiveMean_i + \WorldToCameraTranslation \] The pinhole camera model projects 3D point \(\CameraFramePoint\) to image pixel coordinates \([\PixelColumnCoordinate, \PixelRowCoordinate]^\top\): \[ \pi(\CartesianXCoordinate, \CartesianYCoordinate, \CartesianZCoordinate) = \begin{bmatrix} \HorizontalFocalLength \frac{\CartesianXCoordinate}{\CartesianZCoordinate} + \PrincipalPointColumn \\ \VerticalFocalLength \frac{\CartesianYCoordinate}{\CartesianZCoordinate} + \PrincipalPointRow \end{bmatrix} \] Because perspective projection is non-linear in depth \(\CartesianZCoordinate\), 3D Gaussian Splatting applies an affine first-order Taylor approximation evaluated at the Gaussian center \(\CameraFramePoint\). The projection Jacobian \(\FunctionJacobian \in \mathbb{R}^{2 \times 3}\) evaluates to: \[ \FunctionJacobian = \frac{\partial \pi}{\partial \CameraFramePoint} = \begin{bmatrix} \frac{\partial \PixelColumnCoordinate}{\partial \CartesianXCoordinate} & \frac{\partial \PixelColumnCoordinate}{\partial \CartesianYCoordinate} & \frac{\partial \PixelColumnCoordinate}{\partial \CartesianZCoordinate} \\ \frac{\partial \PixelRowCoordinate}{\partial \CartesianXCoordinate} & \frac{\partial \PixelRowCoordinate}{\partial \CartesianYCoordinate} & \frac{\partial \PixelRowCoordinate}{\partial \CartesianZCoordinate} \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \frac{\HorizontalFocalLength}{\CartesianZCoordinate} & 0 & -\frac{\HorizontalFocalLength \CartesianXCoordinate}{\CartesianZCoordinate^2} \\ 0 & \frac{\VerticalFocalLength}{\CartesianZCoordinate} & -\frac{\VerticalFocalLength \CartesianYCoordinate}{\CartesianZCoordinate^2} \end{bmatrix} \] The projected 2D screen-space covariance \(\ScreenSpaceCovariance \in \mathbb{R}^{2 \times 2}\) is then: \[ \ScreenSpaceCovariance = \FunctionJacobian \WorldToCameraRotation \GaussianPrimitiveCovariance_i \WorldToCameraRotation^\top \FunctionJacobian^\top \] where a low-pass filter (\(\FilteredScreenSpaceCovariance = \ScreenSpaceCovariance + \AntialiasingFilterVariance \IdentityMatrix_2\) with \(\AntialiasingFilterVariance \approx 0.3\text{ pixel}^2\)) is added to prevent aliasing when a Gaussian projects to sub-pixel footprints.

Differentiable alpha-blending and clearance limits

To render pixel color \(\RenderedPixelColor(\PixelColumnCoordinate, \PixelRowCoordinate)\), tile-based rasterization bins Gaussians into \(16 \times 16\) pixel screen tiles, sorts overlapping Gaussians along the camera optical axis by depth \(\CartesianZCoordinate\), and computes front-to-back alpha-blended volume rendering: \[ \RenderedPixelColor = \sum_{i \in \OverlappingGaussianSet} \GaussianViewDependentColor_i \PixelOpacityContribution_i \CumulativeTransmittance_i, \quad \text{where } \CumulativeTransmittance_i = \prod_{j=1}^{\GaussianPrimitiveIndex-1} (1 - \PixelOpacityContribution_j) \] Here \(\CumulativeTransmittance_i\) represents cumulative optical transmittance along the viewing ray, initialized to \(\CumulativeTransmittance_1=1\). In this rendering equation, \(\PixelOpacityContribution_i\) is the pixel-dependent opacity contribution, including the projected Gaussian footprint, rather than the stored opacity \(\GaussianPrimitiveOpacity_i\). An implementation may terminate when the remaining transmittance is sufficiently small; the error tolerance, scene density, and hardware determine the resulting speed.

However, a physical AI systems engineer must recognize the boundary between visual rendering and physical clearance. The 3D Gaussian parameters are optimized strictly against 2D photometric rendering loss (such as \(\ell_1\) color difference and SSIM). Gaussians frequently fit unobserved scene regions with semi-transparent “floaters,” hollow shells, or blurred volumetric density. Neither the stored opacity \(\GaussianPrimitiveOpacity_i\) nor the spatial gradient \(\nabla_{\CartesianPosition} \GaussianPrimitiveDensity_i(\CartesianPosition)\) of its density constitutes a conservative collision surface. Figure 2 partitions this architectural handoff across three functional panels. Panel (a) tracks camera poses from an incoming RGB-D stream. Panel (b) maintains the 3D Gaussian memory primitives (mean, covariance ellipsoid, opacity, and spherical-harmonic color) under adaptive splitting, cloning, and pruning. Panel (c) decouples photometric rasterization from physical safety, routing Gaussian primitives through an unvalidated candidate occupancy or signed distance field before an explicit error-bounding step admits the clearance envelope.

Storage per primitive depends on its packing and spherical-harmonic degree, so a map’s footprint is its primitive count times that packing. A dense cubic grid instead stores \(\mathcal{O}(\GridSideCellCount^3)\) cells, and a sparse occupancy map stores only allocated structure, whose size depends on the observed geometry and the accuracy target. Figure 3 plots these three storage scaling regimes on logarithmic axes as grid resolution spans from 10 to \(10^4\) cells per dimension. The cubic grid grows steeply as \(\mathcal{O}(\GridSideCellCount^3)\) and passes gigabytes at modest resolutions. The surface-allocated octree scales along an \(\mathcal{O}(\GridSideCellCount^2)\) trajectory. The stylized Gaussian scene maintains a flat baseline under an assumed fixed primitive count of 500,000 elements.

For the Gaussian packing in the plot, 500,000 primitives at 56 bytes each require 28 MB. A deployment chooses its representation after measuring map completeness, update time, query time, and the conversion needed for conservative clearance.

Log-odds occupancy and signed-distance fusion

When a pallet jack moves out of a warehouse aisle, an occupancy map that records only range hits leaves a phantom obstacle blocking the path unless subsequent sensor rays actively clear the empty sightlines. Discrete volumetric grids model 3D occupancy uncertainty through recursive Bayesian estimation. Let \(\VoxelOccupancyState_i \in \{0, 1\}\) denote the binary occupancy state of voxel \(\VoxelIndex\) (\(\VoxelOccupancyState_i = 1\) occupied, \(\VoxelOccupancyState_i = 0\) free). Given a temporal sequence of range observations \(\RangeObservationHistory = (\RangeObservation_1, \dots, \RangeObservation_t)\), Bayes’ rule expresses the posterior occupancy: \[ \ProbabilityFunction(\VoxelOccupancyState_i \mid \RangeObservationHistory) = \frac{\ProbabilityFunction(\RangeObservation_t \mid \VoxelOccupancyState_i, \PreviousRangeObservationHistory) \ProbabilityFunction(\VoxelOccupancyState_i \mid \PreviousRangeObservationHistory)}{\ProbabilityFunction(\RangeObservation_t \mid \PreviousRangeObservationHistory)} \] Assuming that observation \(\RangeObservation_t\) is conditionally independent of past observations given the true static occupancy \(\VoxelOccupancyState_i\) (\(\ProbabilityFunction(\RangeObservation_t \mid \VoxelOccupancyState_i, \PreviousRangeObservationHistory) = \ProbabilityFunction(\RangeObservation_t \mid \VoxelOccupancyState_i)\)), and writing the identical relation for the unoccupied state \(\neg \VoxelOccupancyState_i\) (\(\VoxelOccupancyState_i = 0\)): \[ \ProbabilityFunction(\neg \VoxelOccupancyState_i \mid \RangeObservationHistory) = \frac{\ProbabilityFunction(\RangeObservation_t \mid \neg \VoxelOccupancyState_i) \ProbabilityFunction(\neg \VoxelOccupancyState_i \mid \PreviousRangeObservationHistory)}{\ProbabilityFunction(\RangeObservation_t \mid \PreviousRangeObservationHistory)} \] Dividing the two equations cancels the common evidence normalizer \(\ProbabilityFunction(\RangeObservation_t \mid \PreviousRangeObservationHistory)\): \[ \frac{\ProbabilityFunction(\VoxelOccupancyState_i \mid \RangeObservationHistory)}{\ProbabilityFunction(\neg \VoxelOccupancyState_i \mid \RangeObservationHistory)} = \frac{\ProbabilityFunction(\RangeObservation_t \mid \VoxelOccupancyState_i)}{\ProbabilityFunction(\RangeObservation_t \mid \neg \VoxelOccupancyState_i)} \frac{\ProbabilityFunction(\VoxelOccupancyState_i \mid \PreviousRangeObservationHistory)}{\ProbabilityFunction(\neg \VoxelOccupancyState_i \mid \PreviousRangeObservationHistory)} \] Applying Bayes’ rule to the measurement likelihood \(\ProbabilityFunction(\RangeObservation_t \mid \VoxelOccupancyState_i) = \frac{\ProbabilityFunction(\VoxelOccupancyState_i \mid \RangeObservation_t) \ProbabilityFunction(\RangeObservation_t)}{\ProbabilityFunction(\VoxelOccupancyState_i)}\) gives the measurement odds ratio: \[ \frac{\ProbabilityFunction(\RangeObservation_t \mid \VoxelOccupancyState_i)}{\ProbabilityFunction(\RangeObservation_t \mid \neg \VoxelOccupancyState_i)} = \frac{\ProbabilityFunction(\VoxelOccupancyState_i \mid \RangeObservation_t)}{1 - \ProbabilityFunction(\VoxelOccupancyState_i \mid \RangeObservation_t)} \frac{1 - \ProbabilityFunction(\VoxelOccupancyState_i)}{\ProbabilityFunction(\VoxelOccupancyState_i)} \] Taking the natural logarithm of the odds ratio defines the log-odds quantity \(\OccupancyLogOdds(\DiscreteOutcome) = \ln \frac{\ProbabilityFunction(\DiscreteOutcome)}{1 - \ProbabilityFunction(\DiscreteOutcome)}\). Substituting yields the canonical additive log-odds update:4 \[ \OccupancyLogOdds_t(\VoxelOccupancyState_i) = \OccupancyLogOdds_{t-1}(\VoxelOccupancyState_i) + \underbrace{\ln \frac{\ProbabilityFunction(\VoxelOccupancyState_i \mid \RangeObservation_t)}{1 - \ProbabilityFunction(\VoxelOccupancyState_i \mid \RangeObservation_t)}}_{\text{inverse sensor model } \InverseSensorLogOdds} - \underbrace{\ln \frac{\ProbabilityFunction(\VoxelOccupancyState_i)}{1 - \ProbabilityFunction(\VoxelOccupancyState_i)}}_{\text{prior belief } \OccupancyLogOdds_0} \] where \(\ProbabilityFunction(\VoxelOccupancyState_i \mid \RangeObservation_t)\) is the calibrated inverse sensor model. When prior belief is uninformative (\(\ProbabilityFunction(\VoxelOccupancyState_i) = 0.5 \implies \OccupancyLogOdds_0 = 0\)), the update reduces to simple addition: \(\OccupancyLogOdds_t(\VoxelOccupancyState_i) = \OccupancyLogOdds_{t-1}(\VoxelOccupancyState_i) + \InverseSensorLogOdds\).

4 Recursive Log-Odds Occupancy: The log-odds formulation maps probability values from the bounded unit interval to the unbounded real line, turning nonlinear Bayesian product updates into fast additions. Dynamic ranges are clamped by upper and lower saturation bounds to prevent saturation lockup. Failure to clamp log-odds values delays the clearing of dynamic obstacles when physical objects vacate the workspace.

Along a range sensor ray, voxels traversed before the hit receive negative log-odds evidence (\(\FreeSpaceLogOddsIncrement < 0\)), while the voxel containing the return receives positive evidence (\(\OccupiedLogOddsIncrement > 0\)). Voxels beyond the return receive no update. With the uninformative prior used here, zero evidence corresponds to \(\InverseSensorLogOdds=0\). To prevent numerical saturation lockup where an obstacle that was observed occupied hundreds of times requires hundreds of empty observations before clearing, updates are clamped within finite bounds \([\LogOddsLowerClamp, \LogOddsUpperClamp]\) (e.g., \([-2.0, +3.5]\)).

Octree occupancy and free-space raycasting

Occupancy grid mapping (Elfes 1989) tessellates space into cells and applies this update to every cell a ray touches. A volumetric raycast walks each return’s sightline with the three-dimensional digital differential analyzer (3D-DDA) traversal (Amanatides and Woo 1987). Hierarchical stores such as OctoMap (Hornung et al. 2013) and OpenVDB (Museth 2013) allocate cells only where rays have passed, so storage follows the observed volume rather than the bounding cube. Figure 4 traces this spatial-memory pipeline across three successive representations during an indoor mobile robot traversal. Panel (a) shows the unorganized, noisy 3D point cloud acquired by a tilting range scanner. Panel (b) shows the hierarchical 8-ary octree that adaptively clusters occupied volumes. Panel (c) shows the explicit ternary classification that differentiates confirmed free-space voxels (white) and occupied obstacle cells (dark) from unobserved unknown volumes. Free-space evidence recorded along ray sightlines lets the map retire a phantom obstacle once the same region is seen empty, while a never-observed cell stays unknown however long the map runs. Certified Spatial Assertions in Scene Memory applies this rule to the aisle, where only fresh free-space evidence can vouch that a path is clear.

Elfes, Alberto. 1989. “Using Occupancy Grids for Mobile Robot Perception and Navigation.” Computer 22 (6): 46–57. https://doi.org/10.1109/2.30720.
Amanatides, John, and Andrew Woo. 1987. “A Fast Voxel Traversal Algorithm for Ray Tracing.” Eurographics ’87, 3–10.
Museth, Ken. 2013. “VDB: High-Resolution Sparse Volumes with Dynamic Topology.” ACM Transactions on Graphics 32 (3): 1–22. https://doi.org/10.1145/2487228.2487235.

Truncated signed distance fusion

A truncated signed distance field (TSDF), fused volumetrically at camera rate by KinectFusion (Newcombe et al. 2011) and incrementally on robots by Voxblox (Oleynikova et al. 2017), stores in each voxel \(\VoxelCenterPosition\) a metric projective distance \(\TsdfAccumulatedDistance(\VoxelCenterPosition) \in [-\TsdfTruncationBand, +\TsdfTruncationBand]\). This distance is positive in front of an observed return along its sensing ray. Each voxel also stores a dimensionless fusion weight \(\TsdfAccumulatedWeight(\VoxelCenterPosition)\). For a calibrated depth image, transform the voxel center into camera coordinates \(\CameraFramePoint = [\CameraFrameHorizontalCoordinate, \CameraFrameVerticalCoordinate, \CameraFrameDepth]^\top\) and project it to pixel \((\PixelColumnCoordinate, \PixelRowCoordinate)\). Its projective sample is \(\ProjectiveDistanceSample = \MeasuredPixelDepth(\PixelColumnCoordinate, \PixelRowCoordinate) - \CameraFrameDepth\). For a range ray, the equivalent along-ray sample is \(\ProjectiveDistanceSample = \RayReturnRange - \RayVoxelRange\); both are positive before the hit. Near the return, the sample is clamped in meters and fused into the running weighted average: \[ \TruncatedDistanceSample(\VoxelCenterPosition) = \max(-\TsdfTruncationBand, \min(\TsdfTruncationBand, \ProjectiveDistanceSample)), \qquad \TsdfAccumulatedDistance_t(\VoxelCenterPosition) = \frac{\TsdfAccumulatedWeight_{t-1}(\VoxelCenterPosition) \TsdfAccumulatedDistance_{t-1}(\VoxelCenterPosition) + \TsdfObservationWeight_t \TruncatedDistanceSample(\VoxelCenterPosition)}{\TsdfAccumulatedWeight_{t-1}(\VoxelCenterPosition) + \TsdfObservationWeight_t} \] where \(\TsdfObservationWeight_t\) is the dimensionless observation weight and the stored weight \(\TsdfAccumulatedWeight_t = \TsdfAccumulatedWeight_{t-1} + \TsdfObservationWeight_t\) is capped at \(\TsdfWeightCap\). Fusion uses a valid capture-time pose and refuses a sample older than the voxel’s last evidence epoch. A negative sample just behind the measured return contributes to the local zero crossing under the surface model; it does not certify an unseen obstacle interior.

Newcombe, Richard A, Shahram Izadi, Otmar Hilliges, David Molyneaux, David Kim, Andrew J Davison, Pushmeet Kohi, Jamie Shotton, Steve Hodges, and Andrew Fitzgibbon. 2011. “KinectFusion: Real-Time Dense Surface Mapping and Tracking.” IEEE International Symposium on Mixed and Augmented Reality (ISMAR), 127–36.
Oleynikova, Helen, Zachary Taylor, Marius Fehr, Roland Siegwart, and Juan Nieto. 2017. “Voxblox: Incremental 3D Euclidean Signed Distance Fields for on-Board MAV Planning.” IEEE/RSJ International Conference on Intelligent Robots and Systems (IROS), 1366–73.

Interpolating observed samples gives an approximate gradient near a zero crossing, limited by unknown voxels, projective bias, discretization, and moving objects. A projective TSDF is therefore not the nearest-surface Euclidean distance a clearance decision needs. A clearance query therefore reads a validated occupancy or Euclidean signed distance field (ESDF) with an unknown-space policy, the conversion Certified Spatial Assertions in Scene Memory requires of every scene representation before the permission path relies on it.

One frame’s update and its memory traffic

Each incoming depth frame must pass a freshness gate before updating occupancy along its rays and fusing TSDF samples inside the truncation band (algorithm 1), and the notebook after the pseudocode prices the resulting update traffic that Certified Spatial Assertions in Scene Memory charges against the memory bus. The update procedure rejects the frame before touching the map when its capture epoch is too old or older than the last evidence of any cell it would update. It then clears and marks occupancy along each ray and fuses TSDF samples only inside the truncation band. Every updated cell takes the capture epoch, not the update time, so a later consumer can judge each cell’s freshness.

\(\mathcal{P}\)

Napkin Math 1.1: DRAM bandwidth of 3D TSDF voxel grids
Practical Engineering Question: What are the distance-and-weight payload and modeled DRAM bandwidth of sparse vs. dense TSDF representations?

1. Dense allocation. A manipulation workcell of 10 m \(\times\) 10 m \(\times\) 3 m (300 m³) at 1 cm pitch holds 300,000,000 voxels. At 4 bytes per voxel (16-bit distance plus 16-bit fusion weight), excluding occupancy, per-cell evidence epochs, and allocator overhead, the distance-and-weight payload is 1.20 GB. Refining the pitch to 5 mm for tool contact raises the count to 2,400,000,000 voxels and the partial payload to 9.60 GB, which alone exceeds an illustrative 8 GB memory budget.

2. Sparse allocation. Surfaces occupy a thin manifold within the volume. Grouping voxels into 512-voxel leaf blocks and allocating only within the truncation band, assumed to be 3 percent of the volume, gives 17,579 blocks. With a 32-byte leaf header, the payload is 36.6 MB at 1 cm and 292.5 MB at 5 mm. A full map must also budget the separate occupancy layer, per-cell epochs, block lookup and allocator metadata, and capacity for the unknown-space policy.

3. Traversal and DRAM traffic. A 640 \(\times\) 480 depth stream at 60 Hz supplies 18.432 million rays per second. Along a 3 m ray at 1 cm pitch the traversal visits 300 cells. The 294 cells before the hit receive occupancy free-ray updates, and the 6 cells near the return receive TSDF fusion. Sparse TSDF allocation saves map capacity; it removes neither the traversal nor the occupancy updates. At 8 bytes of assumed read/write traffic per update, sending every update to DRAM costs 44.2 GB/s. At an assumed 95 percent cache hit fraction for both occupancy and TSDF updates, the modeled DRAM demand falls to 2.21 GB/s, a 20× reduction. The hit rate depends on ray overlap, atomics, evictions, and writeback on the chosen hardware; it is an input to measure, not a property of the map structure.

Systems insight: Sparse allocation yields 33× less modeled TSDF payload in this surface-fraction scenario, while bus demand depends on measured locality and on all traversed-cell work. Whether that demand fits beside policy inference on a shared bus is the contention question of Contention for Shared Resources.

Function Approximation and Sensitivity Bounds

A sensor perturbation can change a neural action proposal even when the physical scene has barely moved. A sensitivity bound limits that change relative to the input error and supplies a quantity to compare with available actuator headroom. The bound concerns the proposal; the permission path must still check the plant state and enforce current limits.

Multilayer perceptrons in continuous control

For high-rate proprioceptive feedback loops (such as \(500\text{--}1000\text{ Hz}\) quadruped balance, bipedal locomotion, and arm impedance control), physical policies frequently employ Multilayer Perceptrons (MLPs) rather than deep spatial transformers. A canonical continuous MLP policy takes a low-dimensional proprioceptive vector \(\ProprioceptiveInputVector \in \mathbb{R}^{\InputCoordinateCount}\) (joint positions \(\GeneralizedCoordinates\), velocities \(\GeneralizedVelocities\), IMU angular rates, projected gravity, and previous action history). It then evaluates a sequence of \(\LayerCount\) affine transformations interleaved with element-wise non-linear activations: \[ \HiddenActivation_0 = \ProprioceptiveInputVector, \quad \HiddenActivation_{\LayerIndex} = \ActivationFunction_{\LayerIndex}(\WeightMatrix_{\LayerIndex} \HiddenActivation_{\LayerIndex-1} + \BiasVector_{\LayerIndex}) \quad \text{for } \LayerIndex=1, \dots, \LayerCount, \quad \pi(\ProprioceptiveInputVector) = \OutputLayerWeightMatrix \HiddenActivation_{\LayerCount} + \OutputLayerBiasVector \] where \(\WeightMatrix_{\LayerIndex} \in \mathbb{R}^{\LayerWidth_{\LayerIndex} \times \LayerWidth_{\LayerIndex-1}}\) are weight matrices and \(\BiasVector_{\LayerIndex} \in \mathbb{R}^{\LayerWidth_{\LayerIndex}}\) are bias vectors.

Systems profile and roofline limits of MLP policies

A compact MLP can keep its weights and activations in on-chip memory, reducing interference from camera direct memory access and other DRAM clients. Capacity alone does not establish residency: cache replacement, shared clients, scheduling, and accelerator transfers determine the memory level that supplies each layer.

For a dense layer with \(\LayerInputWidth\LayerOutputWidth\) weights, a single input vector requires approximately \(2\LayerInputWidth\LayerOutputWidth\) floating-point operations. If each weight occupies \(\WeightElementStorageSize\) bytes and is read once from the memory level being analyzed, the weight-only arithmetic intensity is \[ \MlpWeightArithmeticIntensity \approx \frac{2}{\WeightElementStorageSize}\;\text{FLOP/byte}. \] Activation traffic lowers this ratio; weight reuse across inputs raises it. The relevant roofline compares this intensity with compute throughput divided by bandwidth at that same memory level. Cache-resident weights therefore change the applicable bandwidth, not the amount of arithmetic.

A deployment budgets weights, scratch buffers, and hidden activations together, preallocates runtime storage, and measures or bounds worst-case execution under interference. A short average forward pass does not by itself justify placing inference in an interrupt handler or guarantee a control deadline.

Lipschitz continuity and spectral normalization

Bounding how strongly a network amplifies input perturbations requires a global sensitivity constant. A neural policy \(\pi: \ProprioceptiveInputSpace \to \ControlCommandSpace\) is \(\PolicyLipschitzBound\)-Lipschitz continuous if there exists a non-negative scalar constant \(\PolicyLipschitzBound\) such that for all \(\ProprioceptiveInputVector_1, \ProprioceptiveInputVector_2 \in \ProprioceptiveInputSpace\): \[ \| \pi(\ProprioceptiveInputVector_1) - \pi(\ProprioceptiveInputVector_2) \|_{\ControlCommandSpace} \le \PolicyLipschitzBound \| \ProprioceptiveInputVector_1 - \ProprioceptiveInputVector_2 \|_{\ProprioceptiveInputSpace} \] For a feedforward network with \(\LayerCount\) layers \(\pi(\ProprioceptiveInputVector) = \ActivationFunction_{\LayerCount}(\WeightMatrix_{\LayerCount} \dots \ActivationFunction_1(\WeightMatrix_1 \ProprioceptiveInputVector))\), let \({\ActivationLipschitzBound}_k\) bound each activation’s slope over the input domain. The composed Lipschitz bound is: \[ \PolicyLipschitzBound \le \prod_{k=1}^{\LayerCount} {\ActivationLipschitzBound}_k \| \WeightMatrix_k \|_2 \] where \(\|\WeightMatrix_k\|_2 = \LargestSingularNumber(\WeightMatrix_k)\) is the spectral norm (the largest singular value of weight matrix \(\WeightMatrix_k\)). Standard activations such as ReLU and Tanh are 1-Lipschitz in their scalar forms; exact GeLU has maximum derivative about \(1.1289\), so assigning it a factor of one would understate the bound.

The product is an upper bound, not necessarily the smallest Lipschitz constant. Its physical interpretation depends on normalization. A network receiving position and velocity in different units needs declared input scales; converting its normalized output to torque introduces another scale factor. Without those conversions, multiplying a position error by a dimensionless network bound cannot yield a torque error.

To bound policy sensitivity directly during training, spectral normalization reparameterizes each weight matrix by its spectral norm: \[ \SpectrallyNormalizedWeightMatrix_k = \frac{\WeightMatrix_k}{\LargestSingularNumber(\WeightMatrix_k)} \] where \(\LargestSingularNumber(\WeightMatrix_k)\) is estimated efficiently via power iteration during forward passes. Division by the exact nonzero spectral norm gives \(\|\SpectrallyNormalizedWeightMatrix_k\|_2=1\). Finite power iteration generally gives an estimate, not a certified upper bound; a safety argument must account for its error. Even an exact network sensitivity bound does not prove closed-loop plant stability.

Perturbation amplification and supervisory clamping

In physical systems, input state \(\ProprioceptiveInputVector\) is corrupted by sensor measurement noise \(\SmallPerturbation \ProprioceptiveInputVector\) bounded by \(\|\SmallPerturbation \ProprioceptiveInputVector\| \le \SensorNoiseBound\). The resulting deviation in commanded control action is bounded by: \[ \| \SmallPerturbation \NominalControlCommand \| = \| \pi(\ProprioceptiveInputVector + \SmallPerturbation \ProprioceptiveInputVector) - \pi(\ProprioceptiveInputVector) \| \le \PolicyLipschitzBound \SensorNoiseBound \] The constant has output-units per input-unit once normalization is included. Compare \(\PolicyLipschitzBound\SensorNoiseBound\) with the actuator headroom at the current operating state. Spectral normalization bounds the network, while stability, safe torque, and sensor validity require a plant model and a state-dependent permission check.

Imitation Learning and Compounding Covariate Shift

Behavioral cloning fits a policy to demonstrator state-action pairs. Two different failures need different remedies: ambiguous demonstrations can make a point predictor average incompatible actions, while feedback can carry even an accurate predictor into states missing from its training data. The decoder derivations address the first problem; the coupling bound and DAgger address the second.

Mode averaging and the decoders that avoid it

When human demonstrators steer left around a central pillar half the time (\(\ContinuousActionVector = +1.0\)) and right the other half (\(\ContinuousActionVector = -1.0\)), a policy trained with mean squared error averages the two commands to \(\ContinuousActionVector = 0.0\) and drives dead center into the pillar. To see why squared-error regression produces that collision, consider an imitation learning objective minimizing mean squared error (\(\ell_2\) loss) over demonstration distribution \(\DemonstrationDataset\): \[ \min_\pi \mathbb{E}_{(\MultimodalObservation, \ContinuousActionVector) \sim \DemonstrationDataset} \left[ \| \ContinuousActionVector - \pi(\MultimodalObservation) \|_2^2 \right] \] By the law of total expectation, decomposing the error around the conditional mean \(\mathbb{E}[\ContinuousActionVector \mid \MultimodalObservation]\) yields an orthogonal variance decomposition: \[ \begin{aligned} \mathbb{E}_{\ContinuousActionVector \mid \MultimodalObservation} \left[ \| \ContinuousActionVector - \pi(\MultimodalObservation) \|_2^2 \mid \MultimodalObservation \right] &= \mathbb{E}_{\ContinuousActionVector \mid \MultimodalObservation} \left[ \| (\ContinuousActionVector - \mathbb{E}[\ContinuousActionVector \mid \MultimodalObservation]) + (\mathbb{E}[\ContinuousActionVector \mid \MultimodalObservation] - \pi(\MultimodalObservation)) \|_2^2 \mid \MultimodalObservation \right] \\ &= \mathbb{E}_{\ContinuousActionVector \mid \MultimodalObservation} \left[ \| \ContinuousActionVector - \mathbb{E}[\ContinuousActionVector \mid \MultimodalObservation] \|_2^2 \mid \MultimodalObservation \right] + \| \mathbb{E}[\ContinuousActionVector \mid \MultimodalObservation] - \pi(\MultimodalObservation) \|_2^2 \\ &\quad + 2 \left\langle \mathbb{E}_{\ContinuousActionVector \mid \MultimodalObservation}[\ContinuousActionVector - \mathbb{E}[\ContinuousActionVector \mid \MultimodalObservation] \mid \MultimodalObservation], \, \mathbb{E}[\ContinuousActionVector \mid \MultimodalObservation] - \pi(\MultimodalObservation) \right\rangle \end{aligned} \] Because \(\mathbb{E}_{\ContinuousActionVector \mid \MultimodalObservation}[\ContinuousActionVector - \mathbb{E}[\ContinuousActionVector \mid \MultimodalObservation] \mid \MultimodalObservation] = \ZeroVector\), the cross-term vanishes identically, leaving: \[ \mathbb{E}_{(\MultimodalObservation, \ContinuousActionVector) \sim \DemonstrationDataset} \left[ \| \ContinuousActionVector - \pi(\MultimodalObservation) \|_2^2 \right] = \mathbb{E}_{\MultimodalObservation} \left[ \operatorname{Tr}(\operatorname{Cov}(\ContinuousActionVector \mid \MultimodalObservation)) \right] + \mathbb{E}_{\MultimodalObservation} \left[ \| \mathbb{E}[\ContinuousActionVector \mid \MultimodalObservation] - \pi(\MultimodalObservation) \|_2^2 \right] \] The first term is the irreducible conditional variance of demonstrator behavior. The second term is non-negative and strictly minimized if and only if the policy evaluates to the conditional expectation: \[ \pi^*(\MultimodalObservation) = \mathbb{E}[\ContinuousActionVector \mid \MultimodalObservation] = \int_{\ActionSpace} \ContinuousActionVector\, \DemonstrationActionDistribution(\ContinuousActionVector \mid \MultimodalObservation) \, d\ContinuousActionVector \] In digital machine learning tasks (such as computer vision regression), predicting the conditional expectation over ambiguous targets produces a blurry reconstruction, a soft pixel average that remains safely confined to software memory. In physical AI systems, commanded actions cross the causal boundary into continuous Euclidean space governed by non-convex obstacles, joint limits, and irreversible contact dynamics.

Demonstrator behavior in physical manipulation and navigation is inherently multimodal:

  • Dodging an obstacle: an expert steers left (\(\ContinuousActionVector = +1.0\)) or right (\(\ContinuousActionVector = -1.0\)) around a central pillar with equal probability \(\Pr(\ContinuousActionVector = +1 \mid \MultimodalObservation) = \Pr(\ContinuousActionVector = -1 \mid \MultimodalObservation) = 0.5\).
  • Symmetrical manipulation: an end effector approaches a cylindrical object from azimuth \(\AzimuthAngle = 0^\circ\) or \(\AzimuthAngle = 180^\circ\).
  • Assembly routing: threading a flexible cable clockwise or counter-clockwise around a structural fastener.

Because demonstration density \(\DemonstrationActionDistribution(\ContinuousActionVector \mid \MultimodalObservation)\) is supported on disjoint, disconnected modes separated by an obstacle, the conditional expectation computes the arithmetic mean: \[ \pi^*(\MultimodalObservation) = 0.5(+1.0) + 0.5(-1.0) = 0.0 \] This mean action lies in a region of zero demonstration probability (\(\Pr(\ContinuousActionVector = 0.0 \mid \MultimodalObservation) = 0\)). In the physical workspace, the coordinate \(\ContinuousActionVector = 0.0\) corresponds precisely to the geometric position of the central obstacle. Commanding \(\pi^*(\MultimodalObservation) = 0.0\) instructs the steering actuator to hold dead center at full cruising speed, driving the physical robot directly into the pillar. In physical autonomy, regression mode averaging does not produce a cosmetic blur; it can propose a collision unless the permission path rejects it.

Decoder families

A probabilistic decoder can avoid mode averaging by parameterizing the conditional action distribution \(\DemonstrationActionDistribution(\ContinuousActionVector \mid \MultimodalObservation)\) rather than its expectation. The families below trade inference cost against distributional expressiveness; none of them supplies recovery states that the demonstrations lack. Policy Training Regimes compares them by the network evaluations each chunk costs, which is where the choice reaches the control deadline.

  1. Deterministic direct regression (MLP).
