A feedforward network trained with squared error predicts one action per observation and, with sufficient capacity and ideal optimization, fits the conditional mean. The restriction comes from the point-output objective, not from MLPs as a network family; an MLP can also parameterize a mixture or stochastic policy. One evaluation avoids iterative sampling cost but does not resolve ambiguous demonstrations.

  2. Mixture density networks. A Gaussian mixture head predicts nonnegative weights summing to one, means, and positive-definite covariances: \[ \pi(\ContinuousActionVector\mid \MultimodalObservation)=\sum_{\MixtureComponentIndex=1}^\MixtureComponentCount \MixtureComponentWeight(\MultimodalObservation)\,\GaussianDistribution(\ContinuousActionVector;\MixtureComponentMean(\MultimodalObservation),\MixtureComponentCovariance(\MultimodalObservation)). \] Sampling a component or selecting a mode preserves alternatives in one network evaluation. Computing the mixture mean would restore the original averaging problem. Covariance regularization and component coverage matter because likelihood optimization can collapse a component onto a few observations.

  3. Implicit behavioral cloning. Implicit behavioral cloning (IBC) (Florence et al. 2022) learns an energy-based model \(\ImplicitPolicyEnergyFunction(\MultimodalObservation, \ContinuousActionVector) \in \mathbb{R}\), trained with contrastive or InfoNCE objectives to assign low energy to demonstrated state-action pairs and high energy to counterfactual actions. Inference solves \[ \ContinuousActionVector^* = \arg\min_{\ContinuousActionVector} \ImplicitPolicyEnergyFunction(\MultimodalObservation, \ContinuousActionVector) \] An energy function can have several separated minima, and selecting its minimizing action can change discontinuously even when the energy network is continuous. Inference requires iterative derivative-free or gradient-based optimization whose latency and convergence depend on sample counts and accelerator parallelism, with risks of settling into suboptimal local energy minima.

  4. Conditional diffusion and flow matching. Diffusion Policy (Chi et al. 2025) and flow-matching decoders generate entire action trajectory chunks via iterative denoising or ordinary differential equation (ODE) integration. Their training targets injected noise vectors or continuous velocity fields conditioned on corrupted states, observations, and timesteps rather than clean actions, allowing multimodal sampling instead of directly regressing a clean action mean. Approximation error and the sampling procedure still affect whether distinct modes survive. Section 1.5.2 derives both objectives and prices their sampling steps.

  5. Latent-variable chunking. Action Chunking with Transformers (ACT) (Zhao et al. 2023) predicts an action trajectory chunk \(\ActionChunk_t = (\ContinuousActionVector_{t}, \ContinuousActionVector_{t+1}, \dots, \ContinuousActionVector_{t+\ActionChunkHorizon-1}) \in \mathbb{R}^{\ActionChunkHorizon \times \ActionDimension}\) from observation \(\MultimodalObservation_{t}\) using a Conditional Variational Autoencoder (CVAE). The true objective is to maximize the conditional log-marginal likelihood of demonstrated chunks across dataset \(\DemonstrationDataset\): \[ \ln \GenerativeActionModelDistribution(\ActionChunk_t \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}) = \ln \int_{\LatentVariableSpace} \GenerativeActionModelDistribution(\ActionChunk_t, \LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}) \, \DerivativeOperator\LatentStyleVariable \] Because this marginalization is intractable, ACT introduces a variational posterior \(\CvaeRecognitionEncoder(\LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t)\) parameterized by a transformer encoder. Expanding the identity with \(\GenerativeActionModelDistribution(\ActionChunk_t, \LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}) = \GenerativeActionModelDistribution(\ActionChunk_t \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \LatentStyleVariable) \GenerativeActionModelDistribution(\LatentStyleVariable)\): \[ \begin{aligned} \ln \GenerativeActionModelDistribution(\ActionChunk_t \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}) &= \int_{\LatentVariableSpace} \CvaeRecognitionEncoder(\LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t) \ln \GenerativeActionModelDistribution(\ActionChunk_t \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}) \, \DerivativeOperator\LatentStyleVariable \\ &= \int_{\LatentVariableSpace} \CvaeRecognitionEncoder(\LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t) \ln \left( \frac{\GenerativeActionModelDistribution(\ActionChunk_t, \LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t})}{\CvaeRecognitionEncoder(\LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t)} \cdot \frac{\CvaeRecognitionEncoder(\LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t)}{\GenerativeActionModelDistribution(\LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t)} \right) \DerivativeOperator\LatentStyleVariable \\ &= \int_{\LatentVariableSpace} \CvaeRecognitionEncoder(\LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t) \ln \frac{\GenerativeActionModelDistribution(\ActionChunk_t, \LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t})}{\CvaeRecognitionEncoder(\LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t)} \, \DerivativeOperator\LatentStyleVariable + \int_{\LatentVariableSpace} \CvaeRecognitionEncoder(\LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t) \ln \frac{\CvaeRecognitionEncoder(\LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t)}{\GenerativeActionModelDistribution(\LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t)} \, \DerivativeOperator\LatentStyleVariable \\ &= \mathbb{E}_{\LatentStyleVariable \sim \CvaeRecognitionEncoder(\LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t)} \left[ \ln \frac{\GenerativeActionModelDistribution(\ActionChunk_t \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \LatentStyleVariable) \GenerativeActionModelDistribution(\LatentStyleVariable)}{\CvaeRecognitionEncoder(\LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t)} \right] + \KlDivergence\left( \CvaeRecognitionEncoder(\LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t) \,\parallel\, \GenerativeActionModelDistribution(\LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t) \right) \\ &\ge \mathbb{E}_{\LatentStyleVariable \sim \CvaeRecognitionEncoder(\LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t)} \left[ \ln \GenerativeActionModelDistribution(\ActionChunk_t \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \LatentStyleVariable) \right] - \KlDivergence\left( \CvaeRecognitionEncoder(\LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t) \,\parallel\, \GenerativeActionModelDistribution(\LatentStyleVariable) \right) \equiv \EvidenceLowerBound(\ModelParameters, \CvaeEncoderParameters) \end{aligned} \] Assuming standard isotropic Gaussian prior \(\GenerativeActionModelDistribution(\LatentStyleVariable) = \GaussianDistribution(\ZeroVector, \IdentityMatrix)\) and diagonal Gaussian variational posterior \(\CvaeRecognitionEncoder(\LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t) = \GaussianDistribution(\LatentPosteriorMean, \operatorname{diag}(\LatentPosteriorStandardDeviation^2))\), the Kullback-Leibler divergence evaluates in closed form: \[ \KlDivergence\left( \CvaeRecognitionEncoder(\LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t) \,\parallel\, \GaussianDistribution(\ZeroVector, \IdentityMatrix) \right) = \frac{1}{2} \sum_{j=1}^{\LatentDimension} \left( \LatentComponentStandardDeviation^2 + \LatentComponentMean^2 - 1 - \ln(\LatentComponentStandardDeviation^2) \right) \] Sampling directly as \(\LatentStyleVariable \sim \CvaeRecognitionEncoder\) is a stochastic operation that prevents backpropagation because the node has no analytical derivatives with respect to encoder parameters \(\CvaeEncoderParameters\). ACT resolves this via the reparameterization trick: isolating stochasticity into parameter-free standard normal noise \(\ReparameterizationNoise \sim \GaussianDistribution(\ZeroVector, \IdentityMatrix)\): \[ \LatentStyleVariable = \LatentPosteriorMean(\MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t) + \LatentPosteriorStandardDeviation(\MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t) \odot \ReparameterizationNoise, \quad \text{yielding } \frac{\partial \LatentStyleVariable}{\partial \LatentPosteriorMean} = \IdentityMatrix, \quad \frac{\partial \LatentStyleVariable}{\partial \LatentPosteriorStandardDeviation} = \operatorname{diag}(\ReparameterizationNoise) \] To parameterize the reconstruction likelihood, ACT models \(\GenerativeActionModelDistribution(\ActionChunk_t \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \LatentStyleVariable)\) as an isotropic Laplace distribution: \[ \GenerativeActionModelDistribution(\ActionChunk_t \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \LatentStyleVariable) = \frac{1}{(2\LaplaceLikelihoodScale)^{\ActionChunkHorizon \ActionDimension}} \exp\left( -\frac{\|\ActionChunk_t - \Policy(\MultimodalObservation_{t}, \LatentStyleVariable)\|_1}{\LaplaceLikelihoodScale} \right) \] Maximizing the log-likelihood is equivalent to minimizing the \(\ell_1\) norm \(\|\ActionChunk_t - \Policy(\MultimodalObservation_{t}, \LatentStyleVariable)\|_1\). Away from zero residual, \(\ell_1\) loss gives constant-magnitude gradients with respect to each predicted action component, whereas \(\ell_2\) loss gives greater influence to large residuals. Neither loss alone filters demonstrator tremor or guarantees freedom from overfitting; those outcomes depend on the data, regularization, and temporal model. Weighting the KL regularizer by hyperparameter \(\LatentKlWeight > 0\) yields the full training loss: \[ \ActVariationalObjective(\ModelParameters, \CvaeEncoderParameters) = \mathbb{E}_{(\MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t) \sim \DemonstrationDataset, \ReparameterizationNoise \sim \GaussianDistribution(\ZeroVector, \IdentityMatrix)} \left[ \|\ActionChunk_t - \Policy(\MultimodalObservation_{t}, \LatentPosteriorMean + \LatentPosteriorStandardDeviation \odot \ReparameterizationNoise)\|_1 + \LatentKlWeight \KlDivergence\left( \CvaeRecognitionEncoder(\LatentStyleVariable \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \ActionChunk_t) \,\parallel\, \GaussianDistribution(\ZeroVector, \IdentityMatrix) \right) \right] \]

    The latent variable \(\LatentStyleVariable \in \mathbb{R}^{\LatentDimension}\) can encode variation among demonstrations, while the decoder predicts all \(\ActionChunkHorizon\) actions together. Neither separated latent clusters nor kinematic smoothness follows from the architecture alone. Executing \(\ChunkExecutionHorizon\) actions before replanning reduces policy interventions, but increases the time before new observations can alter the proposal. Substituting \(\BehaviorCloningHorizon/\ActionChunkHorizon\) into the single-step compounding bound is not justified without new chunk-level error and cost assumptions.

    At test time the variational encoder, which requires the future demonstration chunk, is discarded. Setting \(\LatentStyleVariable=\ZeroVector\) evaluates the decoder at the prior mean in one forward pass. That latent point is not guaranteed to select the most probable demonstrated mode. Sampling the prior can expose additional modes but also poorly trained latent regions. ACT combines overlapping chunk predictions through temporal ensembling; for example, an exponentially recency-weighted convention is: \[ \ContinuousActionVector_{t} = \frac{\sum_{i=0}^{\min(\ControlStepIndex,\ActionChunkHorizon-1)} \TemporalEnsemblingWeight\,\ActionChunk_{t-i}[\ChunkStepIndex]}{\sum_{i=0}^{\min(\ControlStepIndex,\ActionChunkHorizon-1)}\TemporalEnsemblingWeight}, \] Each weight \(\TemporalEnsemblingWeight\) decays exponentially with the offset \(\ChunkStepIndex\), so this convention favors recent predictions. Averaging incompatible chunks can still create an invalid action; the admitted command must satisfy current limits.

  6. Discrete action tokens. Vision-language-action models such as RT-1, RT-2, and OpenVLA (Brohan, Brown, Carbajal, Chebotar, Dabis, et al. 2023; Brohan, Brown, Carbajal, Chebotar, Chen, et al. 2023; Kim et al. 2024) discretize continuous action dimensions into \(\ActionBinCount\) uniform bins (commonly 256) and emit categorical tokens: \[ \text{bin}(\ContinuousActionComponent_k) = \min\!\left(\ActionBinCount-1,\left\lfloor \ActionBinCount\frac{\ContinuousActionComponent_k-\ActionRangeMinimum}{\ActionRangeMaximum-\ActionRangeMinimum}\right\rfloor\right) \] For an action clipped to the declared range, this assigns one of \(\ActionBinCount\) equal-width bins, including the upper endpoint. Decoding to bin centers gives maximum scalar quantization error \((\ActionRangeMaximum-\ActionRangeMinimum)/(2\ActionBinCount)\). Categorical sampling or mode selection preserves alternatives; taking their mean reintroduces averaging. A decoder that emits one token per action dimension autoregressively needs \(\ActionDimension\) sequential predictions, although other tokenizations and parallel heads have different costs.

Florence, Pete, Corey Lynch, Feryal Behbahani, Tianhe Yu, Wenlong Huang, Fei Xia, et al. 2022. “Implicit Behavioral Cloning.” Conference on Robot Learning (CoRL), 158–68.
Zhao, Tony Z., Vikash Kumar, Sergey Levine, and Chelsea Finn. 2023. “Learning Fine-Grained Bimanual Manipulation with Low-Cost Hardware.” Robotics: Science and Systems (RSS). https://doi.org/10.15607/RSS.2023.XIX.016.
Brohan, Anthony, Noah Brown, Justice Carbajal, Yevgen Chebotar, Joseph Dabis, Chelsea Finn, Keerthana Gopalakrishnan, et al. 2023. “RT-1: Robotics Transformer for Real-World Control at Scale.” Robotics: Science and Systems (RSS). https://doi.org/10.15607/RSS.2023.XIX.025.
Brohan, Anthony, Noah Brown, Justice Carbajal, Yevgen Chebotar, Xi Chen, Krzysztof Choromanski, Tianli Ding, et al. 2023. “RT-2: Vision-Language-Action Models Transfer Web Knowledge to Robotic Control.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2307.15818.
Kim, Moo Jin, Karl Pertsch, Siddharth Karamcheti, Ted Xiao, Ashwin Balakrishna, Suraj Nair, Rafael Rafailov, et al. 2024. “OpenVLA: An Open-Source Vision-Language-Action Model.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2406.09246.

Continuous trajectory diffusion and flow matching

Sampling one distinct trajectory around an obstacle avoids the collision caused by averaging two paths, but running 16 sequential denoising passes through a 48-million-parameter U-Net takes \(40.4\text{ ms}\) and overruns a \(25\text{ ms}\) synchronous setpoint deadline. Continuous trajectory diffusion treats policy synthesis as generative denoising over continuous multi-step action trajectory chunks \(\ActionChunk \in \mathbb{R}^{\ActionChunkHorizon \times \ActionDimension}\) conditioned on visual and proprioceptive observation histories \(\ObservationHistory\) (Chi et al. 2025). Here \(\ActionChunkHorizon\) counts physical action steps, \(\ActionDimension\) counts action dimensions, and the later \(\DecoderEvaluationCount\) counts inference evaluations; diffusion time is not physical execution time. Rather than predicting an unconstrained point estimate via mean squared error, diffusion models represent complex multimodal action distributions without collapsing into mode averaging.

The closed-loop visuomotor diffusion pipeline has three coordinated architectural columns (figure 5). The left observation column encodes visual camera streams and proprioceptive joint history into a conditioning vector. The center denoising column applies a noise network across \(\DecoderEvaluationCount\) sequential reverse-diffusion steps to refine Gaussian noise into a candidate action chunk. The right dispatch column extracts an execution prefix from the prediction horizon and routes those candidate actions across the proposal boundary into the \(1\text{ kHz}\) permission path. It also discards the unexecuted tail before re-planning. The noise-prediction backbone \(\NoisePredictionNetwork\) is typically implemented using either a 1D Temporal Convolutional (Conv1D) U-Net or a Diffusion Transformer (DiT):

  • 1D temporal convolutional U-Net: Convolutions across the action horizon reuse filters at many positions. Feature-wise linear modulation (FiLM) supplies observation and diffusion-step conditioning. Layer widths, intermediate tensors, and accelerator tiling determine whether activations stay on chip.
  • Diffusion transformer (DiT): Attention connects action tokens and visual conditioning. The action horizon and conditioning length determine attention work, while kernel fusion determines how much intermediate storage reaches DRAM. Neither backbone implies a fixed parameter count or per-step latency; the worked budget below declares one illustrative configuration.

Forward diffusion and variance scheduling

The forward diffusion process progressively corrupts a ground-truth demonstrated action chunk \(\ActionChunk^0 \sim \DemonstrationChunkDistribution(\ActionChunk)\) with Gaussian noise across \(\DiffusionStepCount\) discrete timesteps (or continuous diffusion time \(\DiffusionTimeCoordinate \in [0, 1]\)): \[ \ForwardDiffusionDistribution(\NoisedActionChunk \mid \ActionChunk^{k-1}) = \GaussianDistribution\left(\NoisedActionChunk; \sqrt{1 - \DiffusionNoiseVariance_k} \ActionChunk^{k-1}, \DiffusionNoiseVariance_k \IdentityMatrix\right) \] where \(\{\DiffusionNoiseVariance_k \in (0, 1)\}_{k=1}^\DiffusionStepCount\) denotes a predefined variance schedule. Defining \(\DiffusionStepRetention_k = 1 - \DiffusionNoiseVariance_k\), the one-step transition evaluates to: \[ \NoisedActionChunk = \sqrt{\DiffusionStepRetention_k} \ActionChunk^{k-1} + \sqrt{1 - \DiffusionStepRetention_k} \DiffusionGaussianNoise_{k-1}, \quad \DiffusionGaussianNoise_{k-1} \sim \GaussianDistribution(\ZeroVector, \IdentityMatrix) \] Unrolling this recursion for \(\DiffusionStepIndex = 2\): \[ \begin{aligned} \ActionChunk^2 &= \sqrt{\DiffusionStepRetention_2} \left( \sqrt{\DiffusionStepRetention_1} \ActionChunk^0 + \sqrt{1 - \DiffusionStepRetention_1} \DiffusionGaussianNoise_0 \right) + \sqrt{1 - \DiffusionStepRetention_2} \DiffusionGaussianNoise_1 \\ &= \sqrt{\DiffusionStepRetention_2 \DiffusionStepRetention_1} \ActionChunk^0 + \sqrt{\DiffusionStepRetention_2(1 - \DiffusionStepRetention_1)} \DiffusionGaussianNoise_0 + \sqrt{1 - \DiffusionStepRetention_2} \DiffusionGaussianNoise_1 \end{aligned} \] Because the linear combination of two independent zero-mean Gaussian random variables \(\GaussianDistribution(\ZeroVector, \FirstSummandStandardDeviation^2 \IdentityMatrix)\) and \(\GaussianDistribution(\ZeroVector, \SecondSummandStandardDeviation^2 \IdentityMatrix)\) is itself a Gaussian with variance \((\FirstSummandStandardDeviation^2 + \SecondSummandStandardDeviation^2)\IdentityMatrix\), the combined variance of the noise terms simplifies algebraically: \[ \DiffusionStepRetention_2(1 - \DiffusionStepRetention_1) + (1 - \DiffusionStepRetention_2) = \DiffusionStepRetention_2 - \DiffusionStepRetention_1 \DiffusionStepRetention_2 + 1 - \DiffusionStepRetention_2 = 1 - \DiffusionStepRetention_1 \DiffusionStepRetention_2 \] Defining the cumulative variance product \(\DiffusionCumulativeSchedule_{k} = \prod_{\CumulativeScheduleStepIndex=1}^\DiffusionStepIndex \DiffusionStepRetention_{\CumulativeScheduleStepIndex}\), mathematical induction establishes the closed-form marginal distribution at any arbitrary diffusion step \(\DiffusionStepIndex\) directly from clean action chunk \(\ActionChunk^0\): \[ \ForwardDiffusionDistribution(\NoisedActionChunk \mid \ActionChunk^0) = \GaussianDistribution\left(\NoisedActionChunk; \sqrt{\DiffusionCumulativeSchedule_{k}} \ActionChunk^0, (1 - \DiffusionCumulativeSchedule_{k}) \IdentityMatrix\right) \] allowing immediate sampling of noisy trajectories during training without simulating intermediate Markov chain transitions: \[ \NoisedActionChunk = \sqrt{\DiffusionCumulativeSchedule_{k}} \ActionChunk^0 + \sqrt{1 - \DiffusionCumulativeSchedule_{k}} \DiffusionGaussianNoise, \quad \DiffusionGaussianNoise \sim \GaussianDistribution(\ZeroVector, \IdentityMatrix) \]

Reverse denoising and score-matching objective

The generative reverse process synthesizes an action trajectory from pure Gaussian noise \(\PureNoiseActionChunk \sim \GaussianDistribution(\ZeroVector, \IdentityMatrix)\) by parameterizing the conditional reverse transition distribution \(\GenerativeActionModelDistribution(\ActionChunk^{k-1} \mid \NoisedActionChunk, \ObservationHistory)\): \[ \GenerativeActionModelDistribution(\ActionChunk^{k-1} \mid \NoisedActionChunk, \ObservationHistory) = \GaussianDistribution\left(\ActionChunk^{k-1}; \ReverseTransitionMean(\NoisedActionChunk, \ObservationHistory, \DiffusionStepIndex), \ReverseTransitionCovariance_k\right) \] By Bayes’ rule, the true reverse posterior conditioned on the clean trajectory \(\ActionChunk^0\) is tractable and Gaussian: \(\ForwardDiffusionDistribution(\ActionChunk^{k-1} \mid \NoisedActionChunk, \ActionChunk^0) = \GaussianDistribution(\ActionChunk^{k-1}; \ForwardPosteriorMean_k(\NoisedActionChunk, \ActionChunk^0), \ForwardPosteriorVariance_k \IdentityMatrix)\), where: \[ \ForwardPosteriorMean_k(\NoisedActionChunk, \ActionChunk^0) = \frac{\sqrt{\DiffusionCumulativeSchedule_{k-1}} \DiffusionNoiseVariance_k}{1 - \DiffusionCumulativeSchedule_{k}} \ActionChunk^0 + \frac{\sqrt{\DiffusionStepRetention_k}(1 - \DiffusionCumulativeSchedule_{k-1})}{1 - \DiffusionCumulativeSchedule_{k}} \NoisedActionChunk \] Because \(\ActionChunk^0\) is unobserved at test time, the reverse process substitutes an estimate of \(\ActionChunk^0\) derived from Tweedie’s formula. For any Gaussian perturbation \(\PerturbedSignalVector \sim \GaussianDistribution(\PerturbedSignalVector_0, \PerturbationNoiseStandardDeviation^2 \IdentityMatrix)\) with prior density \(\ProbabilityFunction(\PerturbedSignalVector_0)\), the conditional observation density is: \[ \ProbabilityFunction(\PerturbedSignalVector \mid \PerturbedSignalVector_0) = \frac{1}{(2\pi \PerturbationNoiseStandardDeviation^2)^{\PerturbedSignalDimension/2}} \exp\left( -\frac{\|\PerturbedSignalVector - \PerturbedSignalVector_0\|^2}{2\PerturbationNoiseStandardDeviation^2} \right) \] Differentiating with respect to the noisy measurement \(\PerturbedSignalVector\): \[ \nabla_{\PerturbedSignalVector} \ProbabilityFunction(\PerturbedSignalVector \mid \PerturbedSignalVector_0) = -\frac{\PerturbedSignalVector - \PerturbedSignalVector_0}{\PerturbationNoiseStandardDeviation^2} \ProbabilityFunction(\PerturbedSignalVector \mid \PerturbedSignalVector_0) \] The marginal density of noisy observations evaluates to \(\ProbabilityFunction(\PerturbedSignalVector) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^\PerturbedSignalDimension} \ProbabilityFunction(\PerturbedSignalVector \mid \PerturbedSignalVector_0) \ProbabilityFunction(\PerturbedSignalVector_0) \, \DerivativeOperator\PerturbedSignalVector_0\). Differentiating under the integral sign: \[ \nabla_{\PerturbedSignalVector} \ProbabilityFunction(\PerturbedSignalVector) = \int_{\mathbb{R}^\PerturbedSignalDimension} \nabla_{\PerturbedSignalVector} \ProbabilityFunction(\PerturbedSignalVector \mid \PerturbedSignalVector_0) \ProbabilityFunction(\PerturbedSignalVector_0) \, \DerivativeOperator\PerturbedSignalVector_0 = -\frac{1}{\PerturbationNoiseStandardDeviation^2} \int_{\mathbb{R}^\PerturbedSignalDimension} (\PerturbedSignalVector - \PerturbedSignalVector_0) \ProbabilityFunction(\PerturbedSignalVector \mid \PerturbedSignalVector_0) \ProbabilityFunction(\PerturbedSignalVector_0) \, \DerivativeOperator\PerturbedSignalVector_0 \] Dividing both sides by the marginal density \(\ProbabilityFunction(\PerturbedSignalVector)\): \[ \frac{\nabla_{\PerturbedSignalVector} \ProbabilityFunction(\PerturbedSignalVector)}{\ProbabilityFunction(\PerturbedSignalVector)} = \nabla_{\PerturbedSignalVector} \ln \ProbabilityFunction(\PerturbedSignalVector) = -\frac{1}{\PerturbationNoiseStandardDeviation^2} \left( \PerturbedSignalVector - \int_{\mathbb{R}^\PerturbedSignalDimension} \PerturbedSignalVector_0 \frac{\ProbabilityFunction(\PerturbedSignalVector \mid \PerturbedSignalVector_0) \ProbabilityFunction(\PerturbedSignalVector_0)}{\ProbabilityFunction(\PerturbedSignalVector)} \, \DerivativeOperator\PerturbedSignalVector_0 \right) = -\frac{\PerturbedSignalVector - \mathbb{E}[\PerturbedSignalVector_0 \mid \PerturbedSignalVector]}{\PerturbationNoiseStandardDeviation^2} \] Multiplying by \(\PerturbationNoiseStandardDeviation^2\) and isolating \(\mathbb{E}[\PerturbedSignalVector_0 \mid \PerturbedSignalVector]\) proves Tweedie’s formula: \[ \mathbb{E}[\PerturbedSignalVector_0 \mid \PerturbedSignalVector] = \PerturbedSignalVector + \PerturbationNoiseStandardDeviation^2 \nabla_{\PerturbedSignalVector} \ln \ProbabilityFunction(\PerturbedSignalVector) \] Tweedie’s formula reveals that the Bayes-optimal reconstruction of clean state \(\PerturbedSignalVector_0\) equals the corrupted measurement \(\PerturbedSignalVector\) shifted along the marginal Stein score \(\nabla_{\PerturbedSignalVector} \ln \ProbabilityFunction(\PerturbedSignalVector)\) scaled by noise variance \(\PerturbationNoiseStandardDeviation^2\). Rewriting the forward diffusion marginal \(\NoisedActionChunk = \sqrt{\DiffusionCumulativeSchedule_{k}} \ActionChunk^0 + \sqrt{1 - \DiffusionCumulativeSchedule_{k}} \DiffusionGaussianNoise\) as \(\frac{\NoisedActionChunk}{\sqrt{\DiffusionCumulativeSchedule_{k}}} = \ActionChunk^0 + \frac{\sqrt{1 - \DiffusionCumulativeSchedule_{k}}}{\sqrt{\DiffusionCumulativeSchedule_{k}}} \DiffusionGaussianNoise\), the effective noise variance is \(\PerturbationNoiseStandardDeviation^2 = \frac{1 - \DiffusionCumulativeSchedule_{k}}{\DiffusionCumulativeSchedule_{k}}\). At the population optimum of the noise-prediction objective, Tweedie’s formula equates the estimated clean trajectory \(\EstimatedCleanActionChunk^0\) and the Stein score \(\nabla_{\NoisedActionChunk} \ln \ProbabilityFunction(\NoisedActionChunk \mid \ObservationHistory)\): \[ \nabla_{\NoisedActionChunk} \ln \ProbabilityFunction(\NoisedActionChunk \mid \ObservationHistory) = -\frac{\NoisePredictionNetwork(\NoisedActionChunk, \ObservationHistory, \DiffusionStepIndex)}{\sqrt{1 - \DiffusionCumulativeSchedule_{k}}} \] Substituting \(\EstimatedCleanActionChunk^0 = \frac{1}{\sqrt{\DiffusionCumulativeSchedule_{k}}} \left( \NoisedActionChunk - \sqrt{1 - \DiffusionCumulativeSchedule_{k}} \NoisePredictionNetwork(\NoisedActionChunk, \ObservationHistory, \DiffusionStepIndex) \right)\) into the posterior mean \(\ForwardPosteriorMean_k\) reparameterizes the reverse transition mean: \[ \ReverseTransitionMean(\NoisedActionChunk, \ObservationHistory, \DiffusionStepIndex) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{\DiffusionStepRetention_k}} \left( \NoisedActionChunk - \frac{\DiffusionNoiseVariance_k}{\sqrt{1 - \DiffusionCumulativeSchedule_{k}}} \NoisePredictionNetwork(\NoisedActionChunk, \ObservationHistory, \DiffusionStepIndex) \right) \] The noise-prediction network \(\NoisePredictionNetwork\) is optimized via the simplified \(\ell_2\) denoising score-matching objective: \[ \DiffusionMatchingLoss(\ModelParameters) = \mathbb{E}_{k \sim \UniformDistribution\{1, \DiffusionStepCount\}, \DiffusionGaussianNoise \sim \GaussianDistribution(\ZeroVector, \IdentityMatrix), (\ObservationHistory, \ActionChunk^0) \sim \DemonstrationDataset} \left[ \left\| \DiffusionGaussianNoise - \NoisePredictionNetwork\left(\sqrt{\DiffusionCumulativeSchedule_{k}}\ActionChunk^0 + \sqrt{1 - \DiffusionCumulativeSchedule_{k}}\DiffusionGaussianNoise, \ObservationHistory, \DiffusionStepIndex\right) \right\|^2 \right] \] By following the score function \(\nabla_{\NoisedActionChunk} \ln \ProbabilityFunction(\NoisedActionChunk \mid \ObservationHistory)\), reverse diffusion steers stochastic sampling toward high-density demonstration modes rather than averaging incompatible modes into an obstacle collision. Each denoising step commits further to a single mode of the conditional action distribution.

Continuous flow matching and vector field regression

While standard diffusion models formulate generation as reverse stochastic differential equations (SDEs), continuous flow matching (Lipman et al. 2022) generates trajectories along deterministic probability flows defined by ordinary differential equations (ODEs): \[ \frac{\DerivativeOperator\FlowSampleVector_t}{d\FlowTimeCoordinate} = \LearnedVelocityField(\FlowSampleVector_t, \FlowTimeCoordinate, \ObservationHistory) \] The continuous vector field \(\LearnedVelocityField\) drives a source noise distribution \(\FlowProbabilityPath_0(\FlowSampleVector) = \GaussianDistribution(\ZeroVector, \IdentityMatrix)\) to the empirical demonstration target distribution \(\FlowProbabilityPath_1(\FlowSampleVector) = \DemonstrationChunkDistribution(\FlowSampleVector)\) across continuous time \(\FlowTimeCoordinate \in [0, 1]\), governed by the continuity equation: \[ \frac{\partial \FlowProbabilityPath_t(\FlowSampleVector)}{\partial \FlowTimeCoordinate} + \nabla \cdot \left( \FlowProbabilityPath_t(\FlowSampleVector) \MarginalVelocityField_t(\FlowSampleVector) \right) = 0 \] For a straight conditional interpolation path, the continuous trajectory interpolates linearly between source noise \(\FlowSampleVector_0 \sim \GaussianDistribution(\ZeroVector, \IdentityMatrix)\) and demonstration chunk \(\FlowSampleVector_1 \sim \DemonstrationDataset\): \[ \ConditionalFlowMap_t(\FlowSampleVector_0) = (1 - \FlowTimeCoordinate) \FlowSampleVector_0 + \FlowTimeCoordinate \FlowSampleVector_1, \quad \FlowTimeCoordinate \in [0, 1] \] The conditional velocity along each paired-sample path is constant across time: \[ \ConditionalVelocityField_t(\FlowSampleVector \mid \FlowSampleVector_1) = \frac{\DerivativeOperator}{d\FlowTimeCoordinate} \ConditionalFlowMap_t(\FlowSampleVector_0) = \FlowSampleVector_1 - \FlowSampleVector_0 \] and the trajectory acceleration vanishes identically: \(\frac{\DerivativeOperator^2}{d\FlowTimeCoordinate^2} \ConditionalFlowMap_t(\FlowSampleVector_0) = \ZeroVector\). The Conditional Flow Matching (CFM) theorem (Lipman et al. 2022) proves a gradient identity. Regressing the neural vector field \(\LearnedVelocityField\) against this conditional vector field over paired samples \((\FlowSampleVector_0, \FlowSampleVector_1)\) gives the same gradients as regressing it against the intractable marginal velocity field \(\MarginalVelocityField_t(\FlowSampleVector)\): \[ \nabla_\ModelParameters \mathbb{E}_{t, \FlowSampleVector_0, \FlowSampleVector_1} \left[ \| \LearnedVelocityField(\ConditionalFlowMap_t(\FlowSampleVector_0), \FlowTimeCoordinate, \ObservationHistory) - (\FlowSampleVector_1 - \FlowSampleVector_0) \|^2 \right] = \nabla_\ModelParameters \mathbb{E}_{t, \FlowSampleVector \sim \FlowProbabilityPath_t} \left[ \| \LearnedVelocityField(\FlowSampleVector, \FlowTimeCoordinate, \ObservationHistory) - \MarginalVelocityField_t(\FlowSampleVector) \|^2 \right] \] The network is therefore trained via direct mean squared regression: \[ \FlowMatchingLoss(\ModelParameters) = \mathbb{E}_{t \sim \UniformDistribution[0, 1], \FlowSampleVector_0 \sim \GaussianDistribution(\ZeroVector, \IdentityMatrix), \FlowSampleVector_1 \sim \DemonstrationDataset} \left[ \left\| \LearnedVelocityField\left((1 - \FlowTimeCoordinate)\FlowSampleVector_0 + \FlowTimeCoordinate\FlowSampleVector_1, \FlowTimeCoordinate, \ObservationHistory\right) - (\FlowSampleVector_1 - \FlowSampleVector_0) \right\|^2 \right] \] The conditional paths above are straight for each sampled pair, but the learned marginal velocity averages over pairs that reach the same intermediate point. Its integrated trajectories need not remain straight, and their numerical error need not vanish. Independent noise/data pairing also does not establish globally optimal transport. Flow matching can support fewer evaluations in a measured task, but the step count must satisfy an action-quality criterion; it is not a universal consequence of the interpolation formula.

Lipman, Yaron, Ricky TQ Chen, Heli Ben-Hamu, Maximilian Nicklas, and Matt Le. 2022. “Flow Matching for Generative Modeling.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2210.02747.

Denoising steps against the control deadline

In continuous generative policies, the number of reverse evaluation steps \(\DecoderEvaluationCount\) is the governing systems lever trading off policy expressiveness at the physical plant against computational latency on accelerator hardware. The reverse trajectory discretizes a continuous probability flow ODE or reverse SDE. Discretizing this path across \(\DecoderEvaluationCount\) finite steps introduces numerical integration errors:

  • Sampling accuracy: Reducing the number of evaluations increases numerical approximation error for a fixed solver and learned field. How that error changes multimodal coverage, collision rate, or setpoint smoothness depends on the learned distribution and solver. The architecture alone supplies no minimum acceptable step count.
  • Computational latency and roofline limits: Reverse diffusion is an inherently sequential Markov chain: evaluation \(\DiffusionStepIndex-1\) requires the tensor output of evaluation \(\DiffusionStepIndex\). Denoising steps cannot be parallelized across the time dimension on GPU or NPU accelerators. Total chunk generation latency scales linearly with \(\DecoderEvaluationCount\):5 \[ \ProposerInferenceTime = \VisualEncodingTime + \DecoderEvaluationCount \cdot \DecoderPassTime \] where \(\VisualEncodingTime\) is the one-time visual feature extraction latency per chunk and \(\DecoderPassTime\) is the forward execution time of noise-prediction network \(\NoisePredictionNetwork\). A weight-transfer lower bound is \(\ParameterStreamingTime=\WeightMemory/\SustainedMemoryBandwidth\) when every evaluation reloads those weights. The roofline intensity must count all operations and traffic, including convolutional or token reuse and activation spills. Batch size alone does not make every decoder memory bound. The worked budget below evaluates this transfer bound separately from its assumed execution time.

5 Diffusion Denoising Latency Budget: Sequential reverse diffusion cannot parallelize across sampling steps because each input requires the previous step’s output. Total inference time \(\ProposerInferenceTime = \VisualEncodingTime + \DecoderEvaluationCount \DecoderPassTime\) scales linearly with step count \(\DecoderEvaluationCount\), making step reduction or asynchronous chunking essential for real-time actuation.

A setpoint producer that must finish synchronously within \(\ControlSetpointPeriod\) misses its contract when \(\ProposerInferenceTime>\ControlSetpointPeriod\). The consequence depends on the controller’s defined hold, interpolation, and fallback behavior; a deadline miss does not inherently imply a torque drop. Physical AI architectures resolve this latency-expressiveness trade-off through three coordinated mechanisms:

  1. Action chunking: The policy predicts a multi-step trajectory chunk \(\ActionChunk \in \mathbb{R}^{\ActionChunkHorizon \times \ActionDimension}\) spanning prediction horizon \(\ActionChunkHorizon\). Executing an execution horizon \(\ChunkExecutionHorizon \le \ActionChunkHorizon\) amortizes the \(\DecoderEvaluationCount\)-step generation latency over \(\ChunkExecutionHorizon \ControlSetpointPeriod\) of physical robot motion.
  2. Fewer solver evaluations: Flow matching, improved solvers, or distillation can reduce sequential evaluations when validation shows that the resulting action quality remains adequate. Reduced compute buys timing slack at the cost of a new approximation and validation obligation.
  3. Dual-rate asynchronous decoupling: The application processor runs the generative denoising loop asynchronously at a relaxed rate (\(10\text{--}20\text{ Hz}\)), sending each action chunk across the proposal boundary over a black-channel link that carries a checksum and sequence number. A discrete safety microcontroller, which shares no memory with the application processor, receives the chunk into its own double buffer and executes synchronously at \(1\text{ kHz}\). It interpolates spline knots, enforces slew-rate limits, and gates commands through deterministic control barrier functions (The Machine in Five Levels).

The numerical execution of this dual-rate pipeline spans both processors (algorithm 2), and its geometry, denoising trajectory, and hardware split appear across the three panels of figure 6. Panel (a) demonstrates multimodal action selection. It contrasts direct MSE regression, which averages divergent paths into an obstacle collision, against conditional diffusion, which samples distinct valid trajectory modes. Panel (b) traces the reverse-diffusion denoising sequence from isotropic Gaussian noise \(\ActionChunk^{\DecoderEvaluationCount}\) to a coherent action chunk \(\ActionChunk^0\). Panel (c) shows the asynchronous dual-rate hardware split, where the application processor streams candidate chunks over a black-channel serial link to a discrete \(1\text{ kHz}\) safety microcontroller running double-buffering, freshness checks, and spline interpolation. Policy Training Regimes uses the timing budget below to evaluate which sampler fits the control deadline. The pseudocode assumes the microcontroller’s receive path has already validated the proposal’s capture epoch, clock conversion, configuration, validity interval, and handoff continuity. An absent, expired, or rejected chunk selects the defined fallback; spline interpolation and slew clamping alone do not implement that permission decision. The transmitted action must also pass the actuator interface that converts its declared units to drive commands.

\(\mathbf{O}_t = (\MultimodalObservation_{t-\ObservationHistoryLength}, \dots, \MultimodalObservation_{t})\)

Consider a 7-DoF arm with a two-finger gripper (\(\ActionDimension = 8\)) whose policy generates chunks of 16 setpoints at a chosen 25 ms setpoint period (40 Hz), a 400 ms horizon. The noise-prediction network is a 1D temporal convolutional U-Net with 48 million parameters, 96 MB at FP16. Its timings below are illustrative inputs, not measurements of any device. The budget has three parts.

  1. Perception. A visual encoder processes two synchronized RGB streams (wrist-mounted and over-the-shoulder) into the conditioning embedding \(\VisualConditioningEmbedding_t\) in \(\VisualEncodingTime =\) 14 ms per chunk. Because the features are extracted once per chunk before denoising begins, \(\VisualEncodingTime\) is a fixed cost whatever the sampling schedule.
  2. Denoising. Each reverse step evaluates \(\NoisePredictionNetwork\) at 14.2 GFLOP. At a peak bandwidth of 204.8 GB/s, streaming the weights once takes at least 0.469 ms, and the arithmetic intensity is about 147.9 FLOP/byte. With kernel launch overhead and activation spills, the time per evaluation is \(\DecoderPassTime =\) 1.65 ms, and the sampler then sets the cost. A standard DDIM sampler with 16 steps denoises in 26.4 ms, for a total inference latency of 40.4 ms. A flow-matching decoder integrated with 2 Euler evaluations, assuming two steps meet the task’s action-quality criterion, needs 3.3 ms, for a total of 17.3 ms.
  3. The deadline. Run synchronously against the 25 ms period, the DDIM sampler overruns by 61.6 percent before any scheduling overhead. The two-evaluation flow decoder fits with 7.7 ms (30.8 percent) of compute-only slack, and it may run in lockstep only if its measured tail latency, bus transfer, and operating-system scheduling also fit. Run asynchronously at 15 Hz (a 66.7 ms period), the DDIM sampler refreshes the chunk buffer with 26.3 ms to spare. The permission path, at 1 kHz in the two-processor implementation, meanwhile interpolates the 400 ms chunk and checks its freshness, falling back when the chunk expires.

Compounding quadratic error in behavioral cloning

A \(1\text{ mm}\) wrist-pose error on step 1 shifts the wrist camera viewpoint on step 2 into a slightly off-center state that the human demonstrator never visited. Whereas offline supervised evaluation samples from a fixed demonstration distribution, in closed-loop execution the current action \(\CognitivePolicyAction_{t}\) alters the subsequent physical state \(\DecisionProcessState_{t+1}\) and the next observation distribution, creating compounding covariate shift.

Let \(\pi^*\) denote the expert demonstrator policy and \(\ImitationLearnedPolicy\) denote the learned policy. Suppose the single-step error probability under the expert’s state distribution is bounded by \(\SingleStepImitationError\) for all timesteps \(\ControlStepIndex \in \{1, \dots, \BehaviorCloningHorizon\}\): \[ \forall \ControlStepIndex \le \BehaviorCloningHorizon:\quad \mathbb{E}_{\DecisionProcessState \sim \ReferenceStateDistribution^t} [ \EventIndicator(\ImitationLearnedPolicy(\DecisionProcessState) \ne \pi^*(\DecisionProcessState)) ] \le \SingleStepImitationError \] In digital supervised learning (such as fixed-image classification), evaluation inputs are held fixed, so the expected count of prediction errors scales as \(\mathcal{O}(\BehaviorCloningHorizon \SingleStepImitationError)\). In closed-loop physical AI, an action error alters the physical state of the robot, transporting it into states \(\DecisionProcessState \notin \text{supp}(\ReferenceStateDistribution)\) that the demonstrator rarely or never visited. Offline training provides no valid statistical guarantee on action error in these learner-induced states.

To derive the compounding error bound, we construct a formal trajectory coupling argument (Ross et al. 2011). Let \(\FirstMistakeEvent_t\) denote the event that the policy makes its first mistake at time \(\ControlStepIndex\) (\(\ImitationLearnedPolicy(\DecisionProcessState_t) \ne \pi^*(\DecisionProcessState_t)\) and \(\ImitationLearnedPolicy(\DecisionProcessState_{\EarlierControlStepIndex}) = \pi^*(\DecisionProcessState_{\EarlierControlStepIndex})\) for all \(\EarlierControlStepIndex < \ControlStepIndex\)). Couple learner and expert trajectories with the same initial state and transition randomness. Until their first disagreement they coincide, so \(\FirstMistakeEvent_t\) is a subset of the disagreement event on the unconditioned expert trajectory and \(\Pr(\FirstMistakeEvent_t)\le\SingleStepImitationError\). Conditioning on earlier agreement need not preserve the expert state marginal.

6 Compounding Error in Imitation Learning: Ross and colleagues analyzed how learner actions shift the state distribution. Expert corrective labels on learner-visited states can reduce this exposure.

The probability that at least one error has occurred at or before step \(\ControlStepIndex\) is bounded by a union bound:6 \[ \Pr(\text{error by step } \ControlStepIndex) = \Pr\left( \bigcup_{\EarlierControlStepIndex=1}^\ControlStepIndex \FirstMistakeEvent_{\EarlierControlStepIndex} \right) \le \sum_{\EarlierControlStepIndex=1}^\ControlStepIndex \Pr(\FirstMistakeEvent_{\EarlierControlStepIndex}) \le \ControlStepIndex \SingleStepImitationError \] Once an error occurs, the robot diverges into unmodeled states where downstream incremental cost is bounded by at most \(\PerStepCostBound\) per step. Summing over the entire finite execution horizon \(\BehaviorCloningHorizon\): \[ \begin{aligned} \ExpectedTaskCostSum(\ImitationLearnedPolicy) - \ExpectedTaskCostSum(\pi^*) &\le \sum_{t=1}^{\BehaviorCloningHorizon} \PerStepCostBound \Pr(\text{error by step } \ControlStepIndex) \\ &\le \PerStepCostBound \sum_{t=1}^{\BehaviorCloningHorizon} \ControlStepIndex \SingleStepImitationError \\ &= \PerStepCostBound \SingleStepImitationError \frac{\BehaviorCloningHorizon(\BehaviorCloningHorizon+1)}{2} = \CovariateDriftBound \end{aligned} \] The quadratic expression is a worst-case upper bound on excess cumulative cost, not a prediction of geometric trajectory error or a probability. Since \(\Pr(\text{error by step }\ControlStepIndex)\le1\), a tighter bound replaces \(\ControlStepIndex\SingleStepImitationError\) by \(\min(1,\ControlStepIndex\SingleStepImitationError)\). For continuous actions, the disagreement indicator needs a specified tolerance or a surrogate loss tied to a cost bound; exact equality is generally too restrictive.

Interactive dataset aggregation (DAgger) linear reduction

The DAgger (Dataset Aggregation) algorithm (Ross et al. 2011) addresses this distribution shift by collecting training data under the learner’s induced state distribution \(\AggregationLearnerStateDistribution\). At each training iteration \(\AggregationRoundIndex\), the loop proceeds in four steps:

  1. Execute policy \(\ImitationLearnedPolicy_i\) under the applicable safety envelope to collect trajectories \(\AggregationRoundTrajectorySet_i = \{\DecisionProcessState_1, \DecisionProcessState_2, \dots, \DecisionProcessState_{\BehaviorCloningHorizon}\}\).
  2. Query the expert to label every visited state \(\DecisionProcessState_t \in \AggregationRoundTrajectorySet_i\) with the optimal recovery action \(\PolicyAction^* = \pi^*(\DecisionProcessState_t)\).
  3. Aggregate the recovery dataset \(\AggregatedRecoveryDataset \leftarrow \AggregatedRecoveryDataset \cup \{(\DecisionProcessState_t, \PolicyAction^*)\}\).
  4. Retrain policy \(\ImitationLearnedPolicy_{i+1}\) on the aggregated dataset.

For the DAgger bound, make a stronger assumption than the earlier per-step \(\PerStepCostBound\): one wrong action followed by the expert changes expected remaining cost by at most \(\DeviationDisadvantage\), independent of horizon. With expert labels on learner-visited states, a no-regret online learner, sufficient hypothesis capacity, and a valid finite-sample bound \(\FiniteSampleGeneralizationBound\), a selected policy can satisfy a bound of the form \[ \ExpectedTaskCostSum(\ImitationLearnedPolicy)-\ExpectedTaskCostSum(\pi^*) \le \BehaviorCloningHorizon \DeviationDisadvantage(\AggregatedSurrogateLoss+\AverageOnlineRegret+\FiniteSampleGeneralizationBound), \] where \(\AggregatedSurrogateLoss\) is best-in-class loss on the induced-state distribution, \(\AverageOnlineRegret\) is average online-learning regret, and \(\FiniteSampleGeneralizationBound\) covers finite-sample generalization and policy selection. It scales linearly in \(\BehaviorCloningHorizon\) only when \(\DeviationDisadvantage=\mathcal{O}(1)\) and those errors remain controlled. The drift states need expert corrective labels; appending recovery trajectories alone does not supply them.

Closed-loop trajectory distributions and evaluation estimands

Scoring a candidate policy on a static disk of recorded human demonstrations tests the policy on states the human visited, not on the states that the candidate’s own motor commands and the permission path will drive the robot into. Let a physical rollout be parameterized by initial state \(\DecisionProcessState_0\), environmental parameters \(\EnvironmentParameters\) (lighting, surface friction, obstacle geometry), plant parameters \(\PlantParameters\) (payload mass, link inertias), and permission-path parameters \(\PermissionPathParameters\) (lease durations, barrier thresholds). Together these form a trial condition \(\TrialCondition = (\DecisionProcessState_0, \EnvironmentParameters, \PlantParameters, \PermissionPathParameters) \sim \OperationalTestDistribution\) drawn from the operating domain. Under candidate policy \(\Policy\) and physical plant transition dynamics \(\PlantTransitionDensity(\DecisionProcessState_{t+1} \mid \DecisionProcessState_t, \AdmittedControlCommand_t)\), the joint probability density of an executed trajectory \(\Trajectory = (\DecisionProcessState_0, \AdmittedControlCommand_0, \DecisionProcessState_1, \AdmittedControlCommand_1, \dots, \DecisionProcessState_\RolloutHorizon)\) over horizon \(\RolloutHorizon\) is: \[ \ClosedLoopTrajectoryDensity(\Trajectory\mid\Policy,\EnvironmentParameters,\PlantParameters,\PermissionPathParameters)=\InitialStateDistribution(\DecisionProcessState_0\mid \EnvironmentParameters,\PlantParameters,\PermissionPathParameters)\prod_{t=0}^{\RolloutHorizon-1}\left[\int \Policy(\CognitivePolicyAction_{t}\mid \ObservationHistorySequence)\,\PermissionPathKernel(\AdmittedControlCommand_t\mid \DecisionProcessState_t,\CognitivePolicyAction_{t})\,d\CognitivePolicyAction_{t}\right]\PlantTransitionDensity(\DecisionProcessState_{t+1}\mid \DecisionProcessState_t,\AdmittedControlCommand_t). \] Here \(\MultimodalObservation_{t}=\SensorObservationFunction(\DecisionProcessState_t,\EnvironmentParameters)\) is the observation, \(\CognitivePolicyAction_{t}\) is a proposal, and \(\PermissionPathKernel\) maps proposals to admitted commands or fallback actions. The state includes the controller and permission-path memory needed to make the transition Markov. A deterministic permission map is represented by a point-mass kernel; marginalizing over operating conditions gives the deployment trajectory distribution.

The discounted state visitation distribution (state marginal) \(\DiscountedStateVisitationDistribution^{\Policy}(\DecisionProcessState)\) induced by policy \(\Policy\) is: \[ \DiscountedStateVisitationDistribution^{\Policy}(\DecisionProcessState) = (1 - \DiscountFactor) \sum_{t=0}^\infty \DiscountFactor^\ControlStepIndex \Pr(\DecisionProcessState_t = \DecisionProcessState \mid \Policy) \] or in the finite-horizon unweighted setting: \(\AverageStateVisitationDistribution^{\Policy}(\DecisionProcessState) = \frac{1}{\RolloutHorizon} \sum_{t=0}^{\RolloutHorizon-1} \Pr(\DecisionProcessState_t = \DecisionProcessState \mid \Policy)\). Given a binary task success indicator \(\EvaluationPredicate(\Trajectory) \in \{0, 1\}\) (e.g., reaching a target receptacle without exceeding torque or clearance limits), the true closed-loop task success probability across operating domain \(\OperationalTestDistribution\) is the formal expectation over trial conditions, including policy and transition randomness inside \(\text{Traj}\): \[ \TrialSuccessProbability(\OperationalTestDistribution) = \mathbb{E}_{\TrialCondition \sim \OperationalTestDistribution}[\EvaluationPredicate(\text{Traj}(\TrialCondition))] \] Because the distribution of visited states \(\DiscountedStateVisitationDistribution^{\Policy}\) depends directly on policy proposals and their admission, offline evaluations over fixed logged datasets \(\DemonstrationDataset\) evaluate predictions under \(\ExpertDiscountedStateVisitationDistribution\). Static validation scores therefore give no statistical bound over \(\DiscountedStateVisitationDistribution^{\Policy}\) when candidate commands deviate from demonstrator actions.

Value Functions and Continuous Reinforcement Learning

When a latch policy learns by trial and error rather than from demonstrations, no expert label tells it which torque vector to apply at each millisecond. It must discover actions that maximize cumulative reward from interaction, making the reward definition, exploration conditions, and simulator fidelity central assumptions. The following derivations use a fully observed Markov state; a deployed actor with partial observations may require an estimator or history encoder. The transition model includes any fixed permission mechanism, while \(\ExpectedReturnObjective\) here denotes reward to maximize rather than the imitation cost minimized above. The latch task as a reward problem builds reward, return, value, and the Bellman relation from the machine’s latch task; the derivations below assume that primer and extend it to continuous actions and off-policy learning.

Continuous Markov decision processes and policy gradients

Continuous physical control is formalized as a Markov Decision Process (MDP) defined by the tuple \(\MarkovDecisionProcess = (\WorldStateSpace, \ContinuousActionSpace, \EnvironmentTransitionDistribution, \RewardFunction, \DiscountFactor, \InitialStateDistribution)\), whose six components are:

  • State space \(\WorldStateSpace \subseteq \mathbb{R}^{\StateSpaceDimension}\) represents continuous physical states (joint angles, angular velocities, base pose, linear velocity);
  • Action space \(\ContinuousActionSpace \subseteq \mathbb{R}^{\ActionDimension}\) represents continuous actuator commands (joint torques, motor phase currents, or impedance setpoints);
  • Transition dynamics \(\EnvironmentTransitionDistribution(\DecisionProcessState_{t+1} \mid \DecisionProcessState_t, \ContinuousActionVector_{t})\) govern continuous plant kinematics and contact physics;
  • Reward function \(\RewardFunction(\DecisionProcessState_t, \ContinuousActionVector_{t}) \in \mathbb{R}\) quantifies task performance, energy efficiency, and actuator regularization;
  • Discount factor \(\DiscountFactor \in [0, 1)\) establishes the effective temporal planning horizon;
  • Initial state distribution \(\InitialStateDistribution(\DecisionProcessState_0)\) characterizes starting configurations.

The goal is to learn a parameterized policy \(\Policy(\ContinuousActionVector \mid \DecisionProcessState)\) that maximizes the expected discounted cumulative return: \[ \ExpectedReturnObjective(\Policy) = \mathbb{E}_{\Trajectory \sim \Policy} \left[ \TrajectoryReturn(\Trajectory) \right] = \int \PolicyTrajectoryDistribution(\Trajectory) \TrajectoryReturn(\Trajectory) \, d\Trajectory \] where trajectory \(\Trajectory = (\DecisionProcessState_0, \ContinuousActionVector_0, \DecisionProcessState_1, \ContinuousActionVector_1, \dots)\) has cumulative return \(\TrajectoryReturn(\Trajectory) = \sum_{t=0}^\infty \DiscountFactor^\ControlStepIndex \RewardFunction(\DecisionProcessState_t, \ContinuousActionVector_{t})\) and probability density: \[ \PolicyTrajectoryDistribution(\Trajectory) = \InitialStateDistribution(\DecisionProcessState_0) \prod_{t=0}^\infty \Policy(\ContinuousActionVector_{t} \mid \DecisionProcessState_t) \EnvironmentTransitionDistribution(\DecisionProcessState_{t+1} \mid \DecisionProcessState_t, \ContinuousActionVector_{t}) \] Differentiating expected return \(\ExpectedReturnObjective(\Policy)\) directly with respect to policy parameters \(\ModelParameters\) yields: \[ \nabla_\ModelParameters \ExpectedReturnObjective(\Policy) = \int \nabla_\ModelParameters \PolicyTrajectoryDistribution(\Trajectory) \TrajectoryReturn(\Trajectory) \, d\Trajectory \] Applying the score-function log-derivative identity \(\nabla_\ModelParameters \PolicyTrajectoryDistribution(\Trajectory) = \PolicyTrajectoryDistribution(\Trajectory) \nabla_\ModelParameters \ln \PolicyTrajectoryDistribution(\Trajectory)\): \[ \nabla_\ModelParameters \ExpectedReturnObjective(\Policy) = \int \PolicyTrajectoryDistribution(\Trajectory) \nabla_\ModelParameters \ln \PolicyTrajectoryDistribution(\Trajectory) \TrajectoryReturn(\Trajectory) \, d\Trajectory = \mathbb{E}_{\Trajectory \sim \Policy} \left[ \nabla_\ModelParameters \ln \PolicyTrajectoryDistribution(\Trajectory) \TrajectoryReturn(\Trajectory) \right] \] Taking the natural logarithm of the trajectory probability density decomposes it into initial, policy, and plant components: \[ \ln \PolicyTrajectoryDistribution(\Trajectory) = \ln \InitialStateDistribution(\DecisionProcessState_0) + \sum_{t=0}^\infty \ln \Policy(\ContinuousActionVector_{t} \mid \DecisionProcessState_t) + \sum_{t=0}^\infty \ln \EnvironmentTransitionDistribution(\DecisionProcessState_{t+1} \mid \DecisionProcessState_t, \ContinuousActionVector_{t}) \] Differentiating with respect to \(\ModelParameters\): \[ \nabla_\ModelParameters \ln \PolicyTrajectoryDistribution(\Trajectory) = \sum_{t=0}^\infty \nabla_\ModelParameters \ln \Policy(\ContinuousActionVector_{t} \mid \DecisionProcessState_t) \] Neither the initial state distribution \(\InitialStateDistribution(\DecisionProcessState_0)\) nor the plant transition dynamics \(\EnvironmentTransitionDistribution(\DecisionProcessState_{t+1} \mid \DecisionProcessState_t, \ContinuousActionVector_{t})\) depend on policy weights \(\ModelParameters\), causing their gradients to vanish identically. In physical systems engineering, this property matters because continuous contact dynamics, dry friction stick-slip transitions, and impact collisions are non-differentiable or discontinuous, which makes analytic trajectory derivatives \(\nabla_{\ContinuousActionVector} \DecisionProcessState_{t+1}\) ill-conditioned or undefined. The policy gradient formulation bypasses differentiating through plant physics.

By causality (future actions cannot alter past rewards) and subtracting a state-dependent baseline \(\ExpectedRemainingReturn(\DecisionProcessState_t)\) to reduce variance without introducing bias, the Policy Gradient Theorem (Sutton and Barto 2018) evaluates the gradient over the state visitation distribution: \[ \nabla_\ModelParameters \ExpectedReturnObjective(\Policy) = \frac{1}{1-\DiscountFactor}\mathbb{E}_{\DecisionProcessState \sim \DiscountedStateVisitationDistribution^{\Policy}, \ContinuousActionVector \sim \Policy(\cdot \mid \DecisionProcessState)} \left[ \nabla_\ModelParameters \ln \Policy(\ContinuousActionVector \mid \DecisionProcessState) \ExpectedHistoryActionReturn^{\Policy}(\DecisionProcessState, \ContinuousActionVector) \right] \] The factor \(1/(1-\DiscountFactor)\) is required because the state visitation distribution is normalized. An action-independent baseline may replace \(\ExpectedHistoryActionReturn\) by \(\ExpectedHistoryActionReturn-\ExpectedRemainingReturn\) without changing the expectation. Here \(\DiscountedStateVisitationDistribution^{\Policy}(\DecisionProcessState) = (1 - \DiscountFactor) \sum_{t=0}^\infty \DiscountFactor^\ControlStepIndex \Pr(\DecisionProcessState_t = \DecisionProcessState \mid \Policy)\) is the discounted state visitation distribution, and \(\ExpectedHistoryActionReturn^{\Policy}(\DecisionProcessState, \ContinuousActionVector) = \mathbb{E}_{\Trajectory \sim \Policy} \left[ \sum_{k=0}^\infty \DiscountFactor^\ReturnStepOffset \RewardFunction(\DecisionProcessState_{t+k}, \ContinuousActionVector_{t+k}) \mid \DecisionProcessState_t = \DecisionProcessState, \ContinuousActionVector_{t} = \ContinuousActionVector \right]\) is the continuous state-action value function.

Sutton, Richard S., and Andrew G. Barto. 2018. Reinforcement Learning: An Introduction. 2nd ed. MIT Press.

Deep deterministic policy gradient (DDPG)

For high-dimensional continuous actuator spaces, stochastic sampling in policy gradients introduces substantial variance. The Deterministic Policy Gradient (DPG) theorem (introduced by Silver and colleagues in 2014) establishes a closed form for a deterministic policy \(\DeterministicPolicy: \WorldStateSpace \to \ContinuousActionSpace\). The policy gradient is the expected gradient of the action-value function along the policy’s chosen direction: \[ \nabla_\ModelParameters \ExpectedReturnObjective(\DeterministicPolicy) = \frac{1}{1-\DiscountFactor}\mathbb{E}_{\DecisionProcessState \sim \DiscountedStateVisitationDistribution^{\DeterministicPolicy}} \left[ \nabla_{\ContinuousActionVector} \ExpectedHistoryActionReturn^{\DeterministicPolicy}(\DecisionProcessState, \ContinuousActionVector)\Big|_{\ContinuousActionVector=\DeterministicPolicy(\DecisionProcessState)} \nabla_\ModelParameters \DeterministicPolicy(\DecisionProcessState) \right] \] Here \(\DiscountedStateVisitationDistribution^{\DeterministicPolicy}\) is the normalized discounted visitation distribution and the theorem uses the true policy value \(\ExpectedHistoryActionReturn^{\DeterministicPolicy}\); DDPG substitutes a learned critic. In discrete Q-learning (such as DQN), selecting the optimal greedy action requires solving an exhaustive search \(\arg\max_\TrajectoryAction \ExpectedHistoryActionReturn(\DecisionProcessState, \TrajectoryAction)\). In continuous control where action space \(\ContinuousActionSpace \subseteq \mathbb{R}^{\ActionDimension}\) has many dimensions (such as a 7-DoF arm or 12-DoF quadruped), solving an inner non-convex optimization at every control cycle is potentially too costly for the control deadline. DPG circumvents this bottleneck by replacing the inner argmax with a differentiable continuous actor network \(\DeterministicPolicy(\DecisionProcessState)\). Applying the chain rule, the actor is updated directly in the direction of the critic’s action gradient \(\nabla_{\ContinuousActionVector} \CriticActionReturnEstimate(\DecisionProcessState, \ContinuousActionVector)\).

Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient (DDPG, introduced by Lillicrap and colleagues in 2015) parameterizes both actor \(\DeterministicPolicy\) and critic \(\CriticActionReturnEstimate\) with deep neural networks. The continuous critic \(\CriticActionReturnEstimate(\DecisionProcessState, \ContinuousActionVector)\) is trained via off-policy temporal difference learning by minimizing Bellman residual error on transitions \((\DecisionProcessState, \ContinuousActionVector, \Reward, \NextDecisionProcessState)\) sampled from an experience replay buffer \(\ExperienceReplayBuffer\): \[ \LossFunction(\CriticParameters) = \mathbb{E}_{(\DecisionProcessState, \ContinuousActionVector, \Reward, \NextDecisionProcessState) \sim \ExperienceReplayBuffer} \left[ \left( \CriticActionReturnEstimate(\DecisionProcessState, \ContinuousActionVector) - \left( \Reward + \DiscountFactor \TargetCriticReturnEstimate(\NextDecisionProcessState, \TargetDeterministicPolicy(\NextDecisionProcessState)) \right) \right)^2 \right] \] Target networks \((\TargetCriticParameters, \TargetActorParameters)\) track active networks via slow Polyak averaging \(\TargetCriticParameters \leftarrow \PolyakAveragingCoefficient \CriticParameters + (1 - \PolyakAveragingCoefficient)\TargetCriticParameters\) with tracking rate \(\PolyakAveragingCoefficient \ll 1\) (e.g., \(\PolyakAveragingCoefficient = 0.005\)). Polyak tracking converts an unstable, moving regression target into a quasi-stationary target, slowing target drift without guaranteeing convergence under function approximation.

Twin delayed DDPG (TD3) and overestimation bias

Standard continuous actor-critic algorithms like DDPG suffer from overestimation bias. Because the critic uses deep function approximation, target values evaluate with nonzero approximation error \(\CriticApproximationError(\NextDecisionProcessState, \SuccessorActionVector)\). In continuous Q-learning, the policy maximizes over estimated values.

The Maximization Bias Lemma formalizes this phenomenon: let \(\FirstTwinCriticReturnEstimate(\NextDecisionProcessState, \SuccessorActionVector)\) and \(\SecondTwinCriticReturnEstimate(\NextDecisionProcessState, \SuccessorActionVector)\) be two noisy estimators of true value \(\ExpectedHistoryActionReturn^*(\NextDecisionProcessState, \SuccessorActionVector)\) subject to zero-mean estimation error \(\mathbb{E}[\CriticApproximationError_i] = 0\). Because the maximum operator is convex, Jensen’s inequality proves: \[ \mathbb{E}\left[ \max_{\SuccessorActionVector} \TwinCriticReturnEstimate(\NextDecisionProcessState, \SuccessorActionVector) \right] \ge \max_{\SuccessorActionVector} \mathbb{E}\left[ \TwinCriticReturnEstimate(\NextDecisionProcessState, \SuccessorActionVector) \right] = \max_{\SuccessorActionVector} \ExpectedHistoryActionReturn^*(\NextDecisionProcessState, \SuccessorActionVector) \] The bias is nonnegative, and can be zero; strict positivity requires errors that sometimes change which action wins. When present, maximization bias can propagate through recursive Bellman backups: overestimating value at step \(\ControlStepIndex+1\) inflates target values at step \(\ControlStepIndex\) and can favor suboptimal, physically destructive actuator proposals.

Introduced by Fujimoto and colleagues in 2018, Twin Delayed DDPG (TD3) reduces overestimation through three architectural mechanisms:

  1. Clipped Double Q-learning: TD3 maintains two separately parameterized critic networks \(\FirstTwinCriticReturnEstimate\) and \(\SecondTwinCriticReturnEstimate\), computing the Bellman target using the minimum of both critics: \[ \BellmanBackupTarget = \Reward + \DiscountFactor \min_{i=1, 2} \TargetTwinCriticReturnEstimate(\NextDecisionProcessState, \SmoothedTargetAction) \] Because the expected minimum satisfies \(\mathbb{E}[\min(\GenericRandomVariable, \PairedRandomVariable)] \le \min(\mathbb{E}[\GenericRandomVariable], \mathbb{E}[\PairedRandomVariable])\), taking the minimum introduces a conservative underestimation bias. A lower reward estimate is not a clearance or stability bound. Underestimation can change which action wins without making that action physically safer.
  2. Target policy smoothing: To prevent the policy from exploiting sharp, non-physical peaks in the learned critic, TD3 injects clipped Gaussian noise into the target action: \[ \SmoothedTargetAction = \text{clip}\left( \TargetDeterministicPolicy(\NextDecisionProcessState) + \text{clip}(\TargetSmoothingNoise, -\SmoothingNoiseClipBound, \SmoothingNoiseClipBound), \, \ActionLowerBound, \, \ActionUpperBound \right), \quad \TargetSmoothingNoise \sim \GaussianDistribution(\ZeroVector, \TargetSmoothingNoiseStandardDeviation^2 \IdentityMatrix) \] This regularizes value estimation over a local actuator ball. Doing so enforces the physical prior that similar motor commands should yield similar expected returns while smoothing the optimization landscape.
  3. Delayed policy updates: Updating the policy network on every critic step causes the actor to chase an inaccurate, rapidly shifting value landscape. TD3 updates actor parameters \(\ModelParameters\) and target networks once every \(\ActorUpdateDelayCount\) critic updates (typically delay ratio \(\ActorUpdateDelayCount = 2\)), giving the critics additional updates before actor weights shift, without guaranteeing stabilization.

Soft actor-critic (SAC) and maximum entropy control

While TD3 provides a deterministic policy, physical autonomy requires stochastic exploration to discover viable contact modes and avoid getting trapped in local contact minima (such as a quadruped foot wedged against an obstacle). Soft Actor-Critic (SAC, developed by Haarnoja and colleagues in 2018) formulates continuous control under the Maximum Entropy Reinforcement Learning framework. The objective augments task rewards with the differential entropy of the policy: \[ \MaximumEntropyObjective(\Policy) = \sum_{t=0}^\infty \mathbb{E}_{(\DecisionProcessState_t, \ContinuousActionVector_{t}) \sim \PolicyStateActionDistribution} \left[ \DiscountFactor^\ControlStepIndex \left( \RewardFunction(\DecisionProcessState_t, \ContinuousActionVector_{t}) + \EntropyTemperature \PolicyEntropy(\Policy(\cdot \mid \DecisionProcessState_t)) \right) \right] \] where \(\PolicyEntropy(\Policy(\cdot \mid \DecisionProcessState_t)) = -\mathbb{E}_{\ContinuousActionVector \sim \Policy}[\ln \Policy(\ContinuousActionVector \mid \DecisionProcessState_t)]\) is the policy entropy, and temperature parameter \(\EntropyTemperature > 0\) controls the trade-off between reward maximization and stochastic exploration.

Soft Bellman backups and squashed Gaussian policy

The soft state-action value satisfies the soft Bellman equation: \[ \SoftExpectedActionReturn(\DecisionProcessState, \ContinuousActionVector) = \RewardFunction(\DecisionProcessState, \ContinuousActionVector) + \DiscountFactor \mathbb{E}_{\NextDecisionProcessState \sim \EnvironmentTransitionDistribution} \left[ \SoftExpectedRemainingReturn(\NextDecisionProcessState) \right], \quad \text{where } \SoftExpectedRemainingReturn(\DecisionProcessState) = \mathbb{E}_{\ContinuousActionVector \sim \pi} \left[ \SoftExpectedActionReturn(\DecisionProcessState, \ContinuousActionVector) - \EntropyTemperature \ln \pi(\ContinuousActionVector \mid \DecisionProcessState) \right] \] To enforce physical actuator limits \(\ContinuousActionVector \in [-\ActionMagnitudeBound, \ActionMagnitudeBound]^{\ActionDimension}\), SAC parameterizes the policy as an invertible squashed Gaussian distribution. The network outputs mean \(\PolicyGaussianMean(\DecisionProcessState)\) and log-standard-deviation \(\ln \PolicyGaussianStandardDeviation(\DecisionProcessState)\). An unbounded latent action is sampled via the reparameterization trick: \[ \UnsquashedLatentAction = \PolicyGaussianMean(\DecisionProcessState) + \PolicyGaussianStandardDeviation(\DecisionProcessState) \odot \ReparameterizationNoise, \quad \ReparameterizationNoise \sim \GaussianDistribution(\ZeroVector, \IdentityMatrix) \] The following density is for normalized actions. Applying the non-linear squashing function \(\ContinuousActionVector = \tanh(\UnsquashedLatentAction)\) bounds actions strictly within \((-1, 1)^{\ActionDimension}\).

By the change-of-variables theorem for probability densities, the log-likelihood of the bounded action \(\ContinuousActionVector\) requires correcting for the Jacobian determinant of the tanh mapping: \[ \Policy(\ContinuousActionVector \mid \DecisionProcessState) = \LatentGaussianPolicyDensity(\UnsquashedLatentAction \mid \DecisionProcessState) \left| \det \frac{\DerivativeOperator\ContinuousActionVector}{\DerivativeOperator\UnsquashedLatentAction} \right|^{-1} \] Because the squashing operator acts element-wise (\(\SquashedActionComponent_i = \tanh(\LatentActionComponent_i)\)), the Jacobian matrix is diagonal: \(\frac{d\SquashedActionComponent_i}{d\LatentActionComponent_i} = 1 - \tanh^2(\LatentActionComponent_i)\). The determinant factors into a product: \[ \det \frac{\DerivativeOperator\ContinuousActionVector}{\DerivativeOperator\UnsquashedLatentAction} = \prod_{i=1}^{\ActionDimension} \left( 1 - \tanh^2(\LatentActionComponent_i) \right) \] Taking the natural logarithm yields the exact closed-form log-density correction: \[ \ln \Policy(\ContinuousActionVector \mid \DecisionProcessState) = \ln \LatentGaussianPolicyDensity(\UnsquashedLatentAction \mid \DecisionProcessState) - \sum_{i=1}^{\ActionDimension} \ln\left( 1 - \tanh^2(\LatentActionComponent_i) \right) \] Physical limits require a further scale and offset; their Jacobian must also be included if densities are expressed in physical units. Here \(\ln \LatentGaussianPolicyDensity(\UnsquashedLatentAction \mid \DecisionProcessState) = -\frac{1}{2} \sum_{i=1}^{\ActionDimension} \left( \left(\frac{\LatentActionComponent_i - \PolicyGaussianMeanComponent_i(\DecisionProcessState)}{\PolicyActionStandardDeviation_i(\DecisionProcessState)}\right)^2 + 2\ln \PolicyActionStandardDeviation_i(\DecisionProcessState) + \ln(2\pi) \right)\).

Dual gradient descent for automatic entropy tuning

Fixed temperature \(\EntropyTemperature\) fails across phases of robot learning: early exploration requires high entropy (\(\EntropyTemperature \gg 0\)), while fine precision assembly requires low entropy (\(\EntropyTemperature \to 0\)). SAC formulates temperature adaptation as a constrained optimization problem: maximizing expected return subject to a minimum average entropy constraint \(\mathbb{E}[\PolicyEntropy(\pi_t)] \ge \TargetPolicyEntropy\), where target entropy is commonly set to \(\TargetPolicyEntropy = -\ActionDimension\).

Solving the dual Lagrangian formulation yields the exact update for temperature \(\EntropyTemperature\): \[ \TemperatureDualObjective(\EntropyTemperature) = \mathbb{E}_{\DecisionProcessState_t \sim \ExperienceReplayBuffer, \ContinuousActionVector_{t} \sim \pi_t} \left[ -\EntropyTemperature \ln \pi_t(\ContinuousActionVector_{t} \mid \DecisionProcessState_t) - \EntropyTemperature \TargetPolicyEntropy \right] \] Differentiating with respect to \(\EntropyTemperature\) gives the dual gradient update rule: \[ \EntropyTemperature \leftarrow \EntropyTemperature - \TemperatureStepSize \nabla_\EntropyTemperature \TemperatureDualObjective(\EntropyTemperature) = \EntropyTemperature - \TemperatureStepSize \left( -\ln \pi_t(\ContinuousActionVector_{t} \mid \DecisionProcessState_t) - \TargetPolicyEntropy \right) \] When expected policy entropy drops below target \(\TargetPolicyEntropy\), \(\mathbb{E}[-\ln\pi_t]<\TargetPolicyEntropy\), the expected gradient is negative and gradient descent raises \(\EntropyTemperature\). Individual samples need not have that sign. Implementations preserve \(\EntropyTemperature>0\), for example by optimizing its logarithm. When entropy exceeds target, \(\EntropyTemperature\) decreases to sharpen exploitation.

Proximal policy optimization (PPO) and GPU simulation

While off-policy algorithms (TD3, SAC) maximize sample efficiency from replay buffers, on-policy Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) (Schulman et al. 2017) is the common choice when thousands of simulated robots run in parallel on one GPU. The Isaac Gym benchmarks and legged-locomotion training that learns to walk in minutes both follow this pattern (Makoviychuk et al. 2021; Rudin et al. 2022). PPO clips the incentive for large probability-ratio changes in its surrogate objective; clipping does not impose a hard trust region on the resulting policy: \[ \PpoClippedSurrogateObjective(\ModelParameters) = \EmpiricalTimestepAverage_t \left[ \min\left( \PolicyProbabilityRatio_t(\ModelParameters) \EstimatedHistoryAdvantage_t, \, \text{clip}(\PolicyProbabilityRatio_t(\ModelParameters), 1 - \RatioClippingWidth, 1 + \RatioClippingWidth) \EstimatedHistoryAdvantage_t \right) \right] \] where the probability ratio is \(\PolicyProbabilityRatio_t(\ModelParameters) = \frac{\Policy(\CognitivePolicyAction_{t} \mid \DecisionProcessState_t)}{\pi_{\RolloutPolicyParameters}(\CognitivePolicyAction_{t} \mid \DecisionProcessState_t)}\), clipping parameter \(\RatioClippingWidth \approx 0.2\), and advantage estimates \(\EstimatedHistoryAdvantage_t\) are computed via Generalized Advantage Estimation (GAE): \[ \GaeAdvantageEstimate_t = \sum_{\LookaheadStepIndex=0}^\infty (\DiscountFactor \TraceDecay)^\LookaheadStepIndex \TemporalDifferenceResidual_{t+\LookaheadStepIndex}^{\ExpectedRemainingReturn}, \quad \text{where } \TemporalDifferenceResidual_t^\ExpectedRemainingReturn = \RewardFunction(\DecisionProcessState_t, \CognitivePolicyAction_{t}) + \DiscountFactor \CriticReturnEstimate(\DecisionProcessState_{t+1}) - \CriticReturnEstimate(\DecisionProcessState_t) \] The parameter \(\TraceDecay\in[0,1]\) trades bootstrapping against longer sampled returns. At \(\TraceDecay=0\) the estimate is a one-step TD residual and depends directly on critic error. At \(\TraceDecay=1\), a complete episode gives the discounted Monte Carlo return minus the baseline; truncated rollouts retain bootstrap error. Terminal transitions omit the continuation value in all Bellman targets above.

Schulman, John, Filip Wolski, Prafulla Dhariwal, Alec Radford, and Oleg Klimov. 2017. “Proximal Policy Optimization Algorithms.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:1707.06347.
Makoviychuk, Viktor, Lukasz Wawrzyniak, Yunrong Guo, Michelle Lu, Kier Storey, Miles Macklin, David Hoeller, et al. 2021. “Isaac Gym: High Performance GPU-Based Physics Simulation for Robot Learning.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2108.10470.
Rudin, Nikita, David Hoeller, Philipp Reist, and Marco Hutter. 2022. “Learning to Walk in Minutes Using Massively Parallel Deep Reinforcement Learning.” Proceedings of the 5th Conference on Robot Learning, Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 164: 91–100.

Massively parallel GPU simulation physics and memory hierarchy

On-policy training consumes fresh rollouts, while off-policy methods reuse a replay buffer. Parallel simulation can make fresh experience inexpensive by keeping physics state, observations, policy inference, and rollout storage on the same accelerator. This reduces host-device copies when the complete pipeline supports device-resident execution.

The storage comparison depends on transition size as well as count. If each stored transition takes \(\TransitionStorageSize\) bytes, a rollout with \(\ParallelEnvironmentCount\) environments and \(\EnvironmentRolloutStepCount\) steps needs approximately \(\ParallelEnvironmentCount \EnvironmentRolloutStepCount \TransitionStorageSize\) bytes, while a replay buffer with \(\ReplayBufferTransitionCount\) transitions needs \(\ReplayBufferTransitionCount \TransitionStorageSize\). Images, privileged state, and training scratch space can dominate either budget. No utilization percentage or training duration follows from choosing PPO; simulation fidelity, contact workload, network shape, and convergence requirements determine throughput and total cost.

Asymmetric actor-critic for sim-to-real transfer

Deploying policies trained in physics simulators to physical hardware encounters the reality gap: unmodeled actuator latency, mechanical compliance, gear backlash, motor thermal derating, and sensor noise cause policies to stumble or destabilize on physical testbeds. An asymmetric actor-critic gives the training critic more information than the deployed actor. A related but distinct technique, Rapid Motor Adaptation (RMA) (Kumar et al. 2021), trains a privileged actor and then predicts its environment latent from onboard history. The distinction determines what must run on the robot:

Kumar, Ashish, Zipeng Fu, Deepak Pathak, and Jitendra Malik. 2021. “RMA: Rapid Motor Adaptation for Legged Robots.” Robotics: Science and Systems XVII. https://doi.org/10.15607/RSS.2021.XVII.011.
  1. Privileged centralized critic: In training simulation, the critic network \(\CriticReturnEstimate(\PrivilegedSimulatorState)\) or \(\CriticActionReturnEstimate(\PrivilegedSimulatorState, \ContinuousActionVector)\) conditions on the complete, privileged simulation state \(\PrivilegedSimulatorState \in \mathbb{R}^{\PrivilegedStateDimension}\). This privileged vector can contain quantities that are unavailable or measured only imperfectly onboard:
    • True surface contact friction coefficients \(\CoulombFrictionCoefficient\);
    • Exact link masses, payload inertia matrices, and center-of-mass offsets \(\FiniteChange \MassCenterPosition\);
    • Ground-truth contact force vectors \(\ContactPointForce\) and normal impulses at each foot/finger;
    • Unoccluded 3D ground-truth obstacle positions and complete terrain heightmaps;
    • Motor winding temperatures and dynamic back-EMF constants.
    A sufficiently complete privileged state can make the critic’s prediction problem Markov and reduce estimation uncertainty. It does not make the actor’s observation complete or remove model error.
  2. Two-phase adaptation and onboard actor: To transfer privileged knowledge to physical hardware where \(\PrivilegedSimulatorState\) cannot be measured, modern architectures use a two-phase distillation pipeline:
    • Phase 1 (Privileged policy training): The actor \(\Policy(\ContinuousActionVector_{t} \mid \MultimodalObservation_{t}, \EnvironmentLatentVector_t)\) receives deployable onboard observations \(\MultimodalObservation_{t}\) and an environment latent vector \(\EnvironmentLatentVector_t = \PrivilegedStateEncoder(\PrivilegedSimulatorState)\), where encoder \(\PrivilegedStateEncoder\) compresses the privileged state into a low-dimensional manifold (\(\EnvironmentLatentDimension \approx 8\text{--}16\)).
    • Phase 2 (Temporal adaptation module): A lightweight 1D temporal convolutional or MLP adaptation module \(\AdaptationModule(\ObservationHistoryWindow)\) is trained via supervised regression to predict the latent vector \(\PredictedEnvironmentLatent_t\) purely from a sliding window history of recent proprioceptive observations (joint positions \(\GeneralizedCoordinates\), velocities \(\GeneralizedVelocities\), and commanded actions \(\ContinuousActionVector\)).
  3. Deployment cost: A plain asymmetric actor-critic discards the privileged critic at deployment and runs only the actor. An RMA-style design also runs its history encoder or adaptation module. That module has a real compute and memory cost and can infer hidden parameters only when the observation history makes them identifiable.
