Critic access to simulator state does not give the actor access to that state.

Domain randomization varies physical parameters during training to expose the policy to a broader range of dynamics. It can improve transfer within the represented conditions, but neither randomization nor privileged training guarantees successful deployment on new terrain.

Bellman optimality and out-of-distribution overestimation

The optimal state-action value function \(\ExpectedHistoryActionReturn^*(\DecisionProcessState, \ContinuousActionVector)\) satisfies the Bellman optimality equation: \[ \ExpectedHistoryActionReturn^*(\DecisionProcessState, \ContinuousActionVector) = \RewardFunction(\DecisionProcessState, \ContinuousActionVector) + \DiscountFactor \mathbb{E}_{\NextDecisionProcessState \sim \EnvironmentTransitionDistribution(\cdot \mid \DecisionProcessState, \ContinuousActionVector)} \left[ \max_{\SuccessorActionVector \in \ActionSpace} \ExpectedHistoryActionReturn^*(\NextDecisionProcessState, \SuccessorActionVector) \right] \] When learning offline from static pre-collected datasets \(\OfflineTransitionDataset\) without interactive physical exploration, evaluating the maximization \(\max_{\SuccessorActionVector \in \ActionSpace} \ExpectedHistoryActionReturn^*(\NextDecisionProcessState, \SuccessorActionVector)\) queries the learned critic on counterfactual actions that were never attempted in the dataset. Outside the dataset’s state-action support, function approximation errors can produce value overestimation on out-of-distribution (OOD) actions. An offline policy can favor these spuriously high values and propose destructive motor setpoints unless the permission path rejects them.

Modern offline continuous RL counters this instability through Conservative Q-Learning (CQL) (Kumar et al. 2020), which adds an explicit regularization term that penalizes Q-values on out-of-distribution actions while maximizing Q-values on logged demonstrator actions: \[ \min_{\CriticActionReturnEstimate} \ConservativePenaltyWeight \left( \mathbb{E}_{\DecisionProcessState \sim \OfflineTransitionDataset, \ContinuousActionVector \sim \ConservativeSamplingDistribution(\cdot \mid \DecisionProcessState)}[\CriticActionReturnEstimate(\DecisionProcessState, \ContinuousActionVector)] - \mathbb{E}_{(\DecisionProcessState, \ContinuousActionVector) \sim \OfflineTransitionDataset}[\CriticActionReturnEstimate(\DecisionProcessState, \ContinuousActionVector)] \right) + \frac{1}{2} \BellmanErrorLoss(\CriticActionReturnEstimate) \] Under its stated coverage, estimation, and regularization assumptions, CQL provides lower-bound results for expected policy value. The displayed regularizer does not guarantee a pointwise bound below \(\ExpectedHistoryActionReturn^*\) for every unseen state-action pair, and reward conservatism does not prevent actuator runaway. Policy proposals still require runtime permission checks.

Kumar, Aviral, Aurick Zhou, George Tucker, and Sergey Levine. 2020. “Conservative q-Learning for Offline Reinforcement Learning.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 33: 1179–91.

SayCan affordance-grounded skill selection

The SayCan framework (Ahn et al. 2022) integrates semantic knowledge from high-capacity language models with physical affordance constraints from trained policies. Let \(\LanguageInstruction\) represent high-level language instruction and \(\CandidateSkill\) represent an available low-level skill. SayCan ranks candidate skills by multiplying a language relevance score and an estimated skill-success score, then normalizing if a selection distribution is needed: \[ \SkillSelectionDistribution(\CandidateSkill \mid \DecisionProcessState, \LanguageInstruction) \propto \LanguageSkillPrior(\CandidateSkill \mid \LanguageInstruction) \cdot \AffordanceSuccessEstimate(\DecisionProcessState, \CandidateSkill) \] Here the two factors separate semantic relevance from physical feasibility:

Ahn, Michael, Anthony Brohan, Yevgen Chebotar, et al. 2022. “Do as I Can, Not as I Say: Grounding Language in Robotic Affordances.” Proceedings of the 6th Conference on Robot Learning (CoRL), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 205: 287–318.
  • \(\LanguageSkillPrior(\CandidateSkill \mid \LanguageInstruction)\) represents the semantic prior, evaluating whether skill \(\CandidateSkill\) contributes logically to completing instruction \(\LanguageInstruction\).
  • \(\AffordanceSuccessEstimate(\DecisionProcessState, \CandidateSkill) \in [0, 1]\) represents the physical affordance probability, modeled by a learned value function that predicts the probability of successfully executing skill \(\CandidateSkill\) from current state \(\DecisionProcessState\).

If the learned value correctly assigns a near-zero score to an unavailable skill, the product deprioritizes it. The learned score estimates success; it bounds no path, contact force, or clearance, so a separate motion-permission check is needed.

Comparing policy architectures by compute and distribution

An MLP point regressor computes one action in a single forward pass but averages ambiguous demonstrations into a single path; a diffusion decoder preserves separate path modes at the cost of \(\DecoderEvaluationCount\) sequential network evaluations per chunk. Deploying continuous control policies on physical robots requires selecting an architecture whose computational profile, memory traffic, and latency fit the control loop deadlines of the physical plant (table 1).

For a concrete choice, count model parameters and activations, identify the memory level serving each operation, and compare the complete measured execution path with the deadline. The worked decoder budget in section 1.5.2 makes those assumptions explicit. Sample efficiency also depends on task coverage and recovery data; it cannot be ranked from the decoder family alone.

Signal Temporal Logic and Continuous Robustness

A boolean pass/fail flag tells an engineer whether a mobile base stayed at least \(0.30\text{ m}\) from an aisle rack during a \(10\text{ s}\) run, but not whether it cleared the rack by \(0.25\text{ m}\) or \(0.2\text{ mm}\). Verifying whether a physical autonomous system satisfies dynamic safety requirements requires moving beyond instantaneous boolean assertions to continuous temporal specifications that score how much margin remained across the interval.

Syntax and semantics of signal temporal logic

A Signal Temporal Logic (STL) specification evaluates a continuous-time physical signal \(\SignalTrajectory(\TimeInstant)\) against temporal requirements defined over time intervals \([\StlIntervalStart, \StlIntervalEnd]\): \[ \StlFormula ::= \top \mid \pi \mid \neg \StlFormula \mid \StlFormula_1 \wedge \StlFormula_2 \mid \square_{[\StlIntervalStart, \StlIntervalEnd]} \StlFormula \mid \lozenge_{[\StlIntervalStart, \StlIntervalEnd]} \StlFormula \mid \StlFormula_1 \StlUntilOperator \StlFormula_2 \] where \(\pi = (\StlPredicateFunction(\SignalTrajectory(\TimeInstant)) \ge 0)\) is an atomic predicate (e.g., clearance \(\ObstacleSeparationDistance(\TimeInstant) - \SafeSeparationDistance \ge 0\)), \(\square_{[\StlIntervalStart, \StlIntervalEnd]}\) denotes the Always operator, \(\lozenge_{[\StlIntervalStart, \StlIntervalEnd]}\) denotes the Eventually operator, and \(\StlUntilOperator\) denotes the Until operator. The symbols match those of Signal temporal logic and quantitative robustness.

Quantitative robustness degree

Beyond a boolean satisfaction verdict (\(\SignalTrajectory \models \StlFormula\)), STL admits a signed quantitative robustness metric \(\StlRobustness(\StlFormula, \SignalTrajectory, \TimeInstant)\).7 For a formula combining pressure, speed, and force, first define dimensionless atomic margins \(\NormalizedPredicateMargin_i(\SignalTrajectory)=\StlPredicateFunction_i(\SignalTrajectory)/\PredicateNormalizationScale_i\), where each positive scale \(\PredicateNormalizationScale_i\) has the same SI unit as its predicate. Then the minimum and maximum below compare like quantities: \[ \begin{aligned} \StlRobustness(\pi, \SignalTrajectory, \TimeInstant) &= \NormalizedPredicateMargin(\SignalTrajectory(\TimeInstant)) \\ \StlRobustness(\neg \StlFormula, \SignalTrajectory, \TimeInstant) &= -\StlRobustness(\StlFormula, \SignalTrajectory, \TimeInstant) \\ \StlRobustness(\StlFormula_1 \wedge \StlFormula_2, \SignalTrajectory, \TimeInstant) &= \min\left( \StlRobustness(\StlFormula_1, \SignalTrajectory, \TimeInstant), \StlRobustness(\StlFormula_2, \SignalTrajectory, \TimeInstant) \right) \\ \StlRobustness(\square_{[\StlIntervalStart, \StlIntervalEnd]} \StlFormula, \SignalTrajectory, \TimeInstant) &= \min_{t' \in [t+\StlIntervalStart, t+\StlIntervalEnd]} \StlRobustness(\StlFormula, \SignalTrajectory, \OperatorWindowTime) \\ \StlRobustness(\lozenge_{[\StlIntervalStart, \StlIntervalEnd]} \StlFormula, \SignalTrajectory, \TimeInstant) &= \max_{t' \in [t+\StlIntervalStart, t+\StlIntervalEnd]} \StlRobustness(\StlFormula, \SignalTrajectory, \OperatorWindowTime) \end{aligned} \] A strictly positive \(\StlRobustness\) establishes satisfaction and a negative value establishes violation under the stated semantics. Zero is a boundary case that needs the predicate convention. The normalized magnitude is dimensionless and depends on the declared scales. Preserve each raw physical margin and unit separately in the fault record; a single minimum of meters, newtons, and kilopascals has no physical meaning.

7 Signal Temporal Logic Robustness: Quantitative semantics map a trace and formula to a signed score. Its magnitude inherits the atomic predicate’s units only when all combined predicates use commensurate units; otherwise a declared normalization gives a dimensionless score. Negative scores identify violations for falsification searches, subject to sampled-time and measurement limits.

The until operator requires \(\StlFormula_1\) to hold until a time when \(\StlFormula_2\) holds. Its robustness is \[ \StlRobustness(\StlFormula_1\StlUntilOperator\StlFormula_2, \SignalTrajectory, \TimeInstant) =\sup_{t'\in[t+\StlIntervalStart,t+\StlIntervalEnd]}\min\!\left(\StlRobustness(\StlFormula_2, \SignalTrajectory, \OperatorWindowTime),\inf_{t''\in[t,t')}\StlRobustness(\StlFormula_1, \SignalTrajectory, \UntilPrefixTime)\right), \] with the infimum over an empty interval taken as \(+\infty\). Infimum and supremum replace minimum and maximum when extrema are not attained. A sampled trace establishes continuous-time satisfaction only with a justified interpolation or intersample variation bound and sufficient margin for measurement error.

Zero-Failure Testing and the Exposure Wall

In digital software machine learning, validation benchmarks evaluate millions of inference queries or generated tokens in parallel across cloud accelerators within minutes. In physical AI systems, one hour of physical robot operation takes one hour of real wall-clock time and inflicts mechanical wear on actuators, gearboxes, and structural linkages. Confidence from a Finite Campaign uses the zero-failure bound and the Rule of Three to price the running machine’s evaluation campaigns. This section derives the discrete and continuous forms that chapter cites. It also shows why the normal approximation fails when zero failures have been observed.

Derivation of the discrete rule of three

Consider an operational test consisting of \(\ZeroFailureTrialCount\) independent, identically distributed Bernoulli trials (such as autonomous navigation missions or pick-and-place cycles), where each trial has an unknown catastrophic failure probability \(\TrialFailureProbability \in [0, 1]\). Let the test observe zero catastrophic failures (\(\ObservedFailureCount = 0\)). Under the hypothesis of failure probability \(\TrialFailureProbability\), the probability of observing zero failures across \(\ZeroFailureTrialCount\) independent trials is: \[ \Pr(\text{zero failures} \mid \TrialFailureProbability) = (1 - \TrialFailureProbability)^\ZeroFailureTrialCount \] To exclude hypothesis \(\TrialFailureProbability\) at a one-sided confidence level \(1 - \HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel\) (e.g., 95 percent confidence with \(\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel = 0.05\)), the likelihood of observing zero failures under that hypothesis must not exceed \(\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel\): \[ (1 - \TrialFailureProbability)^\ZeroFailureTrialCount \le \HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel \] Taking the natural logarithm of both sides: \[ \ZeroFailureTrialCount \ln(1 - \TrialFailureProbability) \le \ln \HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel \] For rare catastrophic events where failure probability is small (\(\TrialFailureProbability \ll 1\)), the first-order Taylor series expansion \(\ln(1 - \TrialFailureProbability) = -\TrialFailureProbability - \frac{\TrialFailureProbability^2}{2} - \dots \approx -\TrialFailureProbability\) yields:8 \[ -\ZeroFailureTrialCount \TrialFailureProbability \le \ln \HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel \iff \TrialFailureProbability \ge \frac{-\ln \HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel}{\ZeroFailureTrialCount} = \frac{\ln(1/\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel)}{\ZeroFailureTrialCount} \] Inverting this approximation gives a conservative sample count \(\ZeroFailureTrialCount\) required to bound the failure probability below \(\TrialFailureProbability\) at confidence \(1 - \HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel\): \[ \ZeroFailureTrialCount \ge \frac{\ln(1/\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel)}{\TrialFailureProbability} \] For standard 95 percent confidence (\(\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel = 0.05\)): \[ \ZeroFailureTrialCount \ge \frac{-\ln(0.05)}{\TrialFailureProbability} = \frac{2.99573}{\TrialFailureProbability} \approx \frac{3}{\TrialFailureProbability} \] This classical result is the discrete Rule of Three: observing zero failures across \(\ZeroFailureTrialCount\) trials bounds the per-trial failure probability to \(\TrialFailureProbability \le 3/\ZeroFailureTrialCount\) at 95 percent confidence. The exact inversion without Taylor approximation is \(\FailureUpperConfidenceLimit = 1 - \HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel^{1/\ZeroFailureTrialCount}\); for small \(\FailureUpperConfidenceLimit\), the approximation \(-\ln(\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel)/\ZeroFailureTrialCount\) slightly exceeds \(\FailureUpperConfidenceLimit\), making the approximation conservative. For a target \(\TargetTrialFailureProbability\), the exact required integer count is \(\lceil\ln\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel/\ln(1-\TargetTrialFailureProbability)\rceil\).

8 Statistical Rule of Three: Zero events in \(\ZeroFailureTrialCount\) independent Bernoulli trials yield the exact one-sided 95 percent upper probability \(1 - 0.05^{1/\ZeroFailureTrialCount} \approx 3/\ZeroFailureTrialCount\) for small probabilities. In continuous time, a stationary Poisson process requires \(\FailureObservationInterval \ge 3.0/\ConstantHazardRate\). Neither bound covers retrained models, modified firmware, or unobserved hazard classes without configuration-equivalence proofs.

Full derivation of the continuous Poisson exposure wall

Autonomous robots operate continuously in time rather than in discrete trials. Let catastrophic events follow a stationary homogeneous Poisson process with constant failure rate \(\ConstantHazardRate > 0\) (failures per operating hour), which emerges naturally from the discrete Bernoulli model via the Poisson Limit Theorem (the law of rare events). Subdividing total fleet operating duration \(\FailureObservationInterval\) into \(\PoissonSubintervalCount\) infinitesimal intervals of duration \(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant = \FailureObservationInterval/\PoissonSubintervalCount\), the per-interval failure probability is \(\SubintervalFailureProbability = \ConstantHazardRate \FiniteChange \TimeInstant = \ConstantHazardRate \FailureObservationInterval / \PoissonSubintervalCount\). In the limit as \(\FiniteChange \TimeInstant \to 0\) (\(\PoissonSubintervalCount \to \infty\)), the binomial probability of observing exactly \(\ObservedFailureCount\) failures converges to the Poisson distribution: \[ \lim_{n \to \infty} \binom{\PoissonSubintervalCount}{\ObservedFailureCount} \left(\frac{\ConstantHazardRate \FailureObservationInterval}{\PoissonSubintervalCount}\right)^\ObservedFailureCount \left(1 - \frac{\ConstantHazardRate \FailureObservationInterval}{\PoissonSubintervalCount}\right)^{\PoissonSubintervalCount - \ObservedFailureCount} = \frac{(\ConstantHazardRate \FailureObservationInterval)^\ObservedFailureCount e^{-\ConstantHazardRate \FailureObservationInterval}}{\ObservedFailureCount!} \] Let \(\CumulativeIncidentCount(\FailureObservationInterval)\) denote the cumulative count of catastrophic incidents observed across cumulative fleet operating duration \(\FailureObservationInterval = \sum_{j=1}^{\FleetRobotCount} \FailureObservationInterval_j\) across \(\FleetRobotCount\) robots. The probability of recording exactly \(\ObservedFailureCount\) incidents in duration \(\FailureObservationInterval\) is: \[ \Pr(\CumulativeIncidentCount(\FailureObservationInterval) = \ObservedFailureCount) = \frac{(\ConstantHazardRate \FailureObservationInterval)^\ObservedFailureCount e^{-\ConstantHazardRate \FailureObservationInterval}}{\ObservedFailureCount!} \] Equivalently, the inter-arrival time \(\FirstIncidentTime\) to the first catastrophic incident follows an exponential distribution \(\FirstIncidentTime \sim \text{Exponential}(\ConstantHazardRate)\) with survival probability \(\Pr(\FirstIncidentTime > \FailureObservationInterval) = e^{-\ConstantHazardRate \FailureObservationInterval}\). When a physical fleet qualification campaign completes with zero observed incidents (\(\ObservedFailureCount = 0\)): \[ \Pr(\CumulativeIncidentCount(\FailureObservationInterval) = 0) = \frac{(\ConstantHazardRate \FailureObservationInterval)^0 e^{-\ConstantHazardRate \FailureObservationInterval}}{0!} = e^{-\ConstantHazardRate \FailureObservationInterval} \] To test whether the operational failure rate is below an acceptable target rate \(\TargetHazardRate\), we formulate the statistical hypothesis test: \[ \StatisticalHypothesis_0: \ConstantHazardRate \ge \TargetHazardRate \quad \text{versus} \quad \StatisticalHypothesis_1: \ConstantHazardRate < \TargetHazardRate \] Under null hypothesis \(\StatisticalHypothesis_0\) with rate \(\TargetHazardRate\), the probability of observing the zero-failure test outcome is \(\Pr(\CumulativeIncidentCount(\FailureObservationInterval) = 0 \mid \TargetHazardRate) = e^{-\TargetHazardRate \FailureObservationInterval}\). To reject \(\StatisticalHypothesis_0\) at significance level \(\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel\) (achieving confidence level \(1 - \HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel\)): \[ \Glyph{p}\text{-value} = \Pr(\CumulativeIncidentCount(\FailureObservationInterval) \le 0 \mid \TargetHazardRate) = e^{-\TargetHazardRate \FailureObservationInterval} \le \HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel \] Taking the natural logarithm of both sides: \[ -\TargetHazardRate \FailureObservationInterval \le \ln \HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel \iff \FailureObservationInterval \ge \frac{-\ln \HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel}{\TargetHazardRate} = \frac{\ln(1/\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel)}{\TargetHazardRate} \] For standard 95 percent confidence (\(\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel = 0.05\)), \(-\ln(0.05) = \ln(20) \approx 2.99573227\). This yields the continuous Rule of Three: \[ \FailureObservationInterval \ge \frac{2.99573}{\TargetHazardRate} \approx \frac{3.0}{\TargetHazardRate} \] Unlike the discrete Bernoulli derivation which required a Taylor approximation for \(\ln(1 - \TrialFailureProbability)\), the continuous Poisson bound \(\FailureObservationInterval \ge \frac{\ln(1/\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel)}{\TargetHazardRate}\) is mathematically exact across all values of \(\TargetHazardRate > 0\).

Systems arithmetic of the exposure wall

For an illustrative target of 1 × 10⁻⁹ failures per operating hour, the one-sided 95 percent bound requires 2.996 × 10⁹ zero-failure operating hours. At the less stringent hypothetical target of 1 × 10⁻⁶ per hour, it still requires 2.996 × 10⁶ hours. These are scenario targets, not rates assigned by a software assurance level or a functional-safety classification.

Parallelism reduces calendar time but not total exposure. Even 1,000 independent, configuration-equivalent robots operating continuously need 342.0 365-day calendar years at the stricter target. Shared environmental hazards and common software faults can invalidate that independence assumption. Pooling fleet hours requires a justified common rate and representative exposure.

The Poisson Exposure Wall limits what zero-event testing alone can establish. A change to policy weights, firmware, sensors, or the operating domain can alter the event process, so earlier hours do not automatically qualify the changed configuration. Reuse requires evidence that the relevant event rate and conditions remain applicable.

Architectural constraints, fault analysis, and runtime monitors therefore carry evidence that a feasible test campaign cannot supply. They are not unconditional guarantees either: a barrier filter or interlock depends on its plant model, sensing validity, fault assumptions, and timing contract. Trial Budgets, Stopping Rules, and Comparisons uses the exposure bound to separate these evidence obligations.

Wald normal confidence interval breakdown and Clopper-Pearson exact bounds

In standard machine learning benchmarks, empirical success or failure rates are commonly summarized using the asymptotic Wald normal approximation. For \(\ObservedTrialFailureCount\) observed failures across \(\TrialCount\) independent Bernoulli trials, the sample failure proportion is \(\ObservedFailureFraction = \ObservedTrialFailureCount/\TrialCount\). The two-sided \((1-\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel)\) Wald confidence interval is: \[ \text{CI}_{\text{Wald}} = \ObservedFailureFraction \pm \SignificanceCriticalQuantile \sqrt{\frac{\ObservedFailureFraction(1 - \ObservedFailureFraction)}{\TrialCount}} \] where \(\SignificanceCriticalQuantile\) is the standard normal critical value (\(\SignificanceCriticalQuantile \approx 1.96\) for 95 percent confidence).

The asymptotic collapse at zero failures (\(\ObservedTrialFailureCount = 0\))

In physical autonomy qualification, candidate systems undergo zero-failure validation tests (\(\ObservedTrialFailureCount = 0\)). When \(\ObservedTrialFailureCount = 0\), the sample proportion is \(\ObservedFailureFraction = 0\), causing the estimated variance \(\ObservedFailureFraction(1-\ObservedFailureFraction)/\TrialCount\) to collapse to zero: \[ \text{CI}_{\text{Wald}}\Big|_{k=0} = 0 \pm \SignificanceCriticalQuantile \sqrt{\frac{0(1 - 0)}{\TrialCount}} = [0, 0] \] The Wald interval asserts, at 95 percent confidence, that the failure probability is exactly zero after a finite test run. It ignores finite-sample uncertainty, and this particular interval excludes every nonzero failure rate. Repeated-sampling coverage of the Wald procedure is not identically zero, but can fall far below its nominal level near the boundary.

For a small nonzero true failure probability, \((1-\TrialFailureProbability)^\ZeroFailureTrialCount\) can remain substantial, so a zero-failure campaign is not evidence of impossibility. The degenerate Wald interval confuses an estimated zero variance with zero uncertainty. Near the opposite boundary it has the same defect, and interior intervals can extend outside the valid probability range.

Clopper-Pearson exact binomial inversion

To maintain non-asymptotic statistical rigor, physical autonomy qualification can use Clopper-Pearson exact binomial confidence intervals (Clopper and Pearson 1934). The Clopper-Pearson interval \([\ExactFailureLowerLimit, \ExactFailureUpperLimit]\) inverts the exact binomial cumulative distribution function without normal approximations: \[ \sum_{\DiscreteOutcome=k}^\TrialCount \binom{\TrialCount}{\DiscreteOutcome} \ExactFailureLowerLimit^\DiscreteOutcome (1 - \ExactFailureLowerLimit)^{\TrialCount - \DiscreteOutcome} = \frac{\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel}{2}, \quad \sum_{\DiscreteOutcome=0}^\ObservedTrialFailureCount \binom{\TrialCount}{\DiscreteOutcome} \ExactFailureUpperLimit^\DiscreteOutcome (1 - \ExactFailureUpperLimit)^{\TrialCount - \DiscreteOutcome} = \frac{\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel}{2} \] By the identity connecting binomial sums to the regularized incomplete Beta function \(\RegularizedEulerIntegral_{\TrialFailureProbability}(\EulerIntegralFirstShape, \EulerIntegralSecondShape)\): \[ \sum_{\DiscreteOutcome=k}^\TrialCount \binom{\TrialCount}{\DiscreteOutcome} \TrialFailureProbability^\DiscreteOutcome (1 - \TrialFailureProbability)^{\TrialCount - \DiscreteOutcome} = \RegularizedEulerIntegral_{\TrialFailureProbability}(\ObservedTrialFailureCount, \TrialCount - \ObservedTrialFailureCount + 1) = \frac{1}{\text{B}(\ObservedTrialFailureCount, \TrialCount - \ObservedTrialFailureCount + 1)} \int_0^{\TrialFailureProbability} \EulerIntegralVariable^{\ObservedTrialFailureCount-1}(1 - \EulerIntegralVariable)^{\TrialCount - \ObservedTrialFailureCount} \, d\EulerIntegralVariable \] for \(0<\ObservedTrialFailureCount<\TrialCount\), these exact boundaries can be computed using quantiles of the Beta distribution: \[ \ExactFailureLowerLimit = \text{BetaQuantile}\left(\frac{\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel}{2}; \, \ObservedTrialFailureCount, \, \TrialCount - \ObservedTrialFailureCount + 1\right), \quad \ExactFailureUpperLimit = \text{BetaQuantile}\left(1 - \frac{\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel}{2}; \, \ObservedTrialFailureCount + 1, \, \TrialCount - \ObservedTrialFailureCount\right) \] At the endpoints, set \(\ExactFailureLowerLimit=0\) for \(\ObservedTrialFailureCount=0\) and \(\ExactFailureUpperLimit=1\) for \(\ObservedTrialFailureCount=\TrialCount\); the corresponding Beta distribution with a zero shape parameter is not defined.

Clopper, C. J., and Egon S. Pearson. 1934. “The Use of Confidence or Fiducial Limits Illustrated in the Case of the Binomial.” Biometrika 26 (4): 404–13.

Exact bounds at zero failures (\(\ObservedTrialFailureCount = 0\))

When zero failures occur in physical testing (\(\ObservedTrialFailureCount = 0\)), the lower bound is \(\ExactFailureLowerLimit = 0\). For a one-sided \((1 - \HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel)\) upper confidence bound, the exact binomial condition simplifies to: \[ \Pr(\DiscreteRandomVariable = 0) = \binom{\ZeroFailureTrialCount}{0} \ExactFailureUpperLimit^0 (1 - \ExactFailureUpperLimit)^\ZeroFailureTrialCount = (1 - \ExactFailureUpperLimit)^\ZeroFailureTrialCount = \HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel \] Solving directly for \(\ExactFailureUpperLimit\): \[ 1 - \ExactFailureUpperLimit = \HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel^{1/\ZeroFailureTrialCount} \iff \ExactFailureUpperLimit = 1 - \HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel^{1/\ZeroFailureTrialCount} = 1 - \exp\left(\frac{1}{\ZeroFailureTrialCount} \ln \HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel\right) \] Applying the Taylor expansion \(e^\ExponentialSeriesArgument = 1 + \ExponentialSeriesArgument + \frac{\ExponentialSeriesArgument^2}{2} + \dots\) with \(\ExponentialSeriesArgument = \frac{\ln \HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel}{\ZeroFailureTrialCount}\): \[ \ExactFailureUpperLimit = 1 - \left( 1 + \frac{\ln \HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel}{\ZeroFailureTrialCount} + \mathcal{O}(\ZeroFailureTrialCount^{-2}) \right) = \frac{-\ln \HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel}{\ZeroFailureTrialCount} + \mathcal{O}(\ZeroFailureTrialCount^{-2}) \] For \(\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel = 0.05\), \(-\ln(0.05) \approx 2.99573\), rigorously recovering the discrete Rule of Three: \(\ExactFailureUpperLimit \approx 3/\ZeroFailureTrialCount\). For a two-sided 95 percent interval (\(\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel = 0.05\), tail probability \(\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel/2 = 0.025\)): \[ \ExactFailureUpperLimit = 1 - (0.025)^{1/\ZeroFailureTrialCount} \approx \frac{-\ln(0.025)}{\ZeroFailureTrialCount} = \frac{3.68888}{\ZeroFailureTrialCount} \approx \frac{3.69}{\ZeroFailureTrialCount} \] Unlike the degenerate Wald interval, the Clopper-Pearson interval guarantees exact non-asymptotic coverage of at least \(1 - \HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel\) for every finite sample size \(\TrialCount \ge 1\).

Sequential Probability Ratio Test

Evaluating embodied physical policies on physical hardware is expensive and induces mechanical wear. A fixed-sample design predeclares sample count \(\TrialCount\) for its planned acceptance decision and inference. A predeclared rejection rule may stop a failing campaign early, and physical safety can require termination at any time; neither decision requires continued exposure to an unsafe candidate. Early acceptance is different. Stopping when an accumulating result looks favorable and reporting the fixed-sample error guarantee can inflate false acceptance. Valid early acceptance requires a stopping rule with its own error calibration.

Abraham Wald’s Sequential Probability Ratio Test (SPRT) (Wald 1945) handles optional stopping for two predeclared simple hypotheses by updating a likelihood ratio after each rollout and comparing it with stopping boundaries. Sequential probability ratio testing applies the test to the running machine’s campaign in the same convention used here. The convention uses per-trial failure probability \(\HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability\), \(\ObservedTrialFailureCount\) failures in \(\SequentialTrialCount\) trials, statistic \(\SequentialLogLikelihoodRatio\), null hypothesis the unacceptable \(\HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability_0\), and error allowances \(\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel\) and \(\StatisticalFalseNegativeRate\).

Log-likelihood ratio and Bernoulli updates

Across consecutive physical rollouts with independent trial outcomes \(\TrialFailureIndicator_1, \TrialFailureIndicator_2, \dots, \TrialFailureIndicator_{\SequentialTrialCount}\), sequential testing compares two competing operational hypotheses:

  • Null hypothesis \(\StatisticalHypothesis_0\): The system failure probability is unacceptable (\(\HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability = \HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability_0\)).
  • Alternative hypothesis \(\StatisticalHypothesis_1\): The system failure probability meets the safety target (\(\HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability = \HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability_1 < \HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability_0\)).

After observing \(\SequentialTrialCount\) trials, the cumulative log-likelihood ratio \(\SequentialLogLikelihoodRatio\) is: \[ \SequentialLogLikelihoodRatio = \sum_{i=1}^\SequentialTrialCount \ln \frac{\Pr(\TrialFailureIndicator_i \mid \StatisticalHypothesis_1)}{\Pr(\TrialFailureIndicator_i \mid \StatisticalHypothesis_0)} \] For discrete Bernoulli trials where \(\TrialFailureIndicator_i \in \{0, 1\}\) (with \(\TrialFailureIndicator_i = 1\) denoting task failure and \(\TrialFailureIndicator_i = 0\) denoting task success), let \(\ObservedTrialFailureCount = \sum_{i=1}^\SequentialTrialCount \TrialFailureIndicator_i\) be the cumulative failure count across \(\SequentialTrialCount\) runs. The log-likelihood ratio simplifies to the exact closed-form evaluation: \[ \SequentialLogLikelihoodRatio = \ObservedTrialFailureCount \ln \left(\frac{\HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability_1}{\HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability_0}\right) + (\SequentialTrialCount - \ObservedTrialFailureCount) \ln \left(\frac{1 - \HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability_1}{1 - \HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability_0}\right) \]

Wald stopping boundaries and decision rules

Wald’s stopping rule rests on declared error rates. The Type I error rate \(\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel\) is the maximum allowable probability of falsely accepting \(\StatisticalHypothesis_1\) when \(\StatisticalHypothesis_0\) is true. The Type II error rate \(\StatisticalFalseNegativeRate\) is the maximum allowable probability of falsely rejecting \(\StatisticalHypothesis_1\) when \(\StatisticalHypothesis_1\) is true. Testing then proceeds according to Wald’s stopping rule: \[ \SprtLowerBoundary < \SequentialLogLikelihoodRatio < \SprtUpperBoundary \] where the usual Wald boundary approximations are: \[ \SprtUpperBoundary = \ln \left(\frac{1 - \StatisticalFalseNegativeRate}{\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel}\right), \quad \SprtLowerBoundary = \ln \left(\frac{\StatisticalFalseNegativeRate}{1 - \HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel}\right) \] At each evaluation step \(\SequentialTrialCount\):

  1. If \(\SequentialLogLikelihoodRatio \ge \SprtUpperBoundary\): Halt and favor \(\StatisticalHypothesis_1\) over the specified \(\StatisticalHypothesis_0\) under the trial model; this is not a general safety certification.
  2. If \(\SequentialLogLikelihoodRatio \le \SprtLowerBoundary\): Halt and favor \(\StatisticalHypothesis_0\) over the specified \(\StatisticalHypothesis_1\); do not release on this evidence.
  3. If \(\SprtLowerBoundary < \SequentialLogLikelihoodRatio < \SprtUpperBoundary\): The accumulated evidence remains inconclusive; execute rollout \(\SequentialTrialCount+1\).

Wald’s approximate thresholds need calibration because the discrete log-likelihood increments can overshoot a boundary. Conservative alternatives are \(\SprtUpperBoundary=\ln(1/\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel)\) and \(\SprtLowerBoundary=\ln\StatisticalFalseNegativeRate\): the likelihood-ratio martingale under \(\StatisticalHypothesis_0\) bounds false acceptance by \(\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel\), and the reciprocal under \(\StatisticalHypothesis_1\) bounds false rejection by \(\StatisticalFalseNegativeRate\). These guarantees apply to the declared simple hypotheses and observation model, not arbitrary deployment shifts.

Average sample number optimality

For Bernoulli failure probability \(\HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability\), the expected log-likelihood increment is \[ \SprtExpectedLogIncrement(\HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability)=\HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability\ln\frac{\HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability_1}{\HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability_0}+(1-\HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability)\ln\frac{1-\HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability_1}{1-\HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability_0}. \] Let \(\SprtStoppingSampleCount\) be the stopping sample count. Ignoring boundary overshoot and using the nominal endpoint error rates gives Wald’s approximate average sample numbers \[ \mathbb{E}_{\HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability_0}[\SprtStoppingSampleCount]\approx\frac{\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel \SprtUpperBoundary+(1-\HypothesisTestSignificanceLevel)\SprtLowerBoundary}{\SprtExpectedLogIncrement(\HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability_0)},\qquad \mathbb{E}_{\HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability_1}[\SprtStoppingSampleCount]\approx\frac{(1-\StatisticalFalseNegativeRate)\SprtUpperBoundary+\StatisticalFalseNegativeRate \SprtLowerBoundary}{\SprtExpectedLogIncrement(\HypothesizedTrialFailureProbability_1)}. \] The denominators are the evidence gained per trial, negative under \(\StatisticalHypothesis_0\) and positive under \(\StatisticalHypothesis_1\). As the hypotheses approach each other, their magnitudes shrink and the required exposure grows. For calibrated boundaries, use the actual operating characteristics and terminal overshoot rather than substituting nominal errors. A predeclared trial cap also changes the protocol and requires an explicit inconclusive outcome or recalibrated terminal rule.

For independent identically distributed outcomes under these two simple hypotheses, SPRT has an average-sample-number efficiency result relative to comparable tests.9 Actual machine-hour savings depend on the two event probabilities, error allowances, overshoot at the stopping boundaries, and trial cost. The displayed thresholds are approximations; a finite-sample operating-characteristic calculation or calibrated simulation is needed for the chosen protocol.

9 Wald Sequential Probability Ratio Test: The efficiency result assumes the specified simple hypotheses and independent, identically distributed outcomes. A sequential design can stop an unpromising test early without claiming a fixed percentage of saved robot hours.

Extreme Value Theory and Tail Risk

Average behavior does not determine the largest disturbance or latency spike a controller will encounter. The central limit theorem concerns normalized sums under conditions such as finite variance; it does not imply that individual observations or their far tails are Gaussian. Even a good Gaussian fit near the center can misstate rare exceedances.

For a genuinely Gaussian variable, a one-sided exceedance above \(\StandardDeviationMultiple\) standard deviations has probability \(1-\StandardNormalCumulativeDistribution(\StandardDeviationMultiple)\), where \(\StandardNormalCumulativeDistribution\) is the standard normal cumulative distribution function. Under independent samples at rate \(\IndependentSampleRate\), its mean waiting time is \(1/[\IndependentSampleRate(1-\StandardNormalCumulativeDistribution(\StandardDeviationMultiple))]\). Correlation changes the clustering and waiting-time distribution, so a control-loop frequency alone does not establish an independent-trial rate.

Physical mechanisms that motivate tail analysis

Disturbances affecting structural survival and controller timing can arise from nonlinear or correlated mechanisms that a Gaussian sensor-noise model does not represent:

  1. Contact impact dynamics: When an autonomous manipulator or mobile robot collides unexpectedly with a rigid obstacle, Hertzian contact mechanics govern the compressive force along the normal of each contact. This force is \(\NormalContactForce = \HertzianContactCoefficient \PenetrationDepth^{3/2}\), where \(\PenetrationDepth\) is deformation and the subscripts name the contact and its normal direction. When compliant protective covers bottom out against rigid metal chassis, contact stiffness shoots up discontinuously (\(\HertzianContactCoefficient \to \BottomedOutContactStiffness\)), converting residual kinetic energy into sharp loads. The relation \(\UnbrakedImpactForce=\BodySpeed\sqrt{\Mass \ContactStiffness}\) applies to an ideal linear spring of stiffness \(\ContactStiffness\) absorbing kinetic energy, not to the Hertzian stiffness coefficient \(\HertzianContactCoefficient\).
  2. Mechanical friction transitions: Stick-slip friction instability, drivetrain gear backlash, and structural resonance modes inject multiplicative, non-Gaussian torque transients into actuator gearboxes during rapid reversals.
  3. Real-time execution jitter: On embedded systems, shared PCIe buses, memory cache thrashing, interrupt storms, and memory reclamation pauses produce tail latency spikes in control loops that break temporal determinism. Head-of-line blocking can amplify latency tails, but a Pareto service-time model requires evidence; bounded service times and arrival processes can produce different waiting-time tails.

These mechanisms motivate inspecting the measured tail; they do not prove a Pareto law. Bounded disturbances, exponential tails, lognormal tails, and power-law tails are all possible. Extreme Value Theory (EVT) supplies limiting models for maxima and threshold exceedances, with tail family and parameter uncertainty estimated from representative data. A positive shape parameter supports a power-law model over the validated range, not an assertion that every physical shock has infinite variance.

The Fisher-Tippett-Gnedenko theorem

While the Central Limit Theorem describes the limiting behavior of normalized sums, the Fisher-Tippett-Gnedenko theorem (Coles 2001) establishes the asymptotic behavior of normalized block maxima: \[ \BlockMaximum_n = \max\{\TailRiskObservation_1, \TailRiskObservation_2, \dots, \TailRiskObservation_n\} \] Let \(\TailRiskObservation_1, \TailRiskObservation_2, \dots, \TailRiskObservation_n\) be a sequence of independent and identically distributed random variables with cumulative distribution function \(\TailRiskDistributionFunction(\TailRiskObservationLevel)\). If there exist normalizing sequences of real constants \(\BlockMaximumNormalizingScale_n > 0\) and \(\BlockMaximumNormalizingShift_n \in \mathbb{R}\) such that the normalized block maximum converges to a non-degenerate limiting distribution \(\BlockMaximumLimitDistribution(\BlockMaximumLevel)\): \[ \lim_{n \to \infty} \Pr\left( \frac{\BlockMaximum_n - \BlockMaximumNormalizingShift_n}{\BlockMaximumNormalizingScale_n} \le \BlockMaximumLevel \right) = \lim_{n \to \infty} \TailRiskDistributionFunction^\BlockSampleCount(\BlockMaximumNormalizingScale_n \BlockMaximumLevel + \BlockMaximumNormalizingShift_n) = \BlockMaximumLimitDistribution(\BlockMaximumLevel) \] then \(\BlockMaximumLimitDistribution(\BlockMaximumLevel)\) belongs exclusively to the Generalized Extreme Value (GEV) family:10 \[ \BlockMaximumLimitDistribution(\BlockMaximumLevel) = \exp\left( -\left[ 1 + \TailShapeParameter \left(\frac{\BlockMaximumLevel - \GevLocationParameter}{\GevScaleParameter}\right) \right]^{-1/\TailShapeParameter} \right) \] defined on \(\{\BlockMaximumLevel : 1 + \TailShapeParameter(\BlockMaximumLevel - \GevLocationParameter)/\GevScaleParameter > 0\}\), with location parameter \(\GevLocationParameter \in \mathbb{R}\), scale parameter \(\GevScaleParameter > 0\), and shape parameter \(\TailShapeParameter \in \mathbb{R}\). The shape parameter \(\TailShapeParameter\) partitions extreme behavior into three distinct tail regimes:

10 Tail Family Is an Empirical Claim: A positive fitted GEV shape parameter (\(\TailShapeParameter > 0\)) provides formal evidence of heavy-tailed physical risk. It does not replace physical instrumentation: block size, temporal autocorrelation, threshold selection, and parameter uncertainty must be validated against real machine rollouts.

  1. Gumbel (Type I, \(\TailShapeParameter = 0\)): The continuous limit as \(\TailShapeParameter \to 0\): \[ \BlockMaximumLimitDistribution(\BlockMaximumLevel) = \exp\left( -\exp\left(-\frac{\BlockMaximumLevel - \GevLocationParameter}{\GevScaleParameter}\right) \right) \] including several unbounded tail forms, rather than only exponential tails. The maximum domain of attraction \(\text{MDA}(\text{Gumbel})\) includes Gaussian, exponential, lognormal, and Gamma distributions.

  2. Fréchet (Type II, \(\TailShapeParameter > 0\), with tail index \(\ParetoTailExponent = 1/\TailShapeParameter\)): The heavy-tailed regime where survival probabilities decay according to a polynomial power law: \(\Pr(\TailRiskObservation > \TailRiskObservationLevel) \sim \TailRiskObservationLevel^{-\ParetoTailExponent}\). The domain of attraction \(\text{MDA}(\text{Fréchet})\) includes Pareto, Cauchy, and Student’s \(\Glyph{t}\) distributions. For a power-law density \(\ProbabilityFunction(\TailRiskObservationLevel) \sim \ParetoTailExponent \TailRiskObservationLevel^{-\ParetoTailExponent-1}\), the \(\TailMomentOrder\)-th raw moment evaluates to: \[ \mathbb{E}[\TailRiskObservation^\TailMomentOrder] = \int_{\TailRiskObservationLevel_0}^\infty \TailRiskObservationLevel^\TailMomentOrder \ProbabilityFunction(\TailRiskObservationLevel) \, d\TailRiskObservationLevel \sim \ParetoTailExponent \int_{\TailRiskObservationLevel_0}^\infty \TailRiskObservationLevel^{\TailMomentOrder - \ParetoTailExponent - 1} \, d\TailRiskObservationLevel \] This integral converges if and only if \(\TailMomentOrder - \ParetoTailExponent - 1 < -1 \iff \TailMomentOrder < \ParetoTailExponent = 1/\TailShapeParameter\). For any moment order \(\TailMomentOrder \ge 1/\TailShapeParameter\), the moment diverges to infinity (\(\mathbb{E}[\TailRiskObservation^\TailMomentOrder] = \infty\)). A direct mathematical consequence is that for \(\TailShapeParameter \ge 0.5\) (\(\ParetoTailExponent \le 2\)), the unbounded power-law model has infinite variance (\(\operatorname{Var}(\TailRiskObservation) = \infty\)). The usual finite-variance central limit theorem then does not apply, and sample variance cannot converge to a finite population variance. For \(\TailShapeParameter \ge 1.0\), the distribution has infinite mean. These are properties of an unbounded fitted model. Finite physical limits can truncate the tail, and a finite dataset cannot establish literal infinite moments.

  3. Weibull (Type III, \(\TailShapeParameter < 0\)): Bounded distributions with a finite physical upper endpoint (\(\BlockMaximumLevel \le \GevLocationParameter - \GevScaleParameter/\TailShapeParameter\)). The domain \(\text{MDA}(\text{Weibull})\) includes Uniform and Beta distributions, applicable when hard physical limits (such as mechanically positive endstops) strictly bound the disturbance.

Peaks-over-threshold and generalized Pareto distribution

Block maxima analysis discards valuable extreme observations occurring within the same block (e.g., secondary collision shocks) and retains uninformative low maxima from quiescent blocks. The Peaks-Over-Threshold (POT) approach, formalized by the Pickands-Balkema-de Haan theorem (Coles 2001), analyzes all excess values \(\ThresholdExcessVariable = \TailRiskObservation - \ExceedanceThreshold\) exceeding a sufficiently high physical threshold \(\ExceedanceThreshold\): \[ \ExcessDistributionFunction(\ThresholdExcess) = \Pr(\TailRiskObservation - \ExceedanceThreshold \le \ThresholdExcess \mid \TailRiskObservation > \ExceedanceThreshold) = \frac{\TailRiskDistributionFunction(\ExceedanceThreshold + \ThresholdExcess) - \TailRiskDistributionFunction(\ExceedanceThreshold)}{1 - \TailRiskDistributionFunction(\ExceedanceThreshold)}, \quad \ThresholdExcess \ge 0 \] The Pickands-Balkema-de Haan theorem proves that for a broad class of underlying physical distributions \(\TailRiskDistributionFunction \in \text{MDA}(\text{GEV}_\TailShapeParameter)\), as threshold \(\ExceedanceThreshold\) approaches the right endpoint \(\TailSupportUpperEndpoint\): \[ \lim_{\ExceedanceThreshold \to \TailSupportUpperEndpoint} \sup_{0 \le \ThresholdExcess < \TailSupportUpperEndpoint - \ExceedanceThreshold} \left| \ExcessDistributionFunction(\ThresholdExcess) - \GeneralizedParetoDistribution(\ThresholdExcess; \GpdScaleParameter(\ExceedanceThreshold), \TailShapeParameter) \right| = 0 \] where, for \(\TailShapeParameter\ne0\), \(\GeneralizedParetoDistribution(\ThresholdExcess)\) is the Generalized Pareto Distribution (GPD): \[ \GeneralizedParetoDistribution(\ThresholdExcess) = 1 - \left( 1 + \frac{\TailShapeParameter \ThresholdExcess}{\GpdScaleParameter} \right)^{-1/\TailShapeParameter} \] defined for \(\ThresholdExcess\ge0\) with \(1+\TailShapeParameter \ThresholdExcess/\GpdScaleParameter>0\), with scale parameter \(\GpdScaleParameter > 0\) and shape parameter \(\TailShapeParameter\) identical to the GEV shape parameter. For \(\TailShapeParameter=0\), take the continuous limit \(\GeneralizedParetoDistribution(\ThresholdExcess)=1-\exp(-\ThresholdExcess/\GpdScaleParameter)\); for \(\TailShapeParameter<0\), the upper endpoint is \(-\GpdScaleParameter/\TailShapeParameter\).

Threshold stability and mean residual life

A key property of the GPD is threshold stability. If the exceedances over threshold \(\ExceedanceThreshold_0\) follow \(\text{GPD}(\GpdScaleParameter_0, \TailShapeParameter)\), then for any higher threshold \(\ExceedanceThreshold>\ExceedanceThreshold_0\) below the upper endpoint, the exceedances over \(\ExceedanceThreshold\) also follow a GPD with the same shape \(\TailShapeParameter\) and linearly shifted scale: \[ \GpdScaleParameter(\ExceedanceThreshold) = \GpdScaleParameter_0 + \TailShapeParameter (\ExceedanceThreshold - \ExceedanceThreshold_0) \] The Mean Residual Life (MRL) evaluates the expected excess conditional on exceeding threshold \(\ExceedanceThreshold\): \[ \mathbb{E}[\TailRiskObservation-\ExceedanceThreshold\mid \TailRiskObservation>\ExceedanceThreshold]=\int_0^{\ExcessSupportUpperEndpoint}[1-\GeneralizedParetoDistribution(\ThresholdExcess)]\,d\ThresholdExcess=\frac{\GpdScaleParameter(\ExceedanceThreshold)}{1-\TailShapeParameter}\quad(\TailShapeParameter<1), \] where \(\ExcessSupportUpperEndpoint=\infty\) for \(\TailShapeParameter\ge0\) and \(\ExcessSupportUpperEndpoint=-\GpdScaleParameter(\ExceedanceThreshold)/\TailShapeParameter\) for \(\TailShapeParameter<0\). The exponential limit at \(\TailShapeParameter=0\) has mean \(\GpdScaleParameter(\ExceedanceThreshold)\).

Substituting the threshold stability relation yields a strictly linear function of threshold \(\ExceedanceThreshold\): \[ \mathbb{E}[\TailRiskObservation - \ExceedanceThreshold \mid \TailRiskObservation > \ExceedanceThreshold] = \frac{\GpdScaleParameter_0 + \TailShapeParameter(\ExceedanceThreshold - \ExceedanceThreshold_0)}{1 - \TailShapeParameter} \] Systems engineers plot empirical mean excess against candidate thresholds \(\ExceedanceThreshold\) (the MRL plot); approximate linearity helps choose a candidate threshold, alongside parameter stability, dependence diagnostics, and uncertainty. It does not certify a unique mathematically valid threshold.

Given \(\ThresholdExceedanceCount\) exceedances \(\{\ThresholdExcess_1, \dots, \ThresholdExcess_{\ThresholdExceedanceCount}\}\) where \(\ThresholdExcess_i = \TailRiskObservationLevel_i - \ExceedanceThreshold > 0\), parameters \((\GpdScaleParameter, \TailShapeParameter)\) can be estimated via Maximum Likelihood Estimation (MLE), with the following expression for \(\TailShapeParameter\ne0\) and valid support: \[ \ln \GeneralizedParetoLikelihood(\GpdScaleParameter, \TailShapeParameter) = -\ThresholdExceedanceCount \ln \GpdScaleParameter - \left( 1 + \frac{1}{\TailShapeParameter} \right) \sum_{i=1}^{\ThresholdExceedanceCount} \ln \left( 1 + \frac{\TailShapeParameter \ThresholdExcess_i}{\GpdScaleParameter} \right) \] Under the GPD model and regularity conditions, including \(\TailShapeParameter>-1/2\), maximum-likelihood estimates have asymptotic normality and efficiency properties. Finite-sample bias, threshold choice, and clustered exceedances still affect the estimate.

Hardware margin and high-quantile sizing

Sizing physical hardware margins against an ultra-rare catastrophic event probability \(\TargetExceedanceProbability\) (e.g., \(\TargetExceedanceProbability = 10^{-7}\) per control cycle) requires extrapolating from \(\ObservedCycleCount\) total operational cycles and \(\ThresholdExceedanceCount\) recorded threshold exceedances (\(\TailRiskObservation > \ExceedanceThreshold\)). With empirical exceedance fraction \(\EmpiricalExceedanceFraction = \ThresholdExceedanceCount / \ObservedCycleCount > \TargetExceedanceProbability\), the high-quantile return level \(\ExceedanceReturnLevel\) satisfies \(\Pr(\TailRiskObservation > \ExceedanceReturnLevel) = \TargetExceedanceProbability\): \[ \begin{aligned} \Pr(\TailRiskObservation > \ExceedanceReturnLevel) &= \Pr(\TailRiskObservation > \ExceedanceThreshold) \Pr(\TailRiskObservation > \ExceedanceReturnLevel \mid \TailRiskObservation > \ExceedanceThreshold) \\ &= \EmpiricalExceedanceFraction \left[ 1 - \GeneralizedParetoDistribution(\ExceedanceReturnLevel - \ExceedanceThreshold) \right] \\ &= \frac{\ThresholdExceedanceCount}{\ObservedCycleCount} \left( 1 + \frac{\TailShapeParameter (\ExceedanceReturnLevel - \ExceedanceThreshold)}{\GpdScaleParameter} \right)^{-1/\TailShapeParameter} = \TargetExceedanceProbability \end{aligned} \] Inverting this relation for \(\ExceedanceReturnLevel\): \[ \left( 1 + \frac{\TailShapeParameter (\ExceedanceReturnLevel - \ExceedanceThreshold)}{\GpdScaleParameter} \right)^{-1/\TailShapeParameter} = \frac{\ObservedCycleCount \TargetExceedanceProbability}{\ThresholdExceedanceCount} \iff 1 + \frac{\TailShapeParameter (\ExceedanceReturnLevel - \ExceedanceThreshold)}{\GpdScaleParameter} = \left( \frac{\ThresholdExceedanceCount}{\ObservedCycleCount \TargetExceedanceProbability} \right)^\TailShapeParameter \] Rearranging yields the closed-form return level formula: \[ \ExceedanceReturnLevel = \ExceedanceThreshold + \frac{\GpdScaleParameter}{\TailShapeParameter} \left[ \left(\frac{\ThresholdExceedanceCount}{\ObservedCycleCount \TargetExceedanceProbability}\right)^{\TailShapeParameter} - 1 \right] \quad (\TailShapeParameter \ne 0) \] Taking the continuous limit as \(\TailShapeParameter \to 0\) using L’Hôpital’s rule (\(\lim_{\TailShapeParameter \to 0} \frac{\PowerLimitBase^\TailShapeParameter - 1}{\TailShapeParameter} = \ln \PowerLimitBase\)): \[ \ExceedanceReturnLevel = \ExceedanceThreshold + \GpdScaleParameter \ln \left( \frac{\ThresholdExceedanceCount}{\ObservedCycleCount \TargetExceedanceProbability} \right) \quad (\TailShapeParameter = 0) \]

Systems engineering sizing rules

For a fitted positive shape parameter, the return level grows as a power of \(1/\TargetExceedanceProbability\); the exponential limit grows logarithmically. Those rates do not yield a numerical ratio between shock loads without matching thresholds, scales, and exceedance frequencies. Report parameter and model uncertainty with the return level, and keep the target unit explicit: a probability per sampled cycle is not a rate per operating hour.

A force quantile can inform spring travel through \(\ProtectiveSpringTravel=\ExceedanceReturnLevel/\ProtectiveSpringStiffness\) only under a valid linear spring model; energy capacity and bottoming-out dynamics still require separate checks. A latency quantile can inform availability analysis, but a watchdog timeout must remain inside the plant’s maximum tolerable delay even when some valid computations exceed it. If that interval is infeasible, the scheduling or fallback design must change.

Queue depth requires an arrival-and-service model converting a delay into accumulated packets or frames; taking the ceiling of a time-valued quantile is dimensionally invalid. Thermal sizing likewise needs the energy and repetition rate of braking events, not a peak-force quantile alone. EVT estimates a tail under stated assumptions; it supplies neither a deterministic maximum nor a guarantee against all future physical failures.

Further Reading

For a deeper foundational understanding of machine learning theory, sequential statistics, and extreme value modeling, consult the following authoritative references:

  • Deep Learning (Goodfellow et al. 2016) provides a comprehensive treatment of neural network optimization, regularized representation learning, and Lipschitz sensitivity bounds.
  • An Introduction to Statistical Modeling of Extreme Values (Coles 2001) establishes the definitive mathematical foundations for GEV, GPD, and tail risk quantification.
  • Sequential Tests of Statistical Hypotheses (Wald 1945) develops the first-principles mathematical framework and average sample number optimality proofs for the Sequential Probability Ratio Test.
  • A Reduction of Imitation Learning and Structured Prediction to No-Regret Online Learning (Ross et al. 2011) establishes the theoretical foundations of compounding error in imitation learning and derives the DAgger algorithm.
Goodfellow, Ian, Yoshua Bengio, and Aaron Courville. 2016. Deep Learning. MIT Press.
Coles, Stuart. 2001. An Introduction to Statistical Modeling of Extreme Values. Springer.
Wald, Abraham. 1945. “Sequential Tests of Statistical Hypotheses.” The Annals of Mathematical Statistics 16 (2): 117–86. https://doi.org/10.1214/aoms/1177731118.
Ross, Stéphane, Geoffrey Gordon, and J. Andrew Bagnell. 2011. “A Reduction of Imitation Learning and Structured Prediction to No-Regret Online Learning.” Proceedings of the Fourteenth International Conference on Artificial Intelligence and Statistics (AISTATS) 15: 627–35.
